Categorical Lusztig cycles and weave schobers
Roger Casals, Merlin Christ

Abstract
We establish the foundations of categorical weave calculus, developing the diagrammatic calculus of weaves and braid varieties within the study of Calabi-Yau triangulated categories and cluster tilting theory. This is achieved by associating a perverse sheaf of triangulated categories to each Demazure weave. A central contribution is the construction and study of the categorical Lusztig cycles and their duals, which we show form simple-minded and silting collections in the category of global sections of such a sheaf of categories. These categorical collections are built using the diagrammatics of weaves and we study their behavior under changes of weaves. For instance, we show that they undergo tilts under weave mutations. En route, we develop the study of categorical weighted braid words, as canonical rigid filtered dg modules over derived preprojective algebras, and the categorical incarnation of the tropical Lusztig rules, as a gluing mechanism for such filtered objects. Appendix A also contains homological results, providing a novel construction of simple-minded and silting collections from full exceptional collections, and characterizing when these arise from a highest weight structure on an abelian category.



1
Introduction

The object of this article is to establish the foundations of categorical weave calculus. This allows for many of the applications of weave calculus and braid varieties to be elevated to the study of relative Calabi-Yau categories and cluster tilting theory. We first associate certain perverse schobers to Demazure weaves, which we refer to as weave schobers. A central contribution is then the construction and study of the categorical Lusztig cycles, which are objects in the triangulated category of global section of such a weave schober and generalize the combinatorial Lusztig weave cycles. We show that the categorical Lusztig cycles of a weave schober form a simple-minded collection, are invariant under weave equivalence, and undergo a simple tilt under weave mutation. En route, we also introduce and develop the study of categorical weighted braid words, as canonical filtered objects in the derived category of the preprojective algebra, the categorical incarnation of the tropical Lusztig rules, as a gluing mechanism for such filtered objects, and the relation between mutations of exceptional collections and mutations of their simple-minded collections.

We also establish corresponding results for the dual categorical Lusztig cycles, which form a silting collection Koszul dual to the categorical Lusztig cycles, and construct explicit weave realizations. In a nutshell, we provide a complete foundational set of results enhancing weave calculus and the study of braid varieties to the context of 3-Calabi–Yau categories and cluster tilting theory. In addition, the results on weave schobers established in this article have a number of applications, some of which will be further explored in subsequent work. These include the construction of additive categorifications for the cluster algebras on braid varieties, and a form of homological mirror symmetry between the Fukaya-Seidel categories associated to a weave and the non-commutative crepant resolutions associated to weave quivers with potentials.

1.1
Scientific context

Weaves were introduced in
[
CZ22
]
within the context of symplectic topology. They have since been developed in different directions, including ones within combinatorial and algebraic frameworks, cf. e.g.
[
CGGS24
,
CGG+25b
]
. In this context, the study of weaves can be understood as a planar diagrammatic calculus, with certain moves & mutations between weaves, and the combinatorics of their cycles and associated functions on braid varieties. See
Figure

1
for two instances of weaves related by weave mutations, and their Lusztig cycles. The basic results on the diagrammatics of weaves, including their moves & mutations, and the Lusztig cycles and their intersection theory, are summarized in
[
CGG+25b
, Section 4]
, see also
[
CGGS24
,
CW24
,
CZ22
]
. This collection of results and diagrammatic techniques on weaves, which is centered around the Lusztig cycles, is often referred to as weave calculus, cf. e.g.
Section

2.1
.

Figure 1:
Two instances of Demazure weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:\beta\longrightarrow\beta^{\prime}
from
\beta=s_{2}s_{1}(s_{1}s_{2})^{3}\in\text{Br}^{+}_{3}
to
\beta^{\prime}=s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}
, where blue weave edges are labeled by
s_{1}\in S_{3}
and red weave edges by
s_{2}\in S_{3}
. (Left) The mutable Lusztig cycles
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
for
\mathfrak{w}
are highlighted in yellow, green and pink respectively, and their intersection quiver is displayed in the square box at the lower right of
\mathfrak{w}
. (Right) The weave
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
obtained from
\mathfrak{w}
by performing weave mutations along the Lusztig cycles
\gamma_{3}
and
\gamma_{2}
, in this order. Its mutable Lusztig cycles
\gamma^{\prime}_{1},\gamma^{\prime}_{2},\gamma^{\prime}_{3}
are also highlighted and their intersection quiver is displayed to the lower left of
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
.

Weave calculus has been successfully used to tackle different problems, some of which include the construction of cluster algebras on braid varieties
[
CGG+25b
,
CGGS24
,
CGG+25a
]
, a proof that the Muller-Speyer twist is Donaldson-Thomas and that source and target positroids seeds quasi-coincide
[
CLSBW23
]
, the Floer theoretic characterization of Gaiotto-Moore-Neitzke spectral networks
[
CN25
,
GMN13
,
GMN14
]
, and the microlocal study of Legendrian links
[
CW24
,
CL22
,
CL26
]
. In interesting cases, including all finite and affine Dynkin types, all cluster seeds and the cluster automorphism groups can be realized by weaves
[
ABL25
,
Hug23a
,
Hug23b
]
, and see also
[
ACSH+25
,
CSHW25
,
HR25
]
for more applications of weave calculus. We emphasize that in all these results, the study of the Lusztig cycles for the corresponding weaves is a key ingredient, as it provides all the necessary data for the cluster seeds, including the quivers and both types of cluster variables.

The main goal of this article is to develop the foundations of categorical weave calculus, which is centered around the categorical Lusztig cycles. The applications above hint towards the existence of such a diagrammatic categorical calculus, but at the same time indicate that its development requires new ideas and techniques. Indeed, on the one hand, such calculus would provide a diagrammatic presentation of many interesting Fukaya-Seidel categories and parts of their spaces of stability conditions, functorially interact with the additive cluster categorifications, and generalize the categorification of higher-rank Fock-Goncharov theory of local systems, cf.
[
Chr25a
,
KL25
]
, to irregular Betti surfaces. On the other hand, at a technical level, it should provide a categorical lift of the tropical Lusztig moves for weighted braid words, the intersection theory of weave cycles in Demazure weaves from
[
CZ22
,
CW24
,
CGG+25b
]
, and feature a triangulated counterpart of the theory of highest weight abelian categories.

The manuscript addresses many of these aspects by developing new ideas and techniques. These include the construction of weave perverse schobers, the study of rigid filtered objects associated to weighted braid words, and new homological insights in the context of
k
-linear stable
\infty
-categories, such as the categorical lift of the tropical Lusztig rules in terms of the propagation of sections of weaves schobers, and the relation between mutations of exceptional collections and mutations for their associated simple-minded and silting collections.

Regarding the technical implementation of such ideas, the categorical weave calculus we present uses the formalism of perverse schobers, introduced in
[
KS14
]
. Intuitively, the perverse schobers we use are a certain class of constructible sheaves of
k
-linear stable
\infty
-categories on a surface. Specifically, we use the theory of perverse schobers parametrized by ribbon graphs embedded in surfaces, introduced in
[
Chr22b
, Sections 3&4]
,
[
CHQ23
, Section 3]
, see also
Section

2.2
below, which provides the key ingredients on perverse schobers needed to develop our results.

1.2
The main results

A central point illustrated by our results is that

There exists a categorical enhancement of weave calculus, constructed using perverse schobers. In it, Lusztig cycles and their calculus have canonical enhancements to a relative CY3 category.

As evidence, we now highlight three of our main results, as follows.

(A)
Let us first start with the categorical enhancement of the Lusztig cycles associated to a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
. By definition, a weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
is a pair consisting of a positive braid word
\beta=s_{i_{0}}\cdots s_{i_{l}}
and a collection of non-negative integers
{\bf a}\coloneqq(a_{0},\ldots,a_{l})
, where
s_{j}
denotes the
j
th Artin generator of the braid group in Type A, i.e. corresponding to the
j
th vertex of the Dynkin diagram. First, as summarized in
Section

2.1.2
, each non-categorical Lusztig cycle of a weave
\mathfrak{w}
can be understood as a certain sequence of weighted braid words
(\beta_{1},{\bf a}_{1})\to\cdots\to(\beta_{k},{\bf a}_{k})
whose weights propagate downwards through
\mathfrak{w}
using the following tropical Lusztig rules:

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\Phi_{1}:(a_{1},a_{2})\mapsto\min(a_{1},a_{2}),\quad\Phi_{2}:(a_{1},a_{2})\mapsto(a_{2},a_{1}),\\
\qquad\qquad\Phi_{3}:(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\mapsto\left(a_{2}+a_{3}-\min(a_{1},a_{3}),\min(a_{1},a_{3}),a_{1}+a_{2}-\min(a_{1},a_{3})\right),

(1)

where
\Phi_{1}
is used at trivalent vertices, the braid word changing according to
s_{i}^{2}\longrightarrow s_{i}
,
\Phi_{2}
at tetravalent vertices, the braid word changing via
s_{i}s_{k}=s_{k}s_{i}
with
i,k
non-adjacent in the Dynkin diagram, and
\Phi_{3}
at hexavalent vertices, where the braid word changes via
s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}=s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}
with
i,j
adjacent.

Therefore, there are two tasks to obtain the right notion of a categorical Lusztig cycle:

(i)

The first task is to construct the categorical version of a weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
. This is achieved in
Section

5
, where we construct a canonical rigid filtered object
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
associated to any weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
arising from Lusztig cycles:

\framebox{$\exists!$ rigid filtered object $L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{D}$ whose graded pieces recover $(\beta,{\bf a})$}

(2)

This is established in
Theorem

5.1
, which refines the above statement. Specifically, the object
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
belongs to the derived
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(\Pi_{2}(A_{n}))
of the additive derived preprojective algebra of type
A_{n}
. The existence and uniqueness of the object
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
is a novel result within the representation theory of
\Pi_{2}(A_{n})
. For its construction, we will rely on a partial geometric model for
\mathcal{D}
in terms of matching
2
-spheres and matching paths in the disc, which allows us to express
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
as a sequence of resolutions for the intersections of matching paths.

(ii)

The second task is to use the sequence
(\beta_{1},{\bf a}_{1})\to\cdots\to(\beta_{k},{\bf a}_{k})
associated to a Lusztig cycle to glue these filtered objects
L_{\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i}}
together into an object of a certain category. This should be done in a manner that the tropical Lusztig rules displayed in
Equation

1
are recovered when considering graded pieces. This step requires constructing such a category from a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
first: we achieve this in
Section

3
by constructing a perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to a weave
\mathfrak{w}
. In this context, the required gluing of the filtered objects is achieved by establishing certain fiber and cofiber sequences between them, as discussed in
Section

4
. For instance, if
(\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i})\to(\beta_{i+1},{\bf a}_{i+1})
is a trivalent vertex,
Proposition

6.5
will establish a cofiber sequence

\framebox{At a trivalent: $\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},L_{\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i}})\otimes S_{p}\longrightarrow L_{\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i}}\longrightarrow L_{\beta_{i+1},{\bf a}_{i+1}}$}

(3)

allowing us to glue the associated filtered objects at that trivalent vertex, as local sections of the weave schober. Similarly, if
(\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i})\to(\beta_{i+1},{\bf a}_{i+1})
is a hexavalent or tetravalent vertex then gluing occurs via the following equivalences:

\framebox{At a non-trivalent vertex: $L_{\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i}}\simeq L_{\beta_{i+1},{\bf a}_{i+1}}$}

(4)

The equivalences in (
4
) are of the underlying objects, which suffices to glue, but the filtrations themselves are typically different. Such equivalences are established in
Proposition

6.2
. The result of such gluings, using (
2
), (
3
) and (
4
), is so that the categorical Lusztig cycles shall be objects in the category of global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, cf.
Definition

4.1
, which will be a smooth and proper
k
-linear stable
\infty
-category with a relative
3
-Calabi–Yau structure.

In a nutshell, these constructions in Sections
3
,
4
and
5
show that categorical Lusztig cycles exist and abide by a categorical enhancement of the tropical Lusztig propagation rules for weighted braid words. Though establishing these results requires developing new results that might be of interest on their own, as illustrated by the content of these sections, the first main result can be summarized as follows:

Theorem A

(Categorical Lusztig cycles)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be a Demazure weave with
m
trivalent vertices and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
its weave schober. Then:

(1)

Let
(\beta,{\bf a})
be a weighted braid word arising from a horizontal slice of a Lusztig cycle of
\mathfrak{w}
. Then, up to equivalence, there exists a unique
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object whose colimit
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{D}
is rigid. In addition, its graded pieces uniquely recover the weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
.

(2)

Let
(\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i})\to(\beta_{i+1},{\bf a}_{i+1})
be the two weighted braid words arising from a horizontal slice of a Lusztig cycle of
\mathfrak{w}
above and under a vertex
p\in{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
. Then

L_{\beta_{i+1},{\bf a}_{i+1}}\simeq\begin{cases}\operatorname{cof}(\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},L_{\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i}})\otimes S_{p}\longrightarrow L_{\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i}})&\mbox{ if }p\mbox{ is trivalent},\\
L_{\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i}}&\mbox{ if }p\mbox{ is non-trivalent}.\end{cases}

(5)

In addition, the categorical equivalences (
5
) recover the tropical Lusztig propagation rules at the vertex
p
by considering the associated graded pieces of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta_{i},{\bf a}_{i}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta_{i+1},{\bf a}_{i+1}}
.

(3)

For each
i\in[1,m]
, there exists a global section
\bm{L}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
whose value at the stalk of height
h
is given by
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, where
(\beta,{\bf a})
is the weighted braid word associated to the combinatorial Lusztig weave cycle
\gamma^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
at height
h
.

By definition, the objects
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}_{i\in[1,m]}
are referred to as the categorical Lusztig cycles.

Theorem
A
is proven in Sections
4
,
5
and
6
, once the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
is introduced and studied in
Section

3
. Three comments on Theorem
A
for now:

(i)

We also show that the rigid geometric realization
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
in Theorem
A
.(1) belongs to the subcategory
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
of matching objects, thus giving a geometric model for
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, cf.
Section

5
.

(ii)

The morphism in (
5
), whose cofiber measures how the objects
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
propagate through a trivalent vertex of
\mathfrak{w}
, is the non-negative truncation
\tau_{\geq 0}(\operatorname{ev})
of the evaluation map of the derived Hom. Its cofiber categorically represents a truncated spherical twist, referred to as a semi-twist. Different versions of semi-twists are ubiquitous in categorical weave calculus, as they categorically capture the tropical propagation rules for weave cycles.

(iii)

Intuitively, Theorem
A
.(3) states that the canonical rigid objects
L_{\beta_{1},{\bf a}_{1}},\ldots,L_{\beta_{k},{\bf a}_{k}}
arising via Theorem
A
.(1) from scanning a given Lusztig cycle of
\mathfrak{w}
, can be glued together to a global section
\bm{L}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
of the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
. The equivalences in Theorem
A
.(2) are a key part of this gluing and the well-definedness of the resulting categorical Lusztig cycles.

(B)
Let us now discuss the categorical enhancement of weave calculus. Theorem
A
provides the categorical Lusztig cycles, with the basic properties that indeed make them categorical avatars of the classical Lusztig weave cycles. A central feature of weave calculus are the notions of weave equivalences and weave mutations. These are summarized in
Section

2.1.1
, see particularly Figures
4
and
5
. In this regard, the first task to start developing a categorical weave calculus is to ensure that:

(i)

The relevant categories, objects and properties associated to a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
should be
invariant under weave equivalences
. In particular, the category
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
and its categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}_{i\in[1,m]}
featuring in Theorem
A
should be invariant under weave equivalences.

(ii)

There must be a categorical enhancement for weave mutation, so that we have precise formulas describing how the relevant categories, objects and homological properties associated to a weave
\mathfrak{w}
change under weave mutations. In particular, we should understand how the category
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
and its categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}_{i\in[1,m]}
change under weave mutations.

We will establish these two items throughout the manuscript, laying the foundations for a categorical weave calculus. Specifically, though the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
itself is not directly a weave invariant, we show in
Section

3
that its
\infty
-category of global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
is invariant under both weave equivalences and weave mutations, cf.
Theorem

3.8
. The behavior of categorical Lusztig cycles is harder to establish, cf.
Section

8
, but the outcome is neat:

The categorical Lusztig cycles form a simple-minded collection in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, and are invariant under weave equivalences. Plus, weave mutations induce mutations of such simple-minded collections.

The proof that the categorical Lusztig cycles form a simple-minded collection, in the sense of
Definition

A.1
.(4), is contentful. It requires us to construct certain exceptional collections in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
from which
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}_{i\in[1,m]}
can be constructed, which we do in
Section

7
and
Section

8.1
. The relation between the exceptional collection and the categorical Lusztig cycles is similar to the relation between the standard objects and the simple objects in a highest weight abelian category. (The precise relation to the theory of highest weight categories is explored in Appendix
A
.) Nevertheless these exceptional collections are
not
invariant under weave equivalence and thus, in a sense, are not well-defined from the viewpoint of weave calculus. We overcome this fact in Sections
8.2
and
8.3
by studying how mutations of exceptional collections affect the associated simple-minded collections, which can be seen as a homological result of interest on its own.

At core, the second result establishing a categorical weave calculus can be summarized as follows:

Theorem B

(Categorical weave calculus)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be a Demazure weave and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
its weave schober. Then the following holds:

(1)

The stable
\infty
-category of global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
is invariant under weave equivalence and weave mutation. In particular, it is an invariant of the boundary braid
\beta
.

(2)

The categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}_{i\in[1,m]}
form a simple-minded collection in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
.

(3)

Let
\mathfrak{w}\sim\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be a weave equivalence. Then the collections of categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
and
\{\bm{L}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\}
coincide, up to a permutation of the indices
i\in[1,m]
.

(4)

Let
\mathfrak{w}\to\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be a forward weave mutation. Then
\{\bm{L}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\}
is obtained from
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
by a forward mutation of simple-minded collections.

Theorem
B
is established in
Section

8
. Note that Theorem
B
.(4) implies that
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
undergoes a backward mutation of simple-minded collections under a backward weave mutation of
\mathfrak{w}
. In a nutshell, Theorem
B
establishes that
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
, built via Theorem
A
, form a simple-minded collection in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, well-defined for the weave equivalence class of
\mathfrak{w}
, and weave mutations lead to a form of tilting for
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
.

(C)
Finally, we also introduce and develop the dual notion of categorical Lusztig cycles in
Section

9
, which we refer to as categorical dual Lusztig cycles. Note that the notion of dual Lusztig cycles is new even within non-categorical weave calculus, as no weave cycles for the cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables were introduced in
[
CGG+25b
]
.
1
1

1

Intuitively, Lusztig cycles yield cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables and dual Lusztig cycles correspond to cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables. Categorically, the categorical Lusztig cycles additively categorify the
\mathcal{X}
-variables, and their categorical duals additively categorify the
\mathcal{A}
-variables, via the cluster tilting object.
Since the Lusztig cycles propagate downwards through the weave and are, coarsely said, as small as possible, the dual Lusztig cycles must propagate the weave upwards and be as large as possible, e.g. they never have compact support.

Categorically, the canonical dual of the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
, considered as a simple-minded collection, is the unique Koszul dual silting collection
\{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
determined by

\operatorname{Ext}^{l}(\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i},\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j})\simeq\begin{cases}k&l=0\text{ and }i=j\\
0&\text{else}\,.\end{cases}

(6)

The Koszul duality referred to above consists of a bijection between silting collections and simple-minded collections which interchanges the respective notions of mutations, see also
Theorem

A.9
and
[
KN
]
.

Nevertheless, the description via (
6
) does not allow us to compute or manipulate categorical dual Lusztig cycles via weave calculus, as it is significantly unclear how
\{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
would be described from the given weave
\mathfrak{w}
. In fact, it can be shown that the objects
\{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
are typically
not
represented by weave cycles in
\mathfrak{w}
, since global sections of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
propagate downwards. We resolve this mismatch, by introducing a duality on weave schobers. Specifically, for each weave
\mathfrak{w}
, we introduce a different weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow}
together with two equivalences of global sections

(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow},\ (\iota^{\uparrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}:R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\longrightarrow R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})\,,

(7)

which intuitively capture the categorical action of rotating counterclockwise and clockwise by
\pi
. Diagram (
8
) provides the categories and functors involved to define the equivalences in (
7
). We will show that the equivalences (
7
) behave like a version of the Ringel duality functor from highest weight theory in the context of stable
\infty
-categories of global sections of perverse schobers, see
Lemmas

9.9
and
9.10
or the discussion in
Section

9.4
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}})\simeq R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\uparrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\downarrow})^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\uparrow})^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\uparrow}}

(8)

The remarkable fact is then that the image
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}
of
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
under the duality
(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}
is representable by an upwards facing weave cycle on
\mathfrak{w}
, even if
\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
is not. In fact, the proofs of our results provide an explicit formula for the weights of this weave cycle, cf.
9.15
. The main statement regarding the silting collection of categorical dual Lusztig cycles is summarized as:

Theorem C

(Weave realization of the silting collection)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be a Demazure weave and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
its weave schober. Let
\{\mathbb{P}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
be the silting collection Koszul dual to the simple-minded collection of categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
. Then, for any
i\in[1,m]
, the following holds:

(1)

The Ringel-type dual
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}
is representable by a positive weave cycle
\rho^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}:E(\mathfrak{w})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

(2)

In addition,
\rho^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
can be obtained by starting at the trivalent vertex
p_{i}\in\mathfrak{w}
and propagating upwards with the tropical Lusztig rules, where at a trivalent vertex
p
with southern edge
\operatorname{e}_{s}\in E(\mathfrak{w})
, the upwards right edge
\operatorname{e}_{ne}
has weight

\framebox{$\rho^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(\operatorname{e}_{ne})=\rho^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(\operatorname{e}_{s})+\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b(p)}))$}\,

(9)

while the upward left edge has weight
\rho^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(\operatorname{e}_{s})
.

(3)

There exists an equivalence
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\mathbb{P}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}_{i}^{\uparrow})
in
\mathcal{D}
. In particular, the evaluation of
\mathbb{P}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
at the bottom edge
e_{1}
is a rigid object which admits a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration, where the weights
{\bf a}={\bf a}(\rho^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i})
for
\beta
are determined by the weave cycle
\rho^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
at the top horizontal slice.

In Theorem
C
.(2),
S_{p}\in\mathcal{D}
is the 2-spherical object associated to the trivalent vertex
p
, cf.
Definition

3.1
, and
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b(p)})\in\mathcal{D}
is an explicit object obtained from the weighted braid word right below
p
and the corresponding weights of
\rho_{i}
, cf.
Equation

180
. We emphasize that the dual Lusztig cycles
\{\rho_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\}
in Theorem
C
, which are (non-categorical) weave cycles, are already new from the perspective of non-categorical weave calculus. The correction term (
9
) is a key new contribution that allows for such dual Lusztig cycles to be described explicitly as weave cycles. This is an instance where the categorical weave calculus, which leads naturally to (
9
), answers an unresolved question within the non-categorical weave calculus.

Theorem
C
.(3) allows us to compute certain evaluations of the silting collection
\{\mathbb{P}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\}
using their Ringel-type duals and their corresponding weave cycles. It is desirable to be able to compute such evaluations explicitly, as Theorem
C
.(3) does via the evaluation
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}_{i}^{\uparrow})
of the Ringel-type dual. Specifically, the evaluations
\{\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\mathbb{P}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\}
determine the cluster tilting object used to additively categorify the cluster algebra associated with the braid variety, as will be explained in
[
CC26
]
.

1.3
A comment on applications

Theorems
A
,
B
,
C
, and the new techniques developed to prove them are a core contribution of this manuscript, establishing the foundations of categorical weave calculus. At the same time, these contributions lead to a number of new results and applications. Indeed, in the same manner that the non-categorical weave calculus leads to new results in a variety of contexts, cf.
Section

1.1
above, the uses of this categorical enhancement are manifold. In particular:

(i)
Symplectic topology
. A symplectic geometric realization of the cluster algebras constructed in
[
CGG+25b
]
stems from the study of Lagrangian fillings of Legendrian links, see e.g.
[
CW24
,
CZ22
]
. This is a geometric problem about certain surfaces in 4-dimensions, and a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
yields such a Lagrangian filling. Intuitively, the cluster algebra is the coordinate ring of functions for a moduli of Lagrangian fillings, with different Lagrangian fillings (e.g. coming from Demazure weaves) providing the cluster charts. It is much desirable to have a symplectic realization for categorical weave calculus, e.g. for the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, its saturated relative 3-Calabi–Yau category
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, and the categorical Lusztig cycles and their duals.

For that, we shall first construct a symplectic 6-manifold
(Y_{\mathfrak{w}},\lambda_{\mathfrak{w}})
with a Lefschetz fibration
\pi:Y_{\mathfrak{w}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
will be the partially wrapped Fukaya category associated to
\pi
. Then we will explain how
(Y_{\mathfrak{w}},\lambda_{\mathfrak{w}})
relates to the Lagrangian fillings in 4-dimensions by using matching 2-disks and the
\mathbb{L}
-compressing systems from
[
CG24
]
. In particular, this yields a rigorous symplectic geometric model for these cluster algebras, from the 4-dimensional study of Lagrangian fillings, and for the cluster categories, here obtained via the Fukaya-Seidel category of a 6-dimensional manifold, with a geometric map connecting these two models. In this symplectic context, we shall explain the meaning of the categorical Lusztig cycles in Theorem
A
, in terms of Lagrangian skeleta, of the dual ones in Theorem
C
, in terms of Lagrangian cocores, and of the cluster algebra from
[
CGG+25b
]
as it relates to the Fukaya-Seidel category.

(ii)
Quivers with potential
. By combining
[
CG24
, Section 2]
and
[
CGG+25b
, Section 7.4]
, there is a canonical ice quiver with potential
(Q_{\mathfrak{w}},W_{\mathfrak{w}})
associated to a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
. We shall establish a relation between the
\infty
-category of global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
of the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, and the perfectly valued derived
\infty
-category of the relative 3-Calabi–Yau Ginzburg dg-algebra of such
(Q_{\mathfrak{w}},W_{\mathfrak{w}})
. The relative Ginzburg algebra is obtained from the categorical dual Lusztig cycles
\{\mathbb{P}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\}
, and its simple modules identify with the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
. In particular, the description in terms of the quiver potential shows that the weave cycle intersection pairing of the Lusztig cycles, see
[
CGG+25b
, Sections 4.5&4.6]
, computes the Euler characteristics of the
\operatorname{Ext}
-groups between the categorical Lusztig cycles. We will also prove that the Donaldson-Thomas transformation of the quiver can be realized via a sequence of weave mutations and that, in categorical weave calculus, the associated transformation coincides with the Ringel-type duality in (
7
).

(iii)
Additive categorifications of cluster algebras
. The results of this manuscript lead to additive categorifications of the cluster algebras associated to braid varieties. In the reduced case
\beta=w_{0}w
,
w\in W
a reduced word, such additive categorifications have been previously studied via the abelian categories of modules over preprojective algebras and their exact subcategories, see for instance
[
GLS06
,
GLS08
,
BIRS09
]
. Our contributions relate to these previous works as follows.

First, we tackle the general case where
\beta
is any braid word with
\delta(\beta)=w_{0}
, not necessarily the reduced case. The silting collections we construct yield cluster tilting objects in the Higgs category or, equivalently by
[
Wu23
, Ex. 8.19]
, in the module category of the preprojective algebra. These cluster tilting objects generalize those associated to the maximal rigid modules from
[
GLS06
,
GLS08
,
BIRS09
]
. The so-called layer modules in these works are a special case of the
2
-spherical objects associated with the trivalent vertices of the weave, see
Definition

3.1
and
Remark

5.3
. In this sense, the filtrations of the maximal rigid modules by layer modules, cf.
[
IR11
, Cor. 2.9]
, are generalized to arbitrary weaves via the filtrations in Theorem
A
and Theorem
C
.(3). In addition,
[
GLS07
,
GLS11
,
IR11
]
show that the endomorphism algebra of the cluster tilting object in the reduced case is quasi-hereditary, i.e. its module category is a highest weight abelian category. The triangulated categories constructed in our paper thus generalize the derived categories of these quasi-hereditary algebras. We allow the associated category to be triangulated, not just abelian, and equip them with a full exceptional collection, in place of a highest weight structure, cf.
Section

9.4
and Appendix
A
.

Second, even in the reduced case and for a given reduced expression, we construct cluster tilting objects corresponding to many cluster seeds, not just one. Specifically, we recover the seed associated to a maximal rigid module by considering the right-inductive weave. The above discussions concern Type A, and details will appear in
[
CC26
]
. We expect this relation to hold in the case of a general finite Dynkin diagram, cf.
Remark

3.7
.

Remark 1.1
.

Relatedly, in the context of local systems with irregular singularities, non-categorical weave calculus allows for the construction of cluster algebras for the coordinate rings of wild character varieties, see e.g.
[
FG06
, Section 1.15]
and
[
CZ22
, Section 3]
, or
[
CN25
]
. Similar to (2) above, there is an important dissonance between the real 4-dimensional geometry that leads to the non-abelianization maps of
[
GMN13
,
GMN14
]
and the cluster algebras in
[
CW24
,
CGG+25b
]
, versus the real 6-dimensional geometry that leads to the 3-Calabi–Yau categorical techniques used to categorify such cluster algebras, see e.g.
[
Gon17
, Section 6]
or
[
Chr22b
,
Chr25a
]
. The categorical weave calculus developed here, in conjunction with application (i) above, resolves this picture by providing a geometric factorization of the appropriate symplectic 3-Calabi–Yau manifold as a high-dimensional branched cover over the symplectic push-off of the Lagrangian filling associated to
\mathfrak{w}
, explaining geometrically how these two approaches are related.
∎

Finally, putting (i)-(ii) above together yields a form of homological mirror symmetry, established through the global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
of the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
: in the A-side there is a Fukaya-Seidel category and its associates, such as the relative Rabinowitz category, and in the B-side there is the relative Ginzburg dg-algebra and its associates, such as the cluster category or Higgs category, cf.
[
Wu23
, Corollary 5.26]
. Here the A-side is readily symplectic, whereas these categories in the B-side are understood as a form of non-commutative crepant resolutions. These further results and applications of categorical weave calculus will be established in
[
CC26
]
.

Organization of the paper
: The manuscript proves the above results as follows.
Section

2
provides the necessary ingredients on weaves and perverse schobers, in
Section

2.1
and
Section

2.2
respectively. The weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
in Theorems
A
and
B
is constructed in Section
3.1
and Theorem
B
.(1) is proven in
Section

3.2
, cf.
Theorem

3.8
. The categorical Lusztig cycles are constructed in Section
4
, using results latter established in
Section

6
. Theorem
A
is also proven in
Section

6
. The proofs in
Section

6
base on results and techniques on categorical weighted braid words developed in
Section

5
. Theorem
B
.(2), (3) and (4) are then proven in
Section

8
, using the constructions from
Section

7
, and Theorem
C
in
Section

9
. Finally, discussions on the relation with the theory of highest weight abelian categories can be found in
Section

9.4
and Appendix
A
.∎

Notation on
\infty
-categories
: We use the theory of stable
\infty
-categories as developed in
[
Lur09
,
Lur17
]
. The
\infty
-category of small
\infty
-categories is denoted by
\operatorname{Cat}_{\infty}
, as in
[
Lur09
, Chapter 3]
. The
\infty
-category of small stable
\infty
-categories is denoted
\operatorname{St}
, cf.
[
Lur17
, Section 1.1.4]
. All stable
\infty
-categories appearing in this paper will be small. We denote by
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{idem}}\subset\operatorname{St}
the full subcategory of idempotent complete stable
\infty
-categories.
\operatorname{Ind}
-completion defines an equivalence between
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{idem}}
and the
\infty
-category of compactly generated, presentable
\infty
-categories and compact objects preserving, left adjoint functors
\mathcal{P}r^{L,\omega}_{\operatorname{St}}
. The symmetric monoidal
\infty
-structure on
\mathcal{P}r^{L,\omega}_{\operatorname{St}}
thus induces a symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{idem}}
. Given a field
k
,
\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)\in\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{idem}}
defines an algebra object and we define
\operatorname{LinCat}_{k}^{\operatorname{sm}}\coloneqq\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)}(\operatorname{St})
to be the
\infty
-category of small, idempotent complete,
k
-linear stable
\infty
-categories. Note that the forgetful functor
\operatorname{LinCat}_{k}^{\operatorname{sm}}\to\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{idem}}
preserves limits by
[
Lur17
, Cor. 3.4.3.2]
. All limits computed in
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{idem}}
in this paper will be preserved by the forgetful functor
\operatorname{St}^{\operatorname{idem}}\to\operatorname{St}
, as a
k
-linear stable
\infty
-category with a full exceptional collection is automatically idempotent complete. The forgetful functor
\operatorname{St}\to\operatorname{Cat}_{\infty}
also preserves limits.

Given a
k
-linear stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{LinCat}_{k}^{\operatorname{sm}}
and two objects
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
, we write
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\in\mathcal{D}(k)
for the morphism object in the
\mathcal{D}(k)
-linear
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{C}
, see
[
Lur17
, Def. 4.2.1.28]
, i.e. the
k
-linear
\infty
-categorical version of the derived Hom. There is also an isomorphism of
k
-vector spaces

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\simeq H_{0}\operatorname{Mor}(X,Y[i])\,.

Finally, all chain complexes are graded homologically and
k[i]
denotes a chain complex concentrated in degree
i
, as in
[
Lur17
]
. In particular, for the non-negative truncation functor
\tau_{\geq 0}
, which we use often for semi-twists, we have
\tau_{\geq 0}(k[-1]\oplus k[2])\simeq k[2]
.
∎

2
Preliminary Ingredients

This section introduces the necessary ingredients to establish the results in this manuscript. Specifically,
Section

2.1
provides a summarizing account of non-categorical weave calculus, including the definition of weave equivalences and mutations, and the non-categorical Lusztig cycles.
Section

2.2
recalls the notion of a perverse schober parametrized by a ribbon graph and provides a few facts about perverse schobers that we use throughout the article.

Figure 2:
A Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\longrightarrow\delta(\beta)
from the 3-stranded braid word
\beta=s_{2}s_{1}(s_{1}s_{2})^{3}
to
\beta^{\prime}=s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}
, where blue weave edges are labeled by
s_{1}\in S_{3}
and red weave edges by
s_{2}\in S_{3}
. Generic horizontal slices are depicted in yellow dashed lines, and the positive braid word associated to each such slice is written to its right. We have highlighted in purple the letters of each braid word to which the next move will be applied when scanning the Demazure weave downward.

2.1
A primer on weave calculus

The focus of the article will be on Demazure weaves, whose non-categorical weave calculus we now briefly summarize. For specificity, we focus on Type
A_{n}
, though similar considerations apply to all types, cf.
[
CGG+25b
, Section 4]
. Let
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
be two positive braid words on
n
-strands, i.e. words on the positive Artin generators
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}
of the braid group on
n
-strands. By definition, a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\longrightarrow\beta^{\prime}
is a sequence of positive braid words
\beta\to\beta_{1}\to\beta_{2}\to\cdots\to\beta^{\prime}
such that each move
\beta_{j}\to\beta_{j+1}
is either a braid move in the positive braid monoid or the move
s_{i}^{2}\to s_{i}
.
Figure

2
depicts an example of a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
diagrammatically. Specifically, by drawing a positive braid word
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{l}}
as a set of parallel vertical strands on the plane, with the
j
th strand labeled by
s_{i_{j}}
, these moves can be depicted diagrammatically as in
Figure

3
. A core aspect of weave calculus are the Lusztig cycles of a Demazure weave, their intersection theory, and their behavior under weave equivalences and mutations, which we will momentarily summarize.

Figure 3:
The three types of vertices allowed in the diagrammatic version of a weave
\mathfrak{w}
. (Left) Trivalent vertex, where all edges are decorated with the same permutation
s_{i}\in S_{n}
. It represents the move
s_{i}^{2}\to s_{i}
. (Center) Hexavalent vertex, with the edge decorations alternating between
s_{i},s_{j}
with
|i-j|=1
. It represents the braid move
s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}\to s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}
. (Right) Tetravalent vertex, with edge decorations as drawn with
|i-k|>1
. It represents the braid move
s_{i}s_{k}=s_{k}s_{i}
.

These diagrams for Demazure weaves are always read top-to-bottom, the diagram itself is often referred to as a weave. Considered as a planar graph, a Demazure weave admits the three types of vertices as in
Figure

3
, and the connected components of the complement of the vertices are said to be weave edges, cf.
[
CW24
, Definition 2.1]
or
[
CZ22
, Section 2]
for the general definition. The trivalent vertices of a weave
\mathfrak{w}
will be denoted
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
, ordered bottom-to-top, e.g. with
p_{i}
at height
i
. Each horizontal slice not intersecting a vertex determines a positive braid word by reading the weave edges left-to-right, as illustrated in
Figure

2
.

Figure 4:
A few weave equivalences, in all of them blue indicates
s_{1}\in S_{3}
, red
s_{2}
, and green
s_{3}
.
(i)
This weave equivalence encodes a syzygy between braid moves, it is also known as the Zamolodchikov relation.
(ii)
A weave equivalence known as the push-through move, as it allows for pushing trivalent vertices through hexavalent vertices.
(iii)
A weave equivalence allowing for distant colors to pass through vertices of each other.

2.1.1
Weave equivalence and mutations

The notion of weave mutation was introduced in
[
CZ22
, Section 4.8]
. Equivalences between weaves, also known as moves, were discussed in
[
CZ22
, Theorem 1.1]
. See also
[
CGGS24
, Section 4]
. We consider the following equivalence relation on weaves:

(i)

Two weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
consisting only of braid moves are declared to be equivalent, see e.g.
Figure

4
.(i).

(ii)

If
|i-j|=1
, the weaves
\mathfrak{w}:s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}\to s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}s_{j}\to s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}
and
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}\to s_{i}s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}\to s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}\to s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}
are declared to be equivalent, cf.
Figure

4
.(ii).

(iii)

If
|i-j|\geq 2
, the weaves
\mathfrak{w}:s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}\to s_{i}s_{i}s_{j}\to s_{i}s_{j}\to s_{j}s_{i}
and
s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}\to s_{j}s_{i}s_{i}\to s_{j}s_{i}
are declared to be equivalent, cf.
Figure

4
.(iii).

By definition, two weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
are said to be equivalent if there is a sequence of local equivalences as above starting at
\mathfrak{w}
and ending at
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
. Note that the equivalences in (ii) and (iii) are parameterized by rank
2
subdiagrams of types
A_{2}
and
A_{1}\times A_{1}
respectively.

Figure 5:
(Left) An elementary weave mutation, between the only two Demazure weaves from
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:s_{1}^{3}\to s_{1}
. (Right) A more elaborate form of weave mutation, obtained by composing weave equivalences with an elementary weave mutation.

By definition, the two weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:s_{i}^{3}\to s_{i}
are said to be are related by an elementary weave mutation, cf.
Figure

5
(left) or
Figure

6
. They are not considered to be weave equivalent. In general, two weaves are said to be related by a weave mutation if they can be related by a sequence of weave equivalences, an elementary weave mutation, and another sequence of weave mutations. See for instance cf.
Figure

5
(right). It is proven in
[
CGGS24
, Theorem 4.6]
that any two Demazure weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
are related by a sequence of equivalence moves and elementary mutations, where we have fixed a braid word for the Demazure product
\delta(\beta)
of
\beta
.

Figure 6:
A redrawing of
Figure

5
(left), with new notation to specify direction. Recall that a weave mutation is given by a sequence of weave equivalences, one local exchange of the weave according to the two diagrams depicted (with any color
s_{i}
), and another sequence of weave equivalences. In a forward weave mutation, the piece of weave on the left is exchanged by the piece of weave on the right. A backward weave mutation is the inverse process.

In order to match the notation used in the tilting theory of finite length hearts, we specifically distinguish the direction of a weave mutation by using the adjectives forward and backward according to
Figure

6
.

2.1.2
Weighted braid words and Lusztig propagation rules

Let
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
be a Demazure weave and
E(\mathfrak{w})
its set of weave edges. By definition, a weave cycle on
\mathfrak{w}
is a function
\gamma:E(\mathfrak{w})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
that assigns a non-negative integer to each edge of the weave. The values of
\gamma
are said to be the weights of the edges for the cycle
\mathfrak{w}
, cf.
[
CGG+25b
, Section 4.4]
.

In the same manner that each horizontal slice of a weave yields a braid word
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
, cf.
Figure

2
, given a weave cycle
\gamma
and a horizontal slice we obtain a braid word
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
and a collection of non-negative integers
{\bf a}\coloneqq(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
, where
a_{j}\coloneqq\gamma(e_{i_{j}})
is the weight of the
j
th weave edge
e_{i_{j}}\in E(\mathfrak{w})
being intersected in that horizontal slice, corresponding to the letter
s_{i_{j}}
in the braid word
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
. By definition, we refer to such a pair
(s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}},{\bf a})
as a weighted braid word. Of particular importance are the following weave cycles associated to the trivalent vertices of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
:

Definition 2.1

(Lusztig cycles)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave and
p\in\mathfrak{w}
a trivalent vertex. By definition, the Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{p}
associated to
p
is the unique weave cycle
\gamma_{p}:E(\mathfrak{W})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that:

(i)

\gamma_{p}(e_{\mathsf{s}})=1
for the unique south edge
e_{\mathsf{s}}
at the trivalent vertex
p
, and
\gamma_{p}(e)=0
if
e
is any weave edge that contains any points above
p
.

(ii)

At any other trivalent vertex with top edges
e_{1},e_{2}
and south edge
e
,
\gamma_{p}
satisfies

\gamma_{p}(e)=\min(\gamma_{p}(e_{1}),\gamma_{p}(e_{2}))\,.

(10)

(iii)

For a 4-valent vertex with top edges
e_{1},e_{2}
and bottom edges
e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime}
,
\gamma_{p}
satisfies

(\gamma_{p}(e^{\prime}_{1}),\gamma_{p}(e^{\prime}_{2}))=(\gamma_{p}(e_{2}),\gamma_{p}(e_{1}))\,.

(iv)

For a 6-valent vertex with top edges
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
and bottom edges
e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},e^{\prime}_{3}
,
\gamma_{p}
satisfies

\displaystyle\gamma_{p}(e^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{p}(e_{2})+\gamma_{p}(e_{3})-\min(\gamma_{p}(e_{1}),\gamma_{p}(e_{3}))\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{p}(e^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle=\min(\gamma_{p}(e_{1}),\gamma_{p}(e_{3}))\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{p}(e^{\prime}_{3})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{p}(e_{2})+\gamma_{p}(e_{1})-\min(\gamma_{p}(e_{1}),\gamma_{p}(e_{3}))\,.\qed

Figure 7:
Two instances of Lusztig cycles on a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:(s_{1}^{2}s_{2}^{2})^{2}\to s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}
. (Left) The Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{1}
associated to the first (top) trivalent vertex. The non-zero weave edges for
\gamma_{1}
are highlighted in yellow, and the specific weight is written next to each weave edge. For instance, at the horizontal pink slice, the weighted braid word is
((s_{2}^{2}s_{1})^{2},(1,2,0,1,0,1))
. (Right) The Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{2}
associated to the second trivalent vertex, with the non-zero weave edges for
\gamma_{2}
highlighted in cyan.

See
Figure

1
and
Figure

7
for instances of Lusztig cycles; in the former a highlighted edge denotes weight 1 and a non-highlighted edge weight 0, in the latter the weights of the weave edges are specified.
Definition

2.1
can be also described in terms of weighted braid words as follows. To construct
\gamma_{p}
, first consider a horizontal weave slice right below the height of
p
, which yields a certain braid word
\beta(p)\coloneqq s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k(p)}}
and assign the weights
{\bf a}(p)\coloneqq(0,0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
, where the only non-zero weight
1
is assigned to the weave edge south of
p
. Then, propagate this weighted braid word
(\beta(p),{\bf a}(p))
downwards by using the following rules for the weights:

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\Phi_{1}:(a_{1},a_{2})\mapsto\min(a_{1},a_{2}),\quad\Phi_{2}:(a_{1},a_{2})\mapsto(a_{2},a_{1}),\\
\qquad\qquad\Phi_{3}:(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\mapsto\left(a_{2}+a_{3}-\min(a_{1},a_{3}),\min(a_{1},a_{3}),a_{1}+a_{2}-\min(a_{1},a_{3})\right),

(11)

where
\Phi_{1}
is used at trivalent vertices, where the braid word changes according to
s_{i}^{2}\longrightarrow s_{i}
,
\Phi_{2}
at tetravalent vertices, where the braid word changes as
s_{i}s_{k}=s_{k}s_{i}
with
i,k
non-adjacent, and
\Phi_{3}
at hexavalent vertices, where the braid word changes via
s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}=s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}
with
i,j
adjacent.

Remark 2.2
.

The downward propagation rules in
Equation

11
above are referred to as tropical Lusztig rules, as they are obtained by tropicalizing the relations

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad x_{i}(t_{1})x_{i}(t_{2})=x_{i}(t_{1}+t_{2}),\quad x_{i}(t_{1})x_{k}(t_{2})=x_{k}(t_{2})x_{i}(t_{1}),\\
x_{i}(t_{1})x_{j}(t_{2})x_{i}(t_{3})=x_{j}\left(\frac{t_{2}t_{3}}{t_{1}+t_{3}}\right)x_{i}(t_{1}+t_{3})x_{j}\left(\frac{t_{1}t_{2}}{t_{1}+t_{3}}\right),\hskip 79.6678pt

where
x_{i}(t)\coloneqq\exp(E_{i,i+1}t)
is the 1-parameter subgroup in
\operatorname{SL}_{n}
corresponding to the positive simple root
\alpha_{i}
. Here the indices
i,j
are adjacent, and
i,k
non-adjacent, in the Dynkin diagram.∎

Finally, of key importance are the intersection theory of weave cycles and their associated variables, especially for the Lusztig cycles in
Definition

2.1
. The precise formulas for their intersections are detailed in
[
CGG+25b
, Sections 4.5 & 4.6]
, and the variables are described in
[
CGG+25b
, Section 5.2]
, cf. also
[
CW24
, Section 4]
. In particular, the collection of Lusztig cycles determines an intersection quiver, as illustrated in
Figure

1
.

2.2
Ingredients on perverse schobers

The weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
, introduced in
Section

3
, will be a perverse schober parametrized by a ribbon graph
{\bf G}
. Let us recall the key definitions and properties of such perverse schobers. We refer to
[
KS14
]
,
[
Chr22a
, Sections 3.1 & 3.2]
, or
[
CHQ23
, Section 3]
for further details.

2.2.1
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schobers

Let us describe
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schobers, and the ingredients needed to define them.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
{\bf G}
be a ribbon graph. By definition, its exit path category
\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})\in\operatorname{Cat}_{\infty}
is the nerve of the
1
-category whose objects are the vertices and edges of
{\bf G}
, and whose non-identity morphisms are of the form
v\to e
, where
e
is an edge of
{\bf G}
incident to a vertex
v
.∎

By the exodromy equivalence, cf. e.g.
[
PT22
, Theorem 5.17]
, constructible sheaves on
{\bf G}
(understood as a stratified space) valued in the
\infty
-category of small stable
\infty
-categories
\operatorname{St}
can equivalently be described as functors
\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})\to\operatorname{St}
. Thus we can use
\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
in
Definition

2.3
and have
\operatorname{Fun}(\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}),\operatorname{St})
as a model for constructible sheaves on
{\bf G}
with values in the
\infty
-category of stable
\infty
-categories. The notion of perversity in classical sheaf theory, cf.
[
BBD82
]
, has the following incarnation in the context of such sheaves of categories on ribbon graphs.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\bf G}_{n}
be the ribbon graph with a single vertex
v
and
n
incident external edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
. The ribbon graph
{\bf G}_{n}
is said to be the
n
-spider
{\bf G}_{n}
. A perverse schober on
{\bf G}_{n}
is defined as follows:

Definition 2.4
.

A perverse schober on the
n
-spider
{\bf G}_{n}
is a functor
\mathcal{F}\colon\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{n})\to\operatorname{St}
such that:

(1)

If
n=1
, the functor

F\colon\mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}\,,\qquad\mbox{where }\mathcal{V}\coloneqq F(v),\quad\mathcal{N}\coloneqq F(e),\quad F\coloneqq\mathcal{F}(v\to e),

given by restricting to the unique edge
e
is required to be spherical.
2
2

2

F
is spherical if it admits a right adjoint
G
, and both the cotwist functor
T_{\mathcal{V}}\coloneqq\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{V}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{unit}}GF)\in\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V})
and twist functor
T_{\mathcal{N}}\coloneqq\operatorname{cof}(FG\xrightarrow{\operatorname{counit}}\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{N}})\in\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N})
are auto-equivalences.

Previous papers of the second author used different conventions for the twist and cotwists functors. The convention employed here is the standard in the literature on spherical objects. It is employed to simplify the notation in the computations involving spherical objects.

(2)

If
n\geq 2
, the collection of functors

(F_{i}\colon\mathcal{V}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}},\qquad\mbox{where }\mathcal{V}^{n}\coloneqq F(v),\quad\mathcal{N}_{i}\coloneqq F(e_{i}),\quad F_{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}(v\to e_{i}),

satisfies that

(a)

there exist adjunctions
F_{i}\dashv G_{i}\dashv H_{i}
for some
G_{i},H_{i}
,

(b)

G_{i}
is fully faithful, which is equivalent to
F_{i}G_{i}\simeq\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}
via the counit,

(c)

F_{i}\circ G_{i-1}
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories,
3
3

3

The convention used in
[
CDW23
]
is that
F_{i}G_{i+1}
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories instead. This difference corresponds to reversing the orientation of the plane containing the
n
-spider. In the convention used here, the wrapping in the resulting partially wrapped Fukaya categories will be counterclockwise.

(d)

F_{i}\circ G_{j}\simeq 0
if
j\neq i,i-1
,

(e)

\operatorname{fib}(H_{i-1})=\operatorname{fib}(F_{i})
as full subcategories of
\mathcal{V}^{n}
.∎

In
Definition

2.4
.(1), the functor
F
uniquely determines
\mathcal{F}
as
v,e
are the only objects and
v\to e
is the only 1-morphism in the 1-category defining
\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{n})
. Similarly, in
Definition

2.4
.(2), the functors
(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})
uniquely determine
\mathcal{F}
for
n\geq 2
.
Definition

2.4
provides local perversity conditions at
{\bf G}_{n}
. Passing to Grothendieck groups, these conditions imply that the arising diagram encodes the sections of a perverse sheaf on the disc with support on
{\bf G}_{n}
, see
[
KS16
]
. This notion is globalized to a ribbon graph as follows:

Definition 2.5

(Perverse schober on
{\bf G}
)
.

Let
{\bf G}
be a ribbon graph. By definition,
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober is a functor

\mathcal{F}\colon\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})\rightarrow\operatorname{St}

such that, for each
n
-valent vertex
v
of
{\bf G}
, the restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to the coslice category
\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})_{v/}
determines a perverse schober on the
n
-spider, as in
Definition

2.4
.∎

We will mostly be interested in
k
-linear perverse schobers, meaning that the functor
\mathcal{F}\colon\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})\rightarrow\operatorname{St}
factors through
\operatorname{LinCat}_{k}^{\operatorname{sm}}\to\operatorname{St}
.

The
\infty
-category of global sections of a
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober is defined as follows:

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober.

(i)

By definition, the
\infty
-category of global sections of
\mathcal{F}
is

R\Gamma({\bf G},\mathcal{F})\coloneqq\operatorname{lim}(\mathcal{F})\in\operatorname{St}\,.

Note that
R\Gamma({\bf G},\mathcal{F})
can be identified with the
\infty
-category of coCartesian sections of the Grothendieck construction
p\colon\Gamma(\mathcal{F})\to\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
of
\mathcal{F}
, see
[
Lur24
,
Prop. 05RX
]
. Since
\mathcal{F}
arises from a strictly commuting diagram
\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})\to\operatorname{Set}_{\Delta}
, there is an explicit simplicial set version of the Grothendieck construction
[
Lur24
,
Def. 025X
]
.

(ii)

By definition, the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{L}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})
of lax sections of
\mathcal{F}
is the
\infty
-category of all sections of the Grothendieck construction
p\colon\Gamma(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
of
\mathcal{F}
. Note that
\mathcal{L}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})
describes the lax limit of
\mathcal{F}
in the
(\infty,2)
-category of stable
\infty
-categories.∎

In
Definition

2.6
,
R\Gamma({\bf G},\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{L}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})
is a full subcategory. The focus in this manuscript is on
R\Gamma({\bf G},\mathcal{F})
, but
\mathcal{L}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})
is used for gluing constructions of global sections, see for instance
Section

7.1.1
.

Remark 2.7
.

A vertex
v
of a
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober
\mathcal{F}
is said to be a singularity if the fiber of the functor

\prod_{i=2}^{n}\mathcal{F}(v\to e_{i}):\mathcal{F}(v)\longrightarrow\prod_{i=2}^{n}\mathcal{F}(e)

is not equivalent to zero. For
n=1
, the fiber above is taken to be
\mathcal{F}(v)
. This fiber is also called the
\infty
-category of vanishing cycles of
\mathcal{F}
at
v
, and it is equivalent to the fiber of any other collection of
n-1
of the
n
functors
\{\mathcal{F}(v\to e_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
.∎

Given a ribbon graph
{\bf G}
together with a chosen collection of internal edges, we can contract these internal edges to produce a new ribbon graph
{\bf G}^{\prime}
. This is denoted by
c\colon{\bf G}\to{\bf G}^{\prime}
, indicating that
{\bf G}^{\prime}
arises via contraction from
{\bf G}
. In order to understand the behavior of weave schobers under weave equivalences and weave mutations, we need the following result on how
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober behave under such contractions:

Proposition 2.8

(​​
[
Chr22a
, Prop. 4.28]
&
[
CHQ23
, Lemma 3.16]
)
.

Let
c\colon{\bf G}\to{\bf G}^{\prime}
be a contraction of ribbon graphs.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober and assume that
c
does not contract two singularities of
\mathcal{F}
into a single vertex. Then there exists a canonical
{\bf G}^{\prime}
-parametrized perverse schober
c_{*}(\mathcal{F})
together with a canonical equivalence of
\infty
-categories

R\Gamma({\bf G},\mathcal{F})\simeq R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\prime},c_{*}(\mathcal{F}))\,.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be a
{\bf G}^{\prime}
-parametrized perverse schober and choose for every singularity
v\in{\bf G}_{0}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}
a preimage under
c
in
{\bf G}_{0}
. Then there exists a canonical
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober
c^{*}(\mathcal{F})
together with a canonical equivalence of perverse schobers
c_{*}c^{*}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\simeq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.∎

Proposition

2.8
will be used shortly when stating and proving
Lemma

2.12
below.

2.2.2
A model for
{\bf G}_{2}
-parametrized perverse schobers

For weave schobers, the ribbon graphs we consider have mostly 2-valent vertices. There is a useful construction of perverse schobers on the
2
-spider
{\bf G}_{2}
from spherical functors, as follows. Given a spherical functor
F\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{N}
, let
\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}
be defined by the following pullback of
\infty
-categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

where
\operatorname{ev_{0}}
is evaluation of a functor
\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{N}
at the vertex
\{0\}
of
\Delta^{1}
. Note that
\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}
describes a model for the
(\infty,2)
-categorical lax limit of the diagram given by
F
. Concretely, an object in
\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}
can be identified with a tuple

(X,Y,\eta),\qquad\mbox{where }X\in\mathcal{V},\ Y\in\mathcal{N},\text{ and }\eta\colon F(X)\to Y\in\mathcal{V}\mbox{ is a morphism}.

(12)

The following construction of
{\bf G}_{2}
-parametrized perverse schobers from a spherical functors is proven in
[
Chr22a
, Prop. 3.7]
:

Lemma 2.9

(Perverse schobers on
{\bf G}_{2}
from spherical functors)
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{N}
be a spherical functor. Then the functors

\varrho_{1}\colon\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}\to\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{N})\xrightarrow{(\{1\}\subset\Delta^{1})^{*}}\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{1}

and

\varrho_{2}\colon\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}\to\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{N})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{cof}}\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{2}

together define a perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{F}
on
{\bf G}_{2}
, with
\mathcal{F}_{F}(v)\coloneqq\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{F}_{F}(e_{i})\coloneqq\mathcal{N}
for
i=1,2
.∎

In the description of
Equation

12
, the functors
\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2}
from
Lemma

2.9
act on objects as

\varrho_{1}(X,Y,\eta)=Y\,,\quad\varrho_{2}(X,Y,\eta)=\operatorname{cof}(\eta)\,.

(13)

Later, we will use the left and right adjoints
\varrho_{1}^{L},\varrho_{1}^{R}:\mathcal{N}_{1}\to\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}
of
\varrho_{1}
, which are given on objects as

\varrho_{1}^{L}(Y)=(0,Y,0),\qquad\varrho_{1}^{R}(Y)=(G(Y),Y,\operatorname{counit}\colon FG(Y)\to Y)\,,

where
G
is the right adjoint of
F
.

Notation 2.10
.

We use the following notation for perverse schobers on ribbon graphs with
2
-valent and
1
-valent vertices, based on the local model from
Lemma

2.9
. Specifically, we can describe a perverse schober in such a ribbon graph by labeling each vertex by a spherical functor
F\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{N}
and each adjacent edge either by
\varrho_{1}
or
\varrho_{2}
, in the
2
-valent case, or
\varrho_{1}=F
in the
1
-valent case, possibly composed with an autoequivalence
E:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{N}
. For instance, we can draw the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E\circ\varrho_{2},\varrho_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{2}}

(14)

This notation in
Equation

14
thus describes a perverse schober on a
2
-valent graph with
2
vertices. In the exit path description, this schober corresponds to the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F^{\prime}}\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\circ\varrho_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{1}}

∎

Remark 2.11
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{N}
be a spherical functor and
Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathcal{N}
two objects. We can ask what are the lifts of
Y_{1},Y_{2}
to an object
W\in\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}
satisfying that
\varrho_{1}(W)\simeq Y_{1}
,
\varrho_{2}(W)\simeq Y_{2}
. To specify such an object
W=(X,Y_{1},\eta)
, we must supply an object
X\in\mathcal{V}
, together with a fiber and cofiber sequence in
\mathcal{N}

F(X)\xlongrightarrow{\eta}Y_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{2}\,.

(15)

Thus, the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{V}\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{F}\mathcal{N}
can be understood as parametrizing
F
-relative triangles in
\mathcal{N}
, as in
Equation

15
. Lifting a collection of local sections on the edges of
{\bf G}
of a
{\bf G}
-parameterized perverse schober, which is locally modeled as above, to a global section thus amounts to specifying such a relative triangle for each vertex of
{\bf G}
.∎

Figure 8:
A sequence of spans of contractions realizing the clockwise
\pi
-rotation for the leftmost ribbon graph
{\bf G}
. The key functors for the schober
\mathcal{F}
on the left are indicated in orange, and those for the resulting schober
(\Pi_{2})_{*}\Pi_{1}^{*}(\pi_{2})_{*}\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F})
, depicted on the right.

2.2.3
Hurwitz moves for perverse schobers

Consider a ribbon graph
{\bf G}
with two adjacent
2
-valent vertices
v,v^{\prime}
, cf.
Figure

9
(left). We can perform two rotations by
\pi
exchanging
v
and
v^{\prime}
, either counterclockwise, as in
Figure

9
, or clockwise, as illustrated in
Figure

8
. The following lemma describes how a
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober changes when performing such rotations, which is a schober analogue of the classical Hurwitz moves, cf.
[
Hur91
, Section II.6]
or
[
Sei08
, Section (16d)]
. In its statement and proof we use
2.10
above.

Figure 9:
Similar to
Figure

8
, this depicts a sequence of spans of contractions realizing the counterclockwise
\pi
-rotation for the leftmost ribbon graph
{\bf G}
. The schober
\mathcal{F}
on the left is written in orange, and the resulting schober
(\Pi_{2})_{*}\Pi_{1}^{*}(\pi_{2})_{*}\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F})
is depicted on the right.

Lemma 2.12
.

(1)

Consider the two spans of contractions of ribbon graphs in
Figure

8
. Denote the leftmost ribbon graph by
{\bf G}
and the rightmost ribbon graph by
{\bf G}^{\prime}
. Let
F,F^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}
be two spherical functors and
\mathcal{F}
the
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober

\mathcal{F}\quad\coloneqq\hbox to171.22pt{\vbox to17.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 85.6106pt\lower-7.41864pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
