3
The perverse schober of a Demazure weave

This section introduces the perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
in
Section

3.1
, and establishes the behavior of its global sections under weave equivalence and weave mutation, in
Section

3.2
.

Figure 10:
A weave
\mathfrak{w}
, depicted left, and its associated graph
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
, drawn to its right. The trivalent vertices
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
of
\mathfrak{w}
and the associated vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}
are also depicted, with
v_{i}
at the same height as
p_{i}
,
i\in[1,m]
. The edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
of
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
are also drawn, with
e_{i}
denoting the edge between
v_{i-1}
and
v_{i}
. The weave schober
{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}}
is a perverse schober on
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
.

3.1
Definition of weave schobers

Let
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\longrightarrow\delta(\beta)
be an
n
-stranded Demazure weave. The height function endows the trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
with a total order, increasing as we read bottom-to-top. We assume that
\mathfrak{w}
is generic, in that no two vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
are at the same height. For instance, if we consider
\mathfrak{w}
as a raked weave as in
[
CGG+25b
, Section 5.1.1]
, the horizontal right semirays
\{r_{v}\}_{v\in V(\mathfrak{w})}
do not intersect each other. These semirays
r_{v}
are depicted in an example as the dashed yellow segments in
Figure

12
. As before, we let
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
be the trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
, ordered from bottom-to-top by height, cf. e.g.
Figure

10
. Let us now start describing the perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to
\mathfrak{w}
.

Domain of definition
. Let
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
be the linear spanning graph of the
1
-gon with
m
vertices, as depicted in
Figure

10
. In practice, we draw the graph
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
such that:

(i)

{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
is drawn vertically to the right side of
\mathfrak{w}
,

(ii)

The
m
vertices of
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
are labeled by
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
from bottom-to-top, and the edges by
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
, with
e_{1}
being the bottom external (non-compact) edge.

(iii)

The
i
th vertex
v_{i}
of
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
is drawn at the same height as the trivalent vertex
p_{i}\in\mathfrak{w}
, so that
v_{m}
lies on top,
v_{1}
on the bottom, and the external edge lies below
v_{1}
.

See Figures
11
and
12
for illustrations of such
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
. We also consider a generic point
v_{-\infty}
in the non-compact edge
e_{1}
. By construction, the perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to
\mathfrak{w}
will be a perverse schober parametrized by
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
.

Generic stalks
. We choose the generic stalks of the perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{w}
, i.e. the stalks at the points of any edge, to be the perfect derived
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(\Pi_{2}(A_{n}))
of the (additive) derived preprojective algebra
\Pi_{2}(A_{n})
of type
A_{n}
, also known as the
2
-Calabi–Yau completion of type
A_{n}

[
Kel11
]
. This choice is in part motivated by the equivalence between
\mathcal{D}
and the wrapped Fukaya category of the 4-dimensional
A_{n}
-Milnor fibre, cf. e.g.
[
LU21
, Section 3]
.
4
4

4

The additive derived preprojective algebra
\Pi_{2}(A_{n})
is equivalent to the multiplicative derived preprojective algebra of type
A_{n}
, and this is true irrespective of the characteristic of the field
k
.
We denote by
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\in\mathcal{D}
the 1-dimensional modules associated with the
n
vertices of the
A_{n}
-quiver, i.e. the simple modules of the vertices in the standard heart of
\mathcal{D}
. We note that
S_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
is
2
-spherical for all
1\leq i\leq n
, that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{i},S_{j})\simeq k
if
|i-j|=1
, and
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{i},S_{j})\simeq 0
if
|i-j|\not=1
.

Figure 11:
The local model for a 6-valent vertex at a weave
\mathfrak{w}
, analyzed in
Example

3.2
. The objects
S_{q_{i}}
associated to the points
q_{i}\in\mathfrak{w}
are depicted. If
q_{i}
has color
i_{j}\in S_{n}
, these objects
S_{q_{i}}
are obtained by following the yellow dashed lines to the right from
q_{i}
, transforming the initial
S_{i_{j}}
at
q_{i}
by twisting by
T_{S_{i_{k}}}
every time a weave
i_{k}
-line is crossed.

Spherical functors
. The spherical functors describing
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
will be defined by spherical objects, which are themselves constructed as follows. Let
p\in\mathfrak{w}
be a point on the Demazure weave, consider the horizontal right semiray
r_{p}
based at
p
, and suppose that
r_{p}
does not intersect any vertex of
\mathfrak{w}
. The intersections of
r_{p}
with
\mathfrak{w}
determine a positive braid word
\beta_{p}=s_{i_{0}}s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{i_{l(p)}}
by reading the
s_{i}
-labels in the associated weave edges left-to-right, cf.
Figure

11
. In these hypotheses and notation, we define:

Definition 3.1

(Spherical objects from weave points)
.

The spherical object associated to
p\in\mathfrak{w}
is

S_{p}\coloneqq S_{\beta_{p}}=T^{-1}_{S_{i_{l(p)}}}\cdots T^{-1}_{S_{i_{2}}}T^{-1}_{S_{i_{1}}}(S_{i_{0}})\in\mathcal{D}

where
T_{S_{j}}=\operatorname{cofib}(\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{j},\mathchar 45\relax)\otimes S_{j}\to\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}})
is the twist functor.∎

In the case that
p\in\mathfrak{w}
is a trivalent vertex,
S_{p}
in
Definition

3.1
coincides with the
\mathfrak{w}
-transport of
S_{i_{0}}
along the right thimble
\wp_{r}(p)
, see
Section

7.2
below. More generally,
Definition

3.1
should be understood as the transport of
S_{i_{0}}
along the horizontal right semiray
r_{p}
. In
Definition

3.1
, the inverse
T_{S_{i_{j}}}^{-1}
of the twist functor
T_{S_{i_{j}}}
exists because
S_{i_{j}}
is a 2-spherical object, and thus
T_{S_{i_{j}}}
is an auto-equivalence by
[
ST01
, Prop. 2.10]
. In this case we have the formula
T_{S_{i_{j}}}^{-1}\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}\to\operatorname{Mor}(\mathchar 45\relax,S_{i_{j}})^{\ast}\otimes S_{i_{j}})
, cf. loc. cit.

Example 3.2
.

Consider the 3-stranded weave
\mathfrak{w}
in
Figure

11
, which is the local model for a 6-valent vertex in a weave
\mathfrak{w}
. The positive braid word at the top is
s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}
, and the one at the bottom is
s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}
. Given the points
q_{i}\in\mathfrak{w}
and their associated dashed yellow lines
r_{q_{i}}
as depicted,
i\in[1,6]
, the associated spherical objects are

\displaystyle S_{q_{1}}

\displaystyle\simeq{T^{-1}_{S_{1}}}{T^{-1}_{S_{2}}}S_{1}\simeq S_{2}

\displaystyle S_{q_{2}}

\displaystyle\simeq{T^{-1}_{S_{1}}}S_{2}

\displaystyle S_{q_{3}}

\displaystyle\simeq S_{1}

\displaystyle S_{q_{4}}

\displaystyle\simeq S_{2}

\displaystyle S_{q_{5}}

\displaystyle\simeq{T^{-1}_{S_{2}}}S_{1}

\displaystyle S_{q_{6}}

\displaystyle\simeq{T^{-1}_{S_{2}}}{T^{-1}_{S_{1}}}S_{2}\simeq S_{1},

where we have used the equivalence
{T_{S_{1}}}S_{2}\simeq T_{S_{2}}^{-1}S_{1}
. These are the objects at the rightmost end of the dashed yellow lines in
Figure

11
.∎

Weave schobers
. Given a spherical object
S\in\mathcal{D}
, we denote by
\mathchar 45\relax\otimes S\colon\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)\to\mathcal{D}
the corresponding spherical functor. Let us use
2.10
to define the perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
of
\mathfrak{w}
:

Definition 3.3

(Perverse schober of a weave)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave with trivalent vertices
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
and associated graph
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
. By definition, the perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to
\mathfrak{w}
is the
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
-parametrized perverse schober given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchar 45\relax\otimes S_{p_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchar 45\relax\otimes S_{p_{m-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchar 45\relax\otimes S_{p_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varrho_{2},\varrho_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varrho_{2},\varrho_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{2}}

We refer to any such perverse schober as a weave schober.∎

Figure 12:
The 3-stranded weave
\mathfrak{w}
for
Example

3.4
. The positive braid word at the top is
\beta=s_{2}s_{1}(s_{1}s_{2})^{3}
, the braid word at the bottom is its Demazure product
\delta(\beta)=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
. The graph
G_{\mathfrak{w}}
over which the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
is depicted to the right of
\mathfrak{w}
. The vertices of
G_{\mathfrak{w}}
are labeled
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4},v_{5}
bottom to top. The south point
v_{-\infty}
is depicted in a round green circle.

Example 3.4
.

Consider the 3-stranded weave
\mathfrak{w}
in
Figure

12
, so
n=3
and
m=5
. The graph
G_{\mathfrak{w}}
is depicted to its right, with the horizontal slicings depicted in dashed yellow lines. These lines match each trivalent vertex
p_{i}
of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
to a vertex
v_{i}
of the graph
G_{\mathfrak{w}}
,
i\in[1,m]
. The weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
is determined by the spherical objects
S_{p_{i}}
at the vertex
v_{i}
. The spherical object
S_{p_{i}}
is determined by
\mathfrak{w}
: starting with the simple
S_{i_{j}}
at the trivalent
p_{i}
, where
i_{j}
is the weave color of
p_{i}
, we transport
S_{i_{j}}
to the right along the dashed yellow lines. By
Definition

3.1
, the rule for such transport is that we apply the inverse twist
T_{S_{i_{k}}}^{-1}
as we transport across a weave
i_{k}
-line, cf.
Section

7.2
. Such transport for
i=4,5
is depicted in
Figure

13
. Specifically, the spherical objects for this weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
are

S_{p_{1}}\simeq T^{-1}_{S_{2}}T^{-1}_{S_{1}}(S_{2})\simeq S_{1},\qquad S_{p_{2}}\simeq S_{2},\qquad S_{p_{3}}\simeq(T_{S_{1}}T_{S_{2}})^{-2}(S_{2})\simeq T^{-1}_{S_{2}}(S_{1}[1])\,,

S_{p_{4}}\simeq T^{-1}_{S_{2}}T^{-1}_{S_{1}}(S_{2})\simeq S_{1},\qquad S_{p_{5}}\simeq T^{-1}_{S_{2}}(T_{S_{2}}T_{S_{1}})^{-2}(S_{1})\simeq T^{-1}_{S_{2}}T^{-1}_{S_{1}}(S_{2}[1])\simeq S_{1}[1]\,

where we have used the three equivalences
T_{S_{2}}^{-1}T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})\simeq S_{1}
, or equivalently
T_{S_{2}}(S_{1})\simeq T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
,
T_{S_{1}}^{-1}T_{S_{2}}^{-1}(S_{1})\simeq S_{2}
, and
T_{S_{i}}^{-1}(S_{i})\simeq S_{i}[1]
, for
i=1,2
.∎

Figure 13:
The weave
\mathfrak{w}
from
Figure

12
featuring in
Example

3.4
. The left-to-right transport of the simple
S_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
, resp.
S_{1}
, for the weave trivalent vertex
p_{4}
, resp.
p_{5}
, is illustrated.

Remark 3.5
.

The stable
\infty
-category of global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
can be more concretely understood as the perfect derived
\infty
-category of the directed dg category on the object
S_{p_{1}},\dots,S_{p_{m}}
in the dg avatar of
\mathcal{D}
in the sense of
[
Sei08
]
, see also the notes
[
Chr26a
]
for more details.

We denote the left adjoint of the (spherical) evaluation functor
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\to\mathcal{F}(e_{1})
of global section to the bottom edge
e_{1}
by
\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{e_{1}}
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave. The functor

\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{e_{1}}\colon\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(e_{1})\to R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})

admits a weak relative left
3
-Calabi–Yau structure and the functor

\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(e_{1})=\mathcal{D}

admits a weak relative right
3
-Calabi–Yau structure.

Proof.

For each
2
-spherical object
S\in\mathcal{D}
, by
[
Chr26b
, Lem. 6.4]
, the functor
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S,\mathchar 45\relax)\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)
admits a weak relative left
3
-Calabi–Yau structure, and the functor
(\mathchar 45\relax)\otimes S\colon\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)\to\mathcal{D}
admits a weak relative right
3
-Calabi–Yau structure. The assertion then follows from
[
Chr26b
, Prop. 5.2]
and
[
Chr26b
, Thm. 5.7]
.
∎

Remark 3.7

(On generalizing to finite Dynkin type)
.

The calculus for Demazure weaves is defined for any finite Dynkin type
\mathfrak{g}
, cf.
[
CGG+25b
, Sections 4 & 6]
. Specifically, the simply-laced cases are constructed directly, and the non-simply-laced cases via folding, cf.
[
CGG+25b
, Section 6.2]
. In line with this, the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
in
Definition

3.3
can be constructed analogously in any of the simply-laced cases, where the stalk category
\mathcal{D}
is taken to be the derived
\infty
-category of the derived preprojective algebra of the corresponding type
\mathfrak{g}
.
5
5

5

If
\mbox{char}(k)=0
, the additive and multiplicative preprojective algebras are isomorphic for simply-laced Dynkin cases.
The folding technique applies to extend the construction of the weave schober in the non-simply-laced cases. In this more general framework, it is our conceptual understanding that the main results in this manuscript generalize to any finite Dynkin type in characteristic 0. A number of technical details would need to be written and verified, and thus we leave the development of categorical weave calculus for any finite Dynkin type as a conjectural generalization.∎

3.2
The schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
under weave equivalences and mutations

Consider a weave equivalence
\mathfrak{w}\simeq\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
between Demazure weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
, cf.
Section

2.1.1
or
[
CGG+25b
, Section 4.2]
. By definition, such an equivalence is said to be height-preserving if the height-induced order of the trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
coincides with that of the trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
. Any weave equivalence can be expressed as a (possibly alternating) composition of height-preserving weave equivalences and the local weave equivalence depicted in
Figure

14
. We refer to these particular weave equivalences as pulley moves: specifically, a forward pulley move exchanges
Figure

14
(left) for
Figure

14
(right), and a backwards pulley move does the reverse.

The distinction between height-preserving weave equivalences and pulley moves is crucial in the study of weave schobers. Indeed, the exchange of heights for trivalent vertices of a weave, whether caused by a pulley move or a weave mutation, is what modifies the weave schober in an interesting manner. The precise statement reads as follows:

Figure 14:
The local weave equivalence that exchanges the height of trivalent vertices, referred to as a pulley move. In this equivalence, there are
no
trivalent vertices whose height is between those of the two trivalent vertices depicted in this picture. The weave lines in color orange indicate arbitrary and possibly different
s_{k}\in W
labels, and the two permutations
s_{i},s_{j}\in W
are also arbitrary.

Theorem 3.8
.

In the notation above, the following holds:

(1)

Let
\mathfrak{w}\simeq\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be a height-preserving weave equivalence. Then, there is an equivalence of weave schobers
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
.

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{w}\simeq\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be a forward pulley move or
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be related by a forward weave mutation, exchanging the heights of the vertices
p_{i-1}
and
p_{i}
. Then, the weave schobers
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
are related as follows:

\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}=\hbox to55.47pt{\vbox to139.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 26.34784pt\lower-69.6401pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(22)

(3)

Let
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be two Demazure weaves and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
their associated weave perverse schobers. Then there is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories of global sections

R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}})\,.

(23)

In particular, the
\infty
-category of global sections of a weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
is an invariant of
\beta
for any Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
, independent of the choice of
\mathfrak{w}
, up to equivalence.
∎

The weave schober in the right of
Equation

22
will also be denoted by
\mu_{i}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
and referred to as the schober obtained from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
via a forward or left (Hurwitz) move. The equivalence of global sections between the two perverse schobers
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
, with
\mathfrak{w}
and
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
related by a pulley move, is a schober-theoretic version of
[
Sei08
, Lem. 5.22]
.

(i) A height exchange for a 6-valent vertex, in
(i.1)
, and for a 4-valent vertex, in
(i.2)
. (ii) Canceling pairs, for 6-valent vertices in

.

3.2.1
Proof of
Theorem

3.8
and
B
.(1)

The proofs of Parts (1) and (2) and
Theorem

3.8
are mutually independent, and Part (3) follows from combining them. We refer to
Section

2.1.1
for the necessary ingredients on weave equivalences and weave mutations.

Proof of Part (1)
. It suffices to establish the equivalence of weave schobers by verifying it for the weave equivalences in
[
CGGS24
, Section 4.2]
, cf. also
[
CZ22
, Theorem 4.2]
. Specifically, these are:

(i)

Height exchange of 4- and 6-vertices
, as depicted in
Figure

15
.(i).

(ii)

Canceling pairs of 4- and 6-vertices
, as depicted in
Figure

15
.(ii).

(iii)

Commutation of distant colors
, which involve an
s_{i}
-weave line passing above a weave vertex involving
s_{j}
-labels with
|i-j|\geq 2
, see e.g.
Figure

16
.(b). Confer also Moves VI and VI’ in
[
CZ22
, Figures 24&25]
, or
[
CGGS24
, Subsection 4.2.3]
.

(iv)

Pushthrough move
, as depicted in
Figure

16
.(a).

(v)

Zamolodchikov relation
, as depicted in
[
CZ22
, Figure 106]
, cf. also Move IV in Figure 22 in ibid, or
[
CGGS24
, Subsection 4.2.6]
.

For
(i)
in the case of a 6-valent vertex, the invariance of the weave schober follows from the fact that the spherical objects
S_{p_{i}}
do not change up to equivalence due to the braid relation

T_{S_{i}}^{-1}T_{S_{i+1}}^{-1}T_{S_{i}}^{-1}\simeq T_{S_{i+1}}^{-1}T_{S_{i}}^{-1}T_{S_{i+1}}^{-1}.

(25)

Indeed, if one considers the path
\wp_{l}
, resp.
\wp_{r}
in the left, resp. right, of
Figure

15
.(i.1), then for any object
L\in\mathcal{D}
at the left endpoint of such paths the resulting object when traversing these paths left-to-right are equivalent by
Equation

25
.
6
6

6

In the notation of
\mathfrak{w}
-transports, introduced in
Definition

7.12
, this equivalence reads
\mbox{Hol}_{\mathfrak{w}}(L,\wp_{l})\simeq\mbox{Hol}_{\mathfrak{w}}(L,\wp_{r}).
It thus follows that any spherical object
S_{p_{i}}
does not change when the height of a 6-valent vertex of
\mathfrak{w}
is changed. For
(i)
in the case of a 4-valent vertex, the same argument applies but using the commutation relation

T_{S_{i}}^{-1}T_{S_{j}}^{-1}\simeq T_{S_{j}}^{-1}T_{S_{i}}^{-1},\quad|i-j|\geq 2\,.

(26)

The invariance of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
under the weave equivalences
(ii)
,
(iii)
for 4 and 6-valent vertices, and
(v)
is implied similarly by Equations
25
and
26
, as they do not involve 3-valent vertices. The remaining cases are
(iii)
, in the case of a 3-valent vertex, and the pushthrough move
(iv)
, as depicted in
Figure

16
. In the former case, invariance of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
follows from
T_{S_{j}}(S_{i})\simeq S_{i}
for
|i-j|\geq 2
. In the latter case, it follows from the equivalence
T_{S_{i}}^{-1}T_{S_{i+1}}^{-1}S_{i}\simeq S_{i+1}
.

Figure 16:
(a) The pushthrough move, where blue color for a weave line indicates
s_{i}
-label and red color indicates
s_{i+1}
-label. (b) A case of commutation of distant colors, with a weave line labeled by
s_{j}
, in green, passing through a trivalent
s_{i}
-vertex,
|i-j|\geq 2
.

Proof of Part (2)
. In either a forward pulley move or a forward weave mutation, only the heights of the two involved trivalent vertices are exchanged. It thus follows that the spherical objects associated to any other trivalent vertices (other than these two) remain identical. By direct computation, the effect of such height exchange on the two spherical objects associated to these two trivalent vertices being exchanged yields
Equation

22
.

Proof of Part (3)
. By
[
CGG+25b
, Lemma 4.4]
or
[
CGGS24
, Theorem 4.6]
any two such weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
are related by a sequence of weave equivalences and weave mutations. Therefore the statement follows from Part (1), and Part (2), combined with
Lemma

2.12
.(1). Indeed,
Lemma

2.12
.(1) supplies the required equivalence of global sections by noting that
\mu_{i}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
is the result of pull-push along the zig-zag realizing the clockwise
\pi
-rotation of the ribbon graph in
Figure

8
.∎

Finally, we emphasize that
Theorem

3.8
.(3) is equivalent to Theorem
B
.(1). Thus Theorem
B
.(1) is now proven. The proofs of Parts (2), (3) and (4) of Theorem
B
are more challenging, and are the content of
Section

8
.

4
Categorical Lusztig cycles

The object of this section is to construct the categorical Lusztig cycles associated to a weave
\mathfrak{w}
, which will be global sections of the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, i.e. objects in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
. Their construction requires as input a number of new results that will be proven in
Section

6
. Assuming these results for now, the construction is presented in
Section

4.1
, while
Section

4.2
collects the main statements about them, which are proven in
Section

8
. We however first summarize the construction of the categorical Lusztig cycles as follows:

Outline of the construction
. Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave and
\{\gamma_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\}
its collection of Lusztig cycles,
i\in[1,m]
. For a fixed Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
, any horizontal slice
h\subseteq\mathbb{D}
that does not intersect a weave vertex specifies a weighted braid word
(\beta_{h},{\bf a}_{h})
. In a nutshell, the categorical Lusztig cycle
{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
is constructed by associating to each slice
h
a local object
L_{\beta_{h},{\bf a}_{h}}
with a filtration adapted to the weighted braid word
(\beta_{h},{\bf a}_{h})
, and assembling such
L_{\beta_{h},{\bf a}_{h}}
together as the horizontal slice
h
scans the weave from the vertex
v_{i}
downwards. Specifically, these two steps are as follows:

Step 1
. Given a weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
arising as a slice of a Lusztig cycle, we will show:

\framebox{$\exists!$ rigid filtered object $L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{D}$ whose graded pieces recover $(\beta,{\bf a})$}

(27)

This is established in
Corollary

6.8
, which refines the above statement.

Step 2
. Consider a vertex
p
of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
and a Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
. Let us denote the rigid filtered objects associated to the slice above and below a trivalent vertex by
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
and
L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}
. These rigid objects are provided by Statement (
27
) above. The relation between
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
and
L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}
is as follows:

1.

If
p
is trivalent, we will show in that there exists a cofiber sequence

\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},L_{\beta,{\bf a}})\otimes S_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta}}{{\longrightarrow}}L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\longrightarrow L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}\,.

(28)

Such sequence is proven in
Proposition

6.5
and captures the data for a propagation of
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
past the trivalent, see also
Remark

2.11
for more explanation.

\framebox{At a trivalent, $L_{\beta,{\bf a}}$ and $L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}$ are glued via the cofiber sequence (\ref{eq:cofiberseq_trivalent0}).}\\

(29)

2.

If
p
is hexavalent or tetravalent, we will show that
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\simeq L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}
, cf.
Proposition

6.2
. Then:

\framebox{At a non-trivalent vertex, $L_{\beta,{\bf a}}$ and $L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}$ are identified via $L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\simeq L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}$.}\\

(30)

Let us for now assume Statement (
27
), the cofiber sequence (
28
), and the equivalence in Statement (
30
), and proceed to construct the categorical Lusztig cycles.

4.1
Definition of the categorical Lusztig cycles

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave with
m
trivalent vertices
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
, ordered from bottom to top. The vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}
and edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
of the graph
{\bf G}_{w}
are also ordered bottom to top, cf.
Figure

10
. Given the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, we describe a global section
\mathfrak{s}\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
by specifying:

1.

Its value
\mathfrak{s}(v_{j})
at the vertices
v_{j}
, i.e. an object in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{j})\simeq\mathcal{V}\times_{F_{j}}\mathcal{D}\simeq\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)\times_{F_{j}}\mathcal{D}
, if
i<m
, or in
\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)
, if
i=m
, according to
Section

2.2.2
and
Section

3
, where
F_{j}\coloneqq\mathchar 45\relax\otimes S_{p_{j}}\colon\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)\to\mathcal{D}
.

2.

Its value
\mathfrak{s}(e_{j})
at the edges
e_{j}
, i.e. an object in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(e_{j})\simeq\mathcal{D}
.

3.

Its value
\mathfrak{s}(v_{j}\to e_{j})
on the generization morphisms
v_{j}\to e_{j}
, which is an object in
\mathcal{F}_{w}(v_{j}\to e_{j})
, i.e. a functor
\mathcal{F}_{w}(v_{j})\to\mathcal{F}_{w}(e_{j})
, and similarly for
v_{j}\to e_{j+1}
,
j\in[1,m-1]
, and
v_{m}\to e_{m}
.

These values for
\mathfrak{s}
need to be compatible with each other and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
in order for them to define an actual global section in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, e.g. so it is coCartesian and not just lax, cf.
Definition

2.6
.

Definition 4.1

(Categorical Lusztig cycles)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave with trivalent vertices
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
and fix
i\in[1,m]
. By definition, the categorical Lusztig cycle
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
is the global section of
\mathcal{F}_{w}
characterized as follows:

1.

Its value at the vertex
v_{j}
is

\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(v_{j})\coloneqq\begin{cases}(v_{j};\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p_{j}},L_{\beta_{j+1},{\bf a}_{j+1}}),L_{\beta_{j+1},{\bf a}_{j+1}},\operatorname{ev})\,&\text{if }j\not=i,\\
(v_{i};k[-1],0,0),&\text{if }i=j\text{ and }i<m,\\
(v_{m};k),&\text{if }i=j=m,\\
\end{cases}

where
(\beta_{j},{\bf a}_{j})
is the weighted braid word associated to the Lusztig weave cycle
\gamma_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
and any choice of generic horizontal weave slice lying below
p_{j}
(but above
p_{j-1}
).

2.

Its value at the edge
e_{j}
is
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(e_{j})\coloneqq(e_{j};L_{(\beta_{j},{\bf a}_{j})})
.

3.

The coCartesian edges
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(v_{j}\to e_{j+1})
are determined by the trivial identity

\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{j}\to e_{j+1})(\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(v_{j}))=\varrho_{1}((v_{j};\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p_{j}},L_{\beta_{j+1},{\bf a}_{j+1}}),L_{\beta_{j+1},{\bf a}_{j+1}},\operatorname{ev}))=L_{\beta_{j+1},{\bf a_{j+1}}}=\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(e_{j+1}).

The coCartesian edges
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(v_{j}\to e_{j})
are determined by the equivalence

\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{j}\to e_{j})(\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(v_{j}))=\varrho_{2}((v_{j};\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p_{j}},L_{\beta_{j+1},{\bf a}_{j+1}}),L_{\beta_{j+1},{\bf a}_{j+1}},\operatorname{ev}))=\operatorname{cof}(\operatorname{ev})\simeq L_{\beta_{j},{\bf a}_{j}}=\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(e_{j})\,,

arising from the fiber and cofiber sequence (
28
) and the equivalences (
30
).

∎

In
Definition

4.1
.(2), we use the equivalence in Statement (
30
) so as to have a well-defined value at the edges. Indeed, two generic horizontal weave slices for the edge
e_{j}
might yield different weighted braid words and different filtrations, and so we must ensure the equivalence of the colimit of these filtered objects. Note that, by construction, each
{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
in
Definition

4.1
is indeed a global section of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, since the generization maps connecting vertices and edges are coCartesian.

Remark 4.2
.

A given Lusztig weave cycle
\gamma_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
is supported at and below the vertex
v_{i}
, and thus the associated weighted braid words
(\beta_{j},{\bf a}_{j})
have all weights zero if
j>i
. Since, in general,
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\simeq 0
if all the weights
{\bf a}
are zero, it follows that the categorical Lusztig cycle
{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
is also supported at and below
v_{i}
, i.e.
{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{j})\simeq 0
and
{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}(e_{j})\simeq 0
if
i<j
.∎

For any weave trivalent vertex
p_{i}
with
i<m
, we can depict part of the categorical Lusztig cycle
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
in
Definition

4.1
near the associated vertex
v_{i}
of
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(v_{i+1};0,0,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(v_{i};k[-1],0,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(v_{i-1};\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p_{i-1}},S_{p_{i}}),S_{p_{i}},\eta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e_{i+1};0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e_{i};S_{p_{i}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(e_{i-1};L_{\beta_{i-1},{\bf a}_{i-1}})}}

where, by
Remark

4.2
, all the values to the left of
(v_{i};k[-1],0,0)
must vanish, i.e. they are of the form
(v_{j};0,0,0)
and
(e_{j};0)
.

Remark 4.3
.

For a Demazure
\mathfrak{w}
, the categorical Lusztig cycle
L_{i}^{w}
allows to recover the weave Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
for all
i\in[1,m]
. Indeed, let
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j}}\colon\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\to\mathcal{F}(e_{j})\simeq\mathcal{D}
be the evaluation functor of global sections at the edge
e_{j}
,
j\in[1,m]
. It follows from
Definition

4.1
that
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j}}({\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq L_{\beta_{j},{\bf a}_{j}}
. By
Lemma

5.30
, the object
L_{\beta_{j},{\bf a}_{j}}\in\mathcal{D}
uniquely determines the weight
{\bf a}_{j}
for
\beta_{j}
. Concretely, the grading pieces of the filtered object
L_{\beta_{j},{\bf a}_{j}}
recover the weighted braid word
(\beta_{j},{\bf a}_{j})
, and the collection of such
(\beta_{j},{\bf a}_{j})
uniquely determine
\gamma_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
.∎

Remark 4.4
.

The weave standard thimbles define a full exceptional collection in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, see
Proposition

7.21
below. The stable
\infty
-category
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
thus admits a conical iterated recollement into
m
-many
\mathcal{D}(k)
’s, with the object
k
in the
i
-th component identifying with
\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
. The restriction of a global section to the
j
-th component of the iterated recollement can be expressed as applying the functor
\operatorname{Mor}(\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}},\mathchar 45\relax)
. The
i
-th restriction of
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
is given by
k
. The
j
-th restriction of
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
to
\mathcal{D}(k)
, with
j\not=i
, is given by the
k
-module
\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p_{j}},L_{\beta_{j+1},{\bf a}_{j+1}})\in\mathcal{D}(k)
appearing in
Definition

4.1
in the definition of
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}(v_{j})
.

4.2
The main results on categorical Lusztig cycles

Let us collect the main results that we will establish on the categorical Lusztig cycles from
Definition

4.1
. To ease notation, given a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
with
m
trivalent vertices, we write
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
implicitly understanding that the index always ranges in
i\in[1,m]
.

(1)
First, we show that the collection of categorical Lusztig cycles is invariant under weave equivalence. Specifically, let
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be two Demazure weaves and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
their associated weave schobers. Recall that given a weave equivalence
\mathfrak{w}\simeq\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
,
Theorem

3.8
.(3) provides an equivalence of
\infty
-categories between the global sections
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
, cf.
Equation

23
. The precise invariance reads as follows:

Theorem 4.5

(Categorical Lusztig cycles are invariant under weave equivalence)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be two Demazure weaves,
\mathfrak{w}\simeq\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
a weave equivalence and
\varepsilon:R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}})
the associated equivalence of global sections of their weave schobers
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
. Consider the trivalent vertices
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
of
\mathfrak{w}
and the permutation
\sigma\colon[m]\to[m]
such that
p_{\sigma(1)}^{\prime},\dots,p_{\sigma(m)}^{\prime}
are the ordered trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
after applying the weave equivalence
\mathfrak{w}\simeq\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
. Then

\varepsilon(\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i})\simeq{\bm{L}}_{\sigma(i)}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\quad\forall i\in[1,m].

∎

In a nutshell,
Theorem

4.5
states that weave equivalences yield the same collections of categorical Lusztig cycles, up to reordering. Conceptually, it makes the collection of categorical Lusztig cycles well-defined as a collection of objects assigned to a weave equivalence class, in the same manner that the isomorphism type of the
\infty
-category
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
is well-defined on the equivalence class of
\mathfrak{w}
, even if the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
itself is not.
Theorem

4.5
is proven in
Section

8.2
.

(2)
Second, we show that the collection of categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
,
i\in[1,m]
, associated to a weave
\mathfrak{w}
forms a simple-minded collection. Easing the notation by
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{w}}\coloneqq R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, this means that each categorical Lusztig cycle
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
is indecomposable and that they satisfy the following:

•

The derived Hom
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{w}}}(\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i},\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i})
is coconnective for all
i\in[1,m]
,

•

The derived Hom
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{w}}}(\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i},\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j})[1]
is coconnective, for all
i<j
,
i,j\in[1,m]
,

•

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
generates
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{w}}
under finite limits, colimits, and direct summands.

See
Definition

A.1
, or
[
KY14
, Section 3.2]
and references therein, for more details on simple-minded collections. In
Section

8.1
we prove the following result:

Theorem 4.6

(Categorical Lusztig cycles are simple-minded)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave. Then the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
form a simple-minded collection in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
.∎

A consequence of
Theorem

4.6
is that, intuitively,
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
behave as the simple objects in an abelian category. Specifically,
Theorem

4.6
is equivalent to the following result:

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave. Then there exists a bounded
t
-structure on
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
such that the simple objects in its heart are given by the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
.∎

Corollary

4.7
follows from
Theorem

4.6
by
[
KY14
, Theorem 6.1]
, see also
[
KN13
, Corollary 9.5]
or
[
AN09
, Section 2]
.

(3)
Third, we show that a mutation of Demazure weaves, from
\mathfrak{w}
to
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
, induces a mutation of simple-minded collections, from
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
to
\bm{L}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
. See
Definition

A.6
and
Proposition

A.7
for the definition and explicit description of mutations for simple-minded collections. In particular, weave mutation induces a tilting of the associated hearts. The specific result we prove in
Section

8.3
reads as follows:

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave. Then the following holds:

(i)

Let
\mathfrak{w}\to\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be a backward weave mutation, and suppose it exchanges the height of the
i
-th and
(i-1)
-th trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
. Then the simple-minded collection
\{\bm{L}_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\}
is the backward simple-minded mutation of
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j}\}
at
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
, i.e.

\bm{L}_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\simeq\begin{cases}\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}[-1]&\mbox{ if }i=j,\\
\operatorname{cof}(\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i},\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j})\otimes\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}[-1]\to\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j})&\mbox{ else }.\end{cases}

(ii)

Let
\mathfrak{w}\to\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be a forward weave mutation, and suppose it exchanges the height of the
i
-th and
(i-1)
-th trivalent vertices. Then the simple-minded collection
\{\bm{L}_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\}
is the forward simple-minded mutation of
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j}\}
at
\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
, i.e.

\bm{L}_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\simeq\begin{cases}\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}[1]&\mbox{ if }i=j,\\
\operatorname{fib}(\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j}\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j},\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i})^{*}\otimes\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}[1])&\mbox{ else }.\end{cases}

∎

Theorem
B
in the introduction is a consequence of
Theorem

4.5
,
Theorem

4.6
and
Theorem

4.8
above. The proof of
Theorem

4.5
,
Theorem

4.6
and
Theorem

4.8
is in itself interesting, as it involves introducing a full exceptional collection
\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{w}}
in the
\infty
-category of global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, and developing a recipe to construct the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
from such a full exceptional collection
\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{w}}
. The construction of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{w}}
is achieved in
Section

7
, and see also
Proposition

A.12
and
Theorem

8.2
for its relation to
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
. Similarly to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, though the full exceptional collection
\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{w}}
is useful for computations, it should not be considered as intrinsically attached to the Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
since it is not invariant under weave equivalences.

Remark 4.9
.

Finally, as stated in Theorem
C
, we also show in
Theorem

9.13
that the Koszul dual collection to the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
can be realized by weave cycles once a Ringel-type duality has been applied, cf.
Proposition

9.5
and
Lemma

9.9
. These facts are proven in detail in
Section

9
and consequently establish the analogous results for such dual silting collection as we have for the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
.∎

5
Filtered objects from weighted braid words

The goal of this section is to construct filtered objects
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{D}
from weighted braid words
(\beta,{\bf a})
.

The geometric realizations of these filtered objects are key for the definition of the categorical Lusztig cycles in
Definition

4.1
, see e.g. Statement (
27
). That said, the theory we develop in this section is more general and thus we present it in a self-contained manner, entirely within the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}
, and detached from the theory of weaves and their Lusztig cycles. We shall only specialize to weighted braid words
(\beta,{\bf a})
arising from Lusztig cycles in
Section

6
, where Statement (
27
), in the more precise form of
Corollary

6.8
, is proven using the general techniques developed in the present section.

The input ingredients for this section are:

1.

The perfect derived
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(\Pi_{2}(A_{n}))
of the additive derived preprojective algebra
\Pi_{2}(A_{n})
of type
A_{n}
. Its simples are denoted by
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\in\mathcal{D}
, as in
Section

3.1
.

2.

Given a positive braid word
\beta\coloneqq s_{i_{0}}s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{i_{l}}
, we define its associated spherical object
S_{\beta}
to be

S_{\beta}\coloneqq T^{-1}_{S_{i_{l}}}\cdots T^{-1}_{S_{i_{2}}}T^{-1}_{S_{i_{1}}}(S_{i_{0}})\in\mathcal{D}

(31)

where
T_{S_{j}}=\operatorname{cofib}(\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{j},\mathchar 45\relax)\otimes S_{j}\to\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}})
is the twist functor along the 2-spherical object
S_{j}
. This is in line with
Definition

3.1
.

The section is organized as follows:

(a)

Section

5.1
establishes homological bounds on
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\alpha},S_{\beta})
for certain types of positive braid words
\alpha,\beta
, and shows that the 2-spherical semi-twists preserve rigidity. Both these technical ingredients are crucial for the existence and uniqueness of the filtered objects
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{D}
associated to weighted braid words
(\beta,{\bf a})
.

(b)

Section

5.2
provides a diagrammatic model, based on the study of matching paths, to describe a subcategory
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
.
Lemma

5.25
will establish that
\mathcal{M}
is stable under a class of semi-twists. These results in
Section

5.2
and the subcategory
\mathcal{M}
are used to prove the existence of the filtered objects
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{D}
associated to certain weighted braid words
(\beta,{\bf a})
.

(c)

Section

5.3
proves the uniqueness of the rigid filtered objects
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
in
\mathcal{D}
, should they exist, and establishes a criterion, based on the diagrammatic model of
Section

5.2
, for the existence of such
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
given a weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
.

The main result that we prove in this section, and use subsequently, is the following:

Theorem 5.1

(Uniqueness of filtered objects
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
)
.

Let
(\beta,{\bf a})
be a weighted braid word. Suppose that there exists a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}\colon N(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\to\mathcal{D}
whose geometric realization is rigid and lies in
\mathcal{M}
. Then any other rigid
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object in
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
.

The necessary definitions for all the notions featured in
Theorem

5.1
, including that of a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object, the subwords
\beta_{i},\beta_{j}
, the degree 1 crossings, and the subcategory
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
, will be provided in
Section

5.2
and
Section

5.3
below.
Theorem

5.1
is proven in
Section

5.3
.

5.1
Bounds on
\operatorname{Ext}
-groups between braid
2
-spherical objects
S_{\beta}

This subsection proves
Lemma

5.4
and
Lemma

5.6
, establishing the vanishing of certain Ext-groups between certain objects of
\mathcal{D}
.

First, the silting object
\Pi_{2}(A_{n})\in\mathcal{D}
determines a
t
-structure, cf.
Lemma

A.19
, which we refer to as the standard
t
-structure. The objects
S_{\beta}
typically do not belong to its associated heart
\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
. For instance, if
\beta=s_{1}^{k}
then
S_{s_{1}^{k}}=S_{1}[k-1]\not\in\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}
for any
k\in[2,\infty)
. That said, it is well known that

\beta\mbox{ reduced }\Longrightarrow S_{\beta}\in\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}.

(32)

Remark 5.2
.

(1)

Suppose that
\beta=s_{i_{l}}\dots s_{i_{0}}
is reduced. Since the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit})
is freely generated by the
n
simples
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
, it canonically identifies with the root lattice in type
A_{n}
. Then the module
S_{\beta}\in\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}
projects to the positive root
s_{i_{l}}\dots s_{i_{1}}(\alpha_{i_{0}})
in
K_{0}(\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit})
.

(2)

By
[
HW22
, Lemma 3.3]
, if an object lies in the homologically non-positive aisle of the standard
t
-structure of
\mathcal{D}
, then so do its images under the action of the negative spherical twists. Therefore the spherical object
S_{\beta}\in\mathcal{D}
lies in the homologically negative aisle of the
t
-structure of
\mathcal{D}
.∎

Remark 5.3
.

For a reduced braid word
\beta
, the repeated (positive) spherical twists
\{S_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\beta}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}
are also known as the layer modules, and arise as the graded pieces of a filtration of the corresponding preprojective algebra, see
[
AIRT12
, Prop. 2.2]
and
[
Can25
, Rem. 4.4]
. We also note that negative spherical twists correspond to positive spherical twists in the opposite category, and that since
\mathcal{D}\simeq\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{op}}
, the usage of negative or positive spherical twists can be considered a convention.

Given two positive braid words
\alpha,\beta
, let us denote by
\alpha<\beta
if
\alpha
is a strict suffix of
\beta
, i.e. if there exist
i_{0},\dots,i_{m}
such that
\beta=s_{i_{0}}s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{i_{m}}\alpha
and
\alpha\neq\beta
. Note that for reduced permutation braids, the corresponding partial order is the Bruhat order. The following vanishing result for the morphism objects (i.e. derived Homs) between such two
2
-spherical objects will be crucial.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\alpha,\beta
be two braid words such that
\alpha<\beta
. Then:

(i)

The morphism object
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\alpha}[-1],S_{\beta})\in\mathcal{D}(k)
is connective, i.e.

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\alpha},S_{\beta})\simeq 0,\quad i\in[2,\infty)\,.

(ii)

The morphism object
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\beta}[-1],S_{\alpha})\in\mathcal{D}(k)
is coconnective, i.e.

\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}}^{i}(S_{\beta},S_{\alpha})\simeq 0,\quad i\in(-\infty,0]\,.

Proof.

For Part (1), after possibly acting with a self-equivalence of
\mathcal{D}
, we can and do assume that
\alpha=s_{i_{l}}
and
\beta=s_{i_{0}}s_{i_{1}}\dots s_{i_{l}}
. In the case that
\beta
is reduced, the statement follows from Statement (
32
). Indeed, there are no extensions higher than degree
2
between the spherical objects in the standard heart
\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}
and a simple
S_{i_{l}}
. Thus
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i_{l}},S_{\beta})\simeq 0
for
i\in[2,\infty)
since
S_{\beta}\in\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}
in this reduced case. Let us now prove the general non-reduced case by an induction on the length
l
.

Suppose
\beta
is not reduced, and thus we can choose an expression for
\beta
such that
s_{i_{k}}=s_{i_{k+1}}
for some index
k\in[0,l-1]
. Let us denote

\beta^{k}\coloneqq s_{i_{0}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}\,,\quad\beta^{k+1}\coloneqq s_{i_{0}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}s_{i_{k+1}}\,,\quad\beta^{\dagger}\coloneqq s_{i_{k+2}}\cdots s_{i_{l}}\,,

so that
\beta=\beta^{k+1}\beta^{\dagger}
. The induction hypothesis implies that the statement holds for
\beta^{k}\beta^{\dagger}
, which has length strictly less than
\beta
. That is, the induction hypothesis implies that
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\alpha}[-1],S_{\beta^{k}\beta^{\dagger}})
is connective, since
\alpha=s_{i_{l}}
is still a suffix for
\beta^{k}\beta^{\dagger}
. The goal is to show that
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i_{l}}[-1],S_{\beta})
is connective.

For the induction step, we use that
S_{\beta^{k+1}}\simeq T_{S_{i_{k+1}}}^{-1}(S_{\beta^{k}})
and the corresponding cofiber sequence

S_{\beta^{k+1}}\longrightarrow S_{\beta^{k}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\beta^{k}},S_{i_{k+1}})^{*}\otimes S_{i_{k+1}}

(33)

induced by the negative twist of
S_{\beta^{k}}
along
S_{i_{k+1}}
. By mapping from

S^{\beta_{\dagger}}[-2]\coloneqq T_{S_{i_{k+2}}}\cdots T_{S_{i_{l-1}}}(S_{i_{l}})[-2]\simeq T_{S_{i_{k+2}}}\cdots T_{S_{i_{l-1}}}T_{S_{i_{l}}}(S_{i_{l}})[-1]

and considering
\operatorname{Ext}
-groups, the sequence (
33
) gives rise to a long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\operatorname{Ext}^{j-1}(S^{\beta_{\dagger}}[-2],\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\beta^{k}},S_{i_{k+1}})^{*}\otimes S_{i_{k+1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{j}(S^{\beta_{\dagger}}[-2],S_{\beta^{k+1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}^{j}(S^{\beta_{\dagger}}[-2],S_{\beta^{k}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}(S^{\beta_{\dagger}}[-2],\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\beta^{k}},S_{i_{k+1}})^{*}\otimes S_{i_{k+1}})}}

for
j\in(0,\infty)
. Since we have the equivalences

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S^{\beta_{\dagger}}[-2],S_{\beta^{k+1}})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i_{l}}[-1],S_{\beta})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S^{\beta_{\dagger}}[-2],S_{\beta^{k}})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i_{l}}[-1],S_{\beta^{k}\beta^{\dagger}})\,,

and
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i_{l}}[-1],S_{\beta^{k}\beta^{\dagger}})
is connective by the inductive hypothesis, it suffices to show that the two terms highlighted in purple vanish. We claim this is the case, and prove it as follows. On the one hand, since
S_{i_{k}}\simeq S_{i_{k+1}}
, as
i_{k}=i_{k+1}
, we obtain that

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\beta^{k}},S_{i_{k+1}})^{*}\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i_{k+1}},S_{\beta^{k}})[2]\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i_{k}}[-1],S_{\beta^{k}})[1]

must be concentrated in homological degrees in
[1,\infty)
, by applying the induction hypothesis to
\beta^{k}
. On the other hand, we also have that

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S^{\beta_{\dagger}}[-2],S_{i_{k+1}})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i_{l}}[-1],S_{s_{i_{k+1}}\beta^{\dagger}})

is connective, by the inductive hypothesis applied to
s_{i_{k+1}}\beta^{\dagger}
. Therefore, the two purple outer entries of the above long exact sequence vanish, as claimed. Therefore, the long exact sequence above and the inductive hypothesis imply that
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i_{l}}[-1],S_{\beta})
is connective, as required. This establishes Part (1). Part (2) follows from Part (1) because
\mathcal{D}
is a 2-Calabi–Yau category.
∎

Example 5.5
.

(1) Let us consider
\alpha=s_{1}
and
\beta=s_{i}s_{1}
, for some
i\in[1,n]
. Then

S_{s_{i}s_{1}}\simeq\begin{cases}S_{1}[1],&\mbox{for }i=1,\\
\operatorname{fib}(S_{2}\to S_{1}[1]),&\mbox{for }i=2,\\
S_{i},&\mbox{for }i\in[3,n],\end{cases}\mbox{ and thus }\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{1}[-1],S_{s_{i}s_{1}})\simeq\begin{cases}k\oplus k[2],&\mbox{for }i=1,\\
k[1],&\mbox{for }i=2,\\
0,&\mbox{for }i\in[3,n].\end{cases}

Therefore
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{1}[-1],S_{s_{i}s_{1}})
are indeed connective, in accordance with
Lemma

5.4
.(1).

(2) Let
\alpha=s_{1}
and
\beta=(s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{1}s_{2}s_{2})s_{1}
. Then
S_{\beta}\simeq S_{2}
and
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{1}[-1],S_{\beta})\simeq k
. More generally, there exist braid words
\beta
of arbitrarily large length such that
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{1}[-1],S_{\beta})\simeq k
.∎

5.1.1
2
-spherical semi-twists preserve rigidity

Let us now show that semi-twists in
\mathcal{D}
preserve rigidity. Semi-twists are defined in
Definition

A.10
. For instance, the negative inverse semi-twist of
X
by
Y
is

T^{-,\leq 0}_{Y}(X)\coloneqq\operatorname{fib}(X\to\tau_{\leq 0}(\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)^{*})\otimes Y)\,,

(34)

where
\tau_{\leq 0}(\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)^{*})\subset\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)^{*}
denotes the maximal summand concentrated in homologically negative degrees. An important homological property of semi-twists is as follows:

Lemma 5.6

(semi-twists preserve rigidity)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
k
-linear
2
-Calabi--Yau
7
7

7

Either weak left or triangulated
2
-Calabi–Yau would be sufficient.
stable
\infty
-category. Let
S\in\mathcal{D}
be a
2
-spherical object. Consider further two compact objects
A,B\in\mathcal{D}
with
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(A,B)\simeq 0
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(A),T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(B))

\displaystyle\simeq 0

(35)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(T_{S}^{+,\geq 0}(A),T_{S}^{+,\geq 0}(B))

\displaystyle\simeq 0

(36)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(T_{S}^{-,>0}(A),T_{S}^{-,>0}(B))

\displaystyle\simeq 0

(37)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(T_{S}^{+,<0}(A),T_{S}^{+,<0}(B))

\displaystyle\simeq 0\,.

(38)

In particular, the functors
T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(\mathchar 45\relax),T_{S}^{+,\geq 0}(\mathchar 45\relax),T_{S}^{-,>0}(\mathchar 45\relax),T_{S}^{+,<0}(\mathchar 45\relax)
preserve rigid objects.

Proof.

The equivalence (
36
) follows from the equivalence (
35
) in
\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{op}}
and, similarly, (
38
) follows from (
37
). It thus suffices to prove (
35
) and (
37
).

Let us start to prove the equivalence (
35
). To ease notation, we denote morphism objects in
\mathcal{D}
by
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
instead of
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
. By the definition of
T^{-,\leq 0}
in (
34
), the morphism object
\operatorname{Mor}(T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(A),T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(B))
is obtained as the totalization of the following square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(A,\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}\otimes S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)^{*}\otimes S,\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}\otimes S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)^{*}\otimes S,B)}}

The grading in the totalization is chosen, so that
\operatorname{Mor}(T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(A),T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(B))
is obtained as the cofiber in the vertical direction and fiber in the horizontal direction. The above square is equivalent to the following square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k\oplus k[-2])\otimes\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\operatorname{Mor}(S,B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(39)

where we have used that
S\in\mathcal{D}
is a 2-spherical object, and thus
\operatorname{Mor}(S,S)\simeq k\oplus k[-2]
, for the bottom left entry of (
39
). The
2
-Calabi–Yau structure of
\mathcal{D}
provides an equivalence
\varepsilon:\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]\to\operatorname{Mor}(S,B)
, which we can use to change the lower right corner
\operatorname{Mor}(S,B)
of (
39
) to
\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]
. In addition, the functoriality of the Calabi–Yau duality implies that the composition of the inclusion

\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]\hookrightarrow\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]

with the morphism

\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\varepsilon)}}{{\longrightarrow}}(k\oplus k[-2])\otimes\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}

restricts to the second summand to an equivalence. Now, by moving the upper right term of (
39
) to the lower left, the totalization of (
39
) is equivalent to the totalization of the following square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}(A,B)\oplus{(k\oplus k[-2])\otimes\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]}}

(40)

In order to compute the totalization of (
40
) we consider the following two trivial squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

(41)

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

(42)

The totalizations of (
41
) and (
42
) vanish, as the identity map is an isomorphism. Since every object in
\mathcal{D}(k)
is equivalent to its homology, we can decompose
\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\simeq\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\oplus\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)
. Therefore we have a morphism of squares from (
41
) to (
40
). The cofiber of such a morphism is the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{<0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(A,B)\oplus\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]}}

(43)

Since the totalization of (
41
) vanishes, the totalization of (
43
) is equivalent to the totalization of (
40
). Similarly, there is a morphism from (
43
) to (
42
), whose fiber is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{<0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{>0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]}}

(44)

As before, the totalization of (
43
) is equivalent to the totalization of (
44
), thus we focus on the latter. Passing to the totalization of (
44
), the term
\tau_{<0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}
only contributes in degrees
i\leq-2
, and t
\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{>0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]
only contributes in degrees
i\geq 0
. Hence,
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(A),T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(B))
vanishes, as required. This establishes the vanishing (
35
).

For the equivalence (
37
), the argument is similar to the above. Namely, the morphism object
\operatorname{Mor}(T_{S}^{-,>0}(A),T_{S}^{-,>0}(B))
is equivalent to the totalization of the following square in
\mathcal{D}(k)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{>0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}(A,B)\oplus{(k\oplus k[-2])\otimes\tau_{<0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{>0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{<0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]}}

Its totalization is in turn equivalent to the totalization of the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{>0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau_{<0}\operatorname{Mor}(A,S)\otimes\tau_{\leq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(B,S)^{*}[-2]}}

which vanishes in degree
-1
, thus establishing the equivalence (
37
). Finally, the fact that each of the four functors
T_{S}^{-,\leq 0}(\mathchar 45\relax),T_{S}^{+,\geq 0}(\mathchar 45\relax),T_{S}^{-,>0}(\mathchar 45\relax),T_{S}^{+,<0}(\mathchar 45\relax)
preserves rigid objects follows from the equivalences in the statement by taking
A=B
.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

A result in line with
Lemma

5.6
, in the case of objects in the module
1
-category of the preprojective algebra, appears in
[
GLS08
, Prop. 5.2]
. A further similar result appears in
[
PYK23
, Lem. 4.5]
.∎

5.2
A geometric model for
\mathcal{D}

In this subsection we explain how to describe certain objects in
\mathcal{D}
and the morphisms between them in terms of immersed paths in the plane. Such objects and their geometric description will be used in
Section

5.3
to construct the filtered objects
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
. In brief, this subsection develops the classical theory of matching cycles in the context of a perverse schober whose
\infty
-category of global sections is equivalent to
\mathcal{D}
.

Figure 17:
The graph
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
in the case of
n=3
, with its four vertices
\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2},\mathsf{v}_{3},\mathsf{v}_{4}
(gray) and its three edges
\mathsf{e}_{1},\mathsf{e}_{2},\mathsf{e}_{3}
(black). There are three
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
, depicted in yellow, purple and gray respectively.

Let
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
be the linear ribbon graph with
n+1
vertices, i.e. an
A_{n+1}
-Dynkin diagram. See
Figure

17
for the case
n=3
. Consider the spherical functor
\phi\colon\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k[t^{\pm 1}])
, with
k[t^{\pm 1}]
the ungraded
k
-algebra of Laurent polynomials, determined by mapping
k
to the simple representation with
k
as its underlying vector space and
t
acting by
1
. Given such a functor
\phi
we define, using
2.10
, the following
k
-linear
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
-parametrized perverse schober:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}\coloneqq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varrho_{2},\varrho_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varrho_{2},\varrho_{1})}

(45)

It follows from
[
Chr21
, Thm. 4.19]
that there exists an equivalence of
k
-linear
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{D}\simeq R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
, i.e.
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to the category of global sections of the schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
in (
45
).

Remark 5.8
.

Specifically, this partial geometric model that we will describe captures, in the context of perverse schobers, aspects of
[
Sei08
, Section (16g)]
, and some of the algebraic structures associated to the symplectic geometry of Lagrangian 2-spheres. See also
[
KS02
, Sections 3&4]
,
[
Chr21
, Section 5]
and
[
CHQ23
, Section 4]
. ∎

5.2.1
Matching objects in
\mathcal{D}

Let
\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
the open disk with center
(\frac{n+3}{2},0)
and diameter
n+2
. Consider the points
\mathsf{v}_{i}=(i,0)\in\mathbb{D}
,
i\in[1,n+1]
, and the punctured disk
\tilde{\mathbb{D}}\coloneqq\mathbb{D}\backslash\{\mathsf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{n+1}\}
. By definition, a grading structure
\nu
on
\mathbb{D}
is a homotopy class of sections of the projectivised tangent bundle
\mathbb{P}(T\tilde{\mathbb{D}})
; a section of
\mathbb{P}(T\tilde{\mathbb{D}})
is also known as a line field. We equip the disk
\mathbb{D}
above with the unique grading structure satisfying that the winding number around each puncture is given by
2
. The corresponding line field
\nu
can then be chosen to be constant, pointing everywhere in the horizontal direction. In particular,
\nu
extends to all of
\mathbb{D}
.

Let us fix an embedding of the geometric realization of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
into
\mathbb{D}
such that the
i
-th vertex
\mathsf{v}_{i}
of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
is mapped to
\mathsf{v}_{i}\in\mathbb{D}
. To ease notation, we do not distinguish the vertices of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
from their images in
\mathbb{D}
and equally refer to them as
\mathsf{v}_{i}
. Note that in this model we are depicting
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
and the schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
horizontally, cf.
Figure

17
and
Equation

45
: this is done intentionally in order to contrast with
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, which we always draw vertically, cf.
Section

3.1
.

Definition 5.9
.

By definition, an immersed curve
\gamma\colon[-1,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}
is a
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path if

•

The image
\gamma((-1,1))
is disjoint from the vertices of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
, and its endpoints
\gamma(-1)
and
\gamma(1)
must be vertices of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
.

•

The complement
\mathbb{D}\setminus\gamma(-1,1)
does not contain any once-punctured disks without marked points on the boundary, and whose boundary entirely consists of points in the image of
\gamma
.
8
8

8

In particular, if
\gamma
wraps around a vertex, it does so at most by one full turn and then necessarily returns from the same direction that it approached the vertex.

•

\gamma
is not homotopic relative the vertices of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
to a constant curve.∎

The
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths in
Definition

5.9
will be always considered up to homotopy relative to the vertices of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
.
Figure

17
depicts three embedded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matchings paths in the case
n=3
.

Definition 5.10
.

A grading of a
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path
\gamma\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{D}
consists of a homotopy class of paths in
\Gamma([-1,1],\gamma^{*}\mathbb{P}(T\tilde{\mathbb{D}}))
from the line field
\gamma^{*}\nu
to the line field of tangents
\dot{\gamma}
.∎

Remark 5.11
.

Gradings of a
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path
\gamma
, as in
Definition

5.10
, form a
\mathbb{Z}
-torsor. We can and typically do choose the line field
\nu
that is parallel to the edges of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
. Note that choosing
\gamma(0)
to lie on an edge
\mathsf{e}
and
\dot{\gamma}(0)
to be parallel to
\mathsf{e}
determines a grading, i.e. a base point for the
\mathbb{Z}
-torsor.∎

Figure 18:
The six different types of local pieces for a
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path, as defined in
Definition

5.9
.

Each
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path from
Definition

5.9
can be uniquely written as the composite of six different homotopy classes of segments, up to reversal of orientation and smooth isotopy relative to its endpoints, with each segment lying in a contractible neighborhood of a vertex
\mathsf{v}_{i}\in{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
. These six types of segments are depicted in
Figure

18
, paired in three different columns. The segments depicted in the
k
th column of
Figure

18
are said to be segments of the
k
th type. For instance, the three
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matchings paths depicted in
Figure

17
only use segments of the first and second type.

In our context, the purpose of the
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths from
Definition

5.9
is that we can associate a global section
X_{\gamma}
of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
, i.e. an object
X_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}
, to each such graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path
\gamma
. This can be seen as an instance of the constructions given in
[
Chr21
, Constr. 5.13]
and
[
CHQ23
, Section 4.3]
.
9
9

9

Note that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
admits a canonical arc system kit in the sense of
[
CHQ23
]
.
The construction can be described as follows.

Construction 5.12

(Matching objects)
.

Let us label the vertices of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
by
\mathsf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{n+1}
left-to-right and the incident edges by
\mathsf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{e}_{n}
, also left-to-right. Fix a
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path
\gamma
: the object
X_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}
will be obtained by gluing local sections associated to the different types of segments for
\gamma
, as in
Figure

18
.

(
5.12
.1).
First, let us choose the grading
\nu
for
\gamma
as in
Remark

5.11
: such grading of
\gamma
determines an integer
d
. From this, we associate a degree
d(\delta)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
to every type of segment
\delta
for
\gamma
, as follows. Denote the segments of
\gamma
by
\delta_{-a},\dots,\delta_{0},\dots,\delta_{b}
, with
a,b>0
, where
\delta_{0}
the segment for the starting point of
\gamma
and such that they are ordered compatibly with the way they are horizontally concatenated to form
\gamma
. In this case, we set
d(\delta_{0})\coloneqq d
and for the next segment in
\delta_{0},\delta_{-1},\dots,\delta_{-a}
(resp.
\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{b}
), we recursively increase (resp. decrease) the degree by
1
if the previous segment was of the third type going clockwise (resp. counterclockwise), and otherwise the degree remains the same.

(
5.12
.2).
Second, we associate to each type of a segment in
Figure

18
a local section. Specifically, with
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
as above, we denote by
p\colon\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{D})\to\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}})
the Grothendieck construction of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
. Objects in
\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
can be described as pairs
(\mathsf{v};O_{\mathsf{v}})\in\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
, where
\mathsf{v}\in\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}})
and
O_{\mathsf{v}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{v})
are objects. By (
45
) and
Section

2.2.2
we have

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{v}_{i})\simeq\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{\phi}\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k[t^{\pm 1}])&\mbox{ if }i\in[2,n],\\
\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)\mbox{ if }i\in\{1,n+1\}.\end{cases}

Let us consider the following partial sections
M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}^{\dagger}}:\Delta^{0}=\{\mathsf{v}_{i}\}\longrightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
of
p
, where the superscript
\dagger
denotes
\dagger\in\{\leftarrow,\rightarrow,\circlearrowleft,\circlearrowright,\supset,\subset\}
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}^{\circlearrowleft}}({\mathsf{v}_{i}})\coloneqq(0,\phi(k),0),\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}^{\circlearrowright}}({\mathsf{v}_{i}})\coloneqq(k\oplus k[-1],\phi(k),\phi\phi^{R}\phi(k)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{counit}}\phi(k))\,,

\displaystyle M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}^{\leftarrow}}({\mathsf{v}_{i}})\coloneqq\begin{cases}k&\mbox{ if }i=1\\
(k;\phi(k),\operatorname{id}_{\phi(k)})&\mbox{ else}\end{cases},\quad M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}^{\rightarrow}}({\mathsf{v}_{i}})\coloneqq\begin{cases}k&\mbox{ if }i=n+1\\
(k[-1];0,0)&\mbox{ else}\end{cases}

\displaystyle M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}^{\supset}}(\mathsf{v}_{i})\coloneqq\begin{cases}k\oplus k[1]&i=1\\
(k\oplus k[1],0,0)&\mbox{ else}\end{cases},\qquad M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}}^{\subset}(\mathsf{v}_{i})\coloneqq\begin{cases}k\oplus k[1]&i=n+1\\
(k\oplus k[1],\phi(k)\oplus\phi(k)[1],\eta)&\mbox{ else}\,,\end{cases}

where the morphism
\eta
for
M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}}^{\subset}(\mathsf{v}_{i})
is

\eta\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{id}_{\phi(k)}&0\\
\alpha&\operatorname{id}_{\phi(k)[1]}\end{pmatrix}

with
\alpha\colon\phi(k)\to\phi(k)[1]
a non-zero morphism. In these descriptions above, for
M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}}^{\supset}(\mathsf{v}_{i})
and
M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}}^{\subset}(\mathsf{v}_{i})
we assume the segment is oriented clockwise. In these cases, if
\delta
is a segment of the third type with the other orientation, then we set
M_{\delta}\coloneqq M_{\delta^{\operatorname{rev}}}[-1]
with
\delta^{\operatorname{rev}}
the segment with the reversed orientation.

Let us extend these partial sections
M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}^{\dagger}}:\Delta^{0}\to\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
above to local lax sections of
\mbox{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}})
via
p
-relative left Kan extensions, i.e. fitting the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}^{\dagger}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mbox{Lan}^{p}_{\iota}(M_{\delta_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}^{\dagger}})}

This leads to the following lax sections associated to segments:

Definition 5.13
.

Consider an ungraded segment
\delta
of type
\dagger
near the vertex
\mathsf{v}_{i}\in{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
. By definition, the local section
M_{\delta}\in\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
associated to
\delta
is the
p
-relative Kan extension of
M_{\delta^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}}
.∎

(
5.12
.3).
Finally, the object
X_{\gamma}
is obtained by gluing the lax sections of its segments constructed in
Definition

5.13
. Specifically, the gluing of these local sections amounts to repeated pushouts in the
\infty
-category of lax sections
\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
, as follows. Let
M_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}\in\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
be the lax section determined by

M_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}(\mathsf{e}_{i})=\phi(k)\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{e}_{i}),\qquad M_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}(x)\simeq 0\mbox{ for all }x\not=\mathsf{e}_{i},\quad x\in\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}).

Let
\mathsf{e}_{j_{i}}
be the edge where the segment
\delta_{i}
of
\gamma
starts, and consider the associated conical inclusion of lax sections
M_{\mathsf{e}_{j_{i}}}[d(\delta_{i})]\hookrightarrow M_{\delta_{i}}[d(\delta_{i})]
. By construction, we always glue in a segment
\delta_{i}
of
\gamma
at a time by passing to the pushout along the morphism
M_{\mathsf{e}_{j_{i}}}[d(\delta_{i})]\hookrightarrow M_{\delta_{i}}[d(\delta_{i})]
. Note that, if
\delta_{i}
is a segment of the first or second type, then this inclusion is determined by evaluating to an equivalence at
\mathsf{e}_{j_{i}}
. The result of this gluing process is an object
X_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
lying in the full subcategory
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})\subset\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
of global sections, which we summarize as follows:

Definition 5.14

(Matching objects)
.

Let
\gamma
be a graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path. By definition, the object
X_{\gamma}\in R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}})
is the result of gluing the local lax sections
M_{\delta}
of the segments
\delta
associated to
\gamma
along the conical inclusions
M_{\mathsf{e}_{\delta}}[d(\delta)]\hookrightarrow M_{\delta}[d(\delta)]
of lax sections, where
\mathsf{e}_{\delta}
is the edge corresponding to
\delta
, as in (
5.12
.3) above.∎

End of Construction
5.12
.

We will use the category additively spanned by the matching objects from
Definition

5.14
:

Definition 5.15
.

By definition, the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\operatorname{Add}(\{X_{\gamma}\})\subset\mathcal{D}
is the additive hull of objects
X_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}
associated with graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths. By definition, an object
X\in\mathcal{D}
is said to be matching if
X\in\mathcal{M}
.∎

Example 5.16

(Simples
S_{i}
are matching)
.

Each of the 2-spherical simples
S_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
is matching, i.e.
S_{i}\in\mathcal{M}
. Specifically,
S_{i}
arises as the global section
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
associated with the
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path
\ell_{i}
given by a (straight) line
\mathsf{e}_{i}
connecting the
i
th and
(i+1)
-the vertices of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
. In this case,
\gamma
consists of two segments
\delta_{1}\coloneqq\delta^{\leftarrow}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}
and
\delta_{2}\coloneqq\delta^{\rightarrow}_{\mathsf{v}_{i+1}}
of the first type, composed at the edge
\mathsf{e}_{i}
. Thus,
X_{\ell_{i}}
is the pushout of a diagram of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\mathsf{e}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\delta_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\delta_{1}}}}

(46)

where the local lax sections
M_{e},M_{\delta_{1}},M_{\delta_{2}}
are as in Construction
5.12
above. We can depict these lax sections as follows:
