It indeed follows that
X_{\ell_{i}}\simeq S_{i}
and thus
S_{i}\in\mathcal{M}
.∎

Remark 5.17
.

Let
\gamma
be a
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path without segments of the third type. Then there exists a canonical grading for
\gamma
such that
X_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}
lies in the standard heart
\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}\subset\mathcal{D}
.∎

Applying a spherical twist to a matching object
X_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{M}
along any of the simples
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
can be described in terms of
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths, as follows. For that, we consider the half-twists
f_{i}\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
, considered up to compactly supported isotopies of
\mathbb{D}
, cf.
[
Don11
, Figure 14.2]
,
[
KS02
, Section 3b]
or
[
Sei08
, Section (16d)]
. The mapping class of such
f_{i}\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
,
i\in[1,n]
, is represented by any compactly supported diffeomorphism that acts on the edge
\mathsf{e}_{i}\in{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
connecting the
i
th and
(i+1)
th vertices
\mathsf{v}_{i},\mathsf{v}_{i+1}\in{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
by a counter-clockwise half-rotation (thus exchanging
\mathsf{v}_{i}
and
\mathsf{v}_{i+1}
) and fixes the other vertices
\mathsf{v}_{j}
of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
for
j\in[1,n+1]
and
j\not\in\{i,i+1\}
. Given a graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path, the grading for the
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path
f_{i}\circ\gamma
is obtained by composition of
f_{i}^{*}(\dot{\gamma})\simeq(f_{i}\circ\gamma)^{*}(\nu)
with the canonical homotopy between line fields
f_{i}^{*}(\nu)\simeq\nu
. Spherical twist along the simples
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
are then described as follows:

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
\gamma
be a graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path,
X_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{D}
its matching object, and
f_{i}\in\mbox{Diff}_{c}(\mathbb{D})
the
i
th half-twist,
i\in[1,n]
. Then

T_{S_{i}}(X_{\gamma})\simeq X_{f_{i}\circ\gamma}.

Lemma

5.18
follows from the same argument used to prove
[
Chr21
, Thm. 7.5]
, see also
[
Sei08
, Remark 16.14]
.
Lemma

5.18
implies that the objects associated to embedded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths can be constructed out of the
2
-spherical objects
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
via iterated spherical twists. In addition, the association of embedded graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths to equivalence classes of global sections of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{D}}
is injective, see e.g. the proof of
[
CHQ23
, Cor. 5.1]
.

Example 5.19
.

Consider the three
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
in
Figure

17
, with
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
corresponding to the
A_{3}
-Dynkin diagram. By
Lemma

5.18
we can describe their associated matching objects as

X_{\gamma_{1}}\simeq T_{S_{1}}(S_{2}),\qquad X_{\gamma_{2}}\simeq T^{-1}_{S_{3}}T_{S_{2}}(S_{1}),\qquad X_{\gamma_{3}}\simeq T_{S_{3}}(S_{2}).

In this instance, we have graded the
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths via the canonical grading, cf.
Remark

5.11
, and since there are no segments of the third type the resulting matching objects are indeed in
\mathcal{D}^{\heartsuit}
, as per
Remark

5.17
.∎

Remark 5.20
.

There can be distinct
immersed
graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths which give rise to equivalent global sections.
For instance, let
\tau_{1}
be the immersed
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path starting at
\mathsf{v}_{i}
, encircling
\mathsf{v}_{i+1}
exactly once, and ending at
\mathsf{v}_{i}
again. Similarly, let
\tau_{2}
be the immersed
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path starting at
\mathsf{v}_{i+1}
, encircling
\mathsf{v}_{i}
exactly once, and ending at
\mathsf{v}_{i+1}
again. Then both
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
, when suitably graded, give rise to equivalent objects
X_{\tau_{1}}\simeq X_{\tau_{2}}
in
\mathcal{D}
, namely the extension
X_{\tau_{1}}\simeq\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to S_{i}[2])
. For the repeated fibers
\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}[2]\to\dots\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}[2(m-1)]\to S_{i}[2m])))
, there are similarly two corresponding
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths.∎

5.2.2
Morphisms between matching objects in
\mathcal{D}

Let us now provide a description of the morphism objects between the matching objects from
Section

5.2.1
in terms of counts of graded intersections between their corresponding
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths. We distinguish two types of intersections: endpoint intersections and crossings. Crossings are transverse intersections of two
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths away from their endpoints. Both types of intersections are considered as directed: the order of the curves at the intersection point is specified.

Index of intersection points
. First, we must associate an index
d(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
to each type of directed intersection
x
, as follows. For the case of crossings, let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
be two
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths and
c_{i}\coloneqq\dot{\gamma_{1}}\simeq(\gamma_{i})^{*}\nu
their canonical gradings,
i\in[1,2]
. Suppose that
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
intersect transversely at an interior point
p=\gamma_{1}(t_{1})=\gamma_{2}(t_{2})
, for some
t_{1},t_{2}\in(-1,1)
. Then we define the index
d(x)\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{P}(T_{p}{\bf S}))\simeq\mathbb{Z}
to be the class
c_{2}(t_{2})\cdot\kappa\cdot c_{1}(t_{1})^{-1}
, where
\kappa
is a path from
\dot{\gamma}_{1}(t_{1})
to
\dot{\gamma}_{2}(t_{2})
given by clockwise rotation in
T_{p}\mathbb{D}
by an angle less than
\pi
.
10
10

10

Note that we indeed have
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{P}(T_{p}{\bf S}))\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, and we choose the isomorphism to be determined by mapping
1
to a clockwise rotation.
In this case, the crossing is said to be in degree
d(x),d(x)+1
, since these are the degrees of the non-trivial extensions to which it will contribute.

For the case of endpoint intersections, let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
be two
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths with an endpoint intersection
x=\mathsf{v}_{i}
for some
i\in[1,n+1]
. We follow
[
IQZ20
, Eq. (2.5)]
to define the intersection index of
x
. For that, we choose a small circle around
\mathsf{v}_{i}
and let
\alpha\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{D}
be a curve going fully around the circle in the clockwise direction. Let
y_{1}
be the crossing from
\gamma_{1}
to
\alpha
, and
y_{2}
the crossing from
\gamma_{2}
to
\alpha
, so that
y_{1},y_{2}
are crossing intersections. The index of the intersection point
x
is then defined as
d(x)\coloneqq d(y_{1})-d(y_{2})
, where we have chosen an arbitrary grading for the circle
\alpha
.

Example 5.21
.

If
\gamma_{1}=\mathsf{e}_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}=\mathsf{e}_{2}
are the first two edges of the geometric realization of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
equipped with their canonical gradings, their endpoint intersection has index
1
.∎

Figure 19:
A summary of the key cases in
Proposition

5.22
. The first column depicts the type intersection from
\gamma_{1}
to
\gamma_{2}
, endpoint above and crossing below. The second column records its contributions to the morphisms between the matching objects
X_{\gamma_{1}}
and
X_{\gamma_{2}}
. The third depicts, when possible, a description of the corresponding extensions in terms of matching objects.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
be two graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths, our goal is to describe the morphism object
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(X_{\gamma_{1}},X_{\gamma_{2}})
in terms of the count of graded intersections of
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
. We will only need the case where at least one of
\gamma_{1}
or
\gamma_{2}
is embedded, and thus we assume that. The following result provides such a description of
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(X_{\gamma_{1}},X_{\gamma_{2}})
, where its two key cases are summarized in
Figure

19
:

Proposition 5.22
.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
be two graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths whose underlying curves are distinct and
X_{\gamma_{1}},X_{\gamma_{2}}\in\mathcal{D}
their associated matching objects. Suppose that either
\gamma_{1}
or
\gamma_{2}
is embedded and choose the order so that
\gamma_{1}
preceeds
\gamma_{2}
. Then there exists an equivalence in
\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(X_{\gamma_{1}},X_{\gamma_{2}})\simeq\bigoplus_{x\text{ end.\penalty\ int.}}k[-d(x)]\oplus\bigoplus_{x\text{ crossing}}k[-d(x)]\oplus k[-d(x)-1]\,.

(47)

Proof.

In the case of embedded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths, (
47
) is proved in different levels of generality and contexts in
[
KS02
,
IQZ20
,
Chr21
]
. Specifically, in the context of perverse schobers,
[
Chr21
, Thm. 6.4]
, implies (
47
) for graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths without segments of the third type. In order to reduce the general case to the case of
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths without segments of the third type, we proceed as follows.

Swapping
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
if necessary, we assume that
\gamma_{1}
is embedded. By acting with an autoequivalence of
\mathcal{D}
, we can and do assume that
X_{\gamma_{1}}\simeq S_{i}
for some
i\in[1,n]
. Let us choose a sequence of graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths
\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\gamma_{m}^{\prime}
without segments of the third type together with iterated fiber and cofiber sequences
Y_{i+1}\to X_{\gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}}\to X_{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}
such that
Y_{m}\simeq X_{\gamma_{2}}
. The number
m
is the number of segments of the third type of
\gamma_{2}
. We do choose this sequence such that the morphism
X_{\gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}}\to X_{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}
arises from an endpoint intersection away from the two endpoints of
\gamma_{1}
and geometrically
\gamma_{i}^{\prime},\gamma_{i+1}^{\prime},\gamma_{1}
do not form a triangle involving two crossings with
\gamma_{1}
and the endpoint intersection. Then the arising fiber and cofiber sequences, which read

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(X_{\gamma_{1}},X_{\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}[-1])\to\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(X_{\gamma_{1}},Y_{i+1})\to\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(X_{\gamma_{1}},X_{\gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}}),

all split. Furthermore the number of intersections of
\gamma_{1}
with
\gamma_{2}
is equal to the number of intersections of
\gamma_{1}
with
\gamma_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\gamma_{m}^{\prime}
. This reduces the general case to cases with no segments of the third type.
∎

Example 5.23
.

Let us continue with
Example

5.19
, cf.
Figure

17
. By
Example

5.16
,
S_{i}\simeq X_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}
, where the edge
\mathsf{e}_{i}
is understood as a straight
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path. By
Example

5.21
, the endpoint intersection index of
\mathsf{e}_{1}
and
\mathsf{e}_{2}
is 1. By
Proposition

5.22
,

\operatorname{Mor}(X_{\mathsf{e}_{1}},X_{\mathsf{e}_{2}})\simeq k[-1],

which indeed recovers the fact that
\mbox{Ext}^{1}(S_{1},S_{2})\simeq k
and
\mbox{Ext}^{i}(S_{1},S_{2})\simeq 0
if
i\neq 1
. Similarly, for
\gamma_{2}
as in
Figure

17
, the morphisms between the matching object
X_{\gamma_{2}}
and
S_{2}
are given by

\operatorname{Mor}(X_{\gamma_{2}},X_{\mathsf{e}_{2}})\simeq k\oplus k[-1],

since their only intersection point is a crossing
x
of degree
d(x)=0
, cf. third row of
Figure

20
.∎

Figure 20:
The intersections of
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths and their contribution to morphisms between the associated matching objects in the case of the canonical grading of
Remark

5.11
. For instance, by
Example

5.21
the first row here must coincide with the first row of
Figure

20
with the endpoint intersection
\mathsf{v}_{i}=x
indexed by
d(x)=1
.

Remark 5.24
.

(1) The resolution of an intersection can fail to be union of
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths because of the second assumption in
Definition

5.9
stating that
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths should not cut out any once-punctured discs. In this case, the
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths describing the fiber are obtained by applying the local move in
Figure

21
.

Figure 21:
The resolution in
Remark

5.24
, describing fibers as matching objects.

(2) For crossings with immersed
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths arising from self-extensions as in
Remark

5.20
, the fibers of both morphisms arising from the crossing can be matching (and be computed by choosing a different representative). However, if the fiber of a morphism between two matching objects arising from an intersection is not matching, it must arise from a crossings and lie in the larger of the two degrees of the crossing.∎

Finally, we will need to use that semi-twists preserve objects in the subcategory
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
. This is the content of the following:

Lemma 5.25

(semi-twists preserve
\mathcal{M}
)
.

Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
be graded
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths and
X_{\gamma},X_{\gamma^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{M}
their associated matching objects. Suppose that
\gamma^{\prime}
is embedded. Then both semi-twists
T_{X_{\gamma^{\prime}}}^{-,\leq 0}(X_{\gamma})
and
T_{X_{\gamma^{\prime}}}^{+,\geq 0}(X_{\gamma})
are matching, i.e. they are objects in
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

By definition,
T_{X_{\gamma^{\prime}}}^{+,\geq 0}(X_{\gamma})=\operatorname{cof}(\tau_{\geq 0}(\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(X_{\gamma^{\prime}},X_{\gamma}))\otimes X_{\gamma^{\prime}}\to X_{\gamma})
. By
Proposition

5.22
, the dimension of the homology of the morphism object
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(X_{\gamma^{\prime}},X_{\gamma})
counts endpoint intersections and crossings of
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
. If an endpoint intersection
x\in\gamma\cap\gamma^{\prime}
gives rise to a negative extension, then the passage to the cofiber of the corresponding morphism
X_{\gamma^{\prime}}[i]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{\to}}X_{\gamma}
, with
i\geq 0
, has the effect of resolving that endpoint intersection of
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
, cf. first row of
Figure

19
.

If the intersection
x\in\gamma\cap\gamma^{\prime}
is a crossing contributing negative extensions, there are two options. First, if both of the corresponding extensions are negative, then the passage to the cofiber
X_{\gamma^{\prime}}[i]\oplus X_{\gamma^{\prime}}[i+1]\to X_{\gamma}
has the effect of applying a Dehn-twist around
\gamma^{\prime}
to
\gamma
at the crossing, for
i\in[0,\infty)
. Second, if only one of the corresponding extensions is negative, then the cofiber of the morphism
X_{\gamma}\to X_{\gamma^{\prime}}
is given by the direct sum of the two
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths arising from resolving the crossing. See the second row of
Figure

19
for a depiction of these cases. Therefore, for either types of intersections, the passage to the cofiber defining the semi-twist remains in
\mathcal{M}
, which proves the statement for
T_{X_{\gamma^{\prime}}}^{+,\geq 0}(X_{\gamma})
. The proof that
T_{X_{\gamma^{\prime}}}^{-,\leq 0}(X_{\gamma})\in\mathcal{M}
is analogous.
∎

5.3
The filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
associated with a weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})

The object of this subsection is to prove
Theorem

5.1
. Let us formally define the notions featured in its statement, including that of a weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
and a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration.

Definition 5.26

(Weighted braid words)
.

Let
\beta=\sigma_{i_{0}}\dots\sigma_{i_{l}}
be a positive braid word of length
l+1
. By definition, a weight
{\bf a}=(a_{0},\dots,a_{l})
for
\beta
consists of a sequence of non-negative integers
a_{i}\geq 0
. A weighted braid word is a pair
(\beta,{\bf a})
consisting of a positive braid word
\beta
and a weight for
\beta
.∎

In the case of permutations,
Definition

5.26
appeared in
[
CGG+25a
, Definition 6.13]
in the form of weighted expressions and their associated Lusztig data. Recall from
[
Lur17
, Definition 1.2.2.9]
that a
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-filtered object in
\mathcal{D}
is a functor
N(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\to\mathcal{D}
, where
N(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
is the nerve of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, with
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
being understood as a poset category.
Given a positive braid word
\beta=\sigma_{i_{0}}\dots\sigma_{i_{l}}
we denote

\beta_{j}\coloneqq\sigma_{i_{j}}\sigma_{i_{j+1}}\dots\sigma_{i_{l}},\quad j\in[0,l]

(48)

Note that
\beta_{0}=\beta
and
\beta_{l}=\sigma_{i_{l}}
.

Definition 5.27

(
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered objects)
.

Let
(\beta,{\bf a})
be a weighted braid word of length
l+1
and
X\in\mathcal{D}
an object. By definition, a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration of
X
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-filtered object
X(\mathchar 45\relax)\colon N(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\to\mathcal{D}
such that:

1.

the
i
th graded piece
\operatorname{gr}_{i}(X(\mathchar 45\relax))\coloneqq\operatorname{cof}(X(i-1)\to X(i))
is

\operatorname{gr}_{i}(X(\mathchar 45\relax))\simeq\begin{cases}S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus a_{i}}&\mbox{ if }i\in[0,l],\\
0&\mbox{ if }i\in[l+1,\infty).\end{cases}

(49)

2.

As objects in
\mathcal{D}
, there exists an equivalence
\operatorname{colim}X(\mathchar 45\relax)\simeq X
.

By definition, a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object
X\in\mathcal{D}
is an object
X\in\mathcal{D}
equipped with a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration.∎

Remark 5.28
.

Given a
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-filtered object
X(\mathchar 45\relax)
in
\mathcal{D}
, the colimit
\operatorname{colim}X(\mathchar 45\relax)
is also called the geometric realization of
X(\mathchar 45\relax)
, as it corresponds to the (simplicial) geometric realization under the Lurie–Dold–Kan correspondence, cf.
[
Lur17
, Rem. 1.2.4.2]
. In the setting of
Definition

5.27
,
\operatorname{colim}X(\mathchar 45\relax)
is equivalent to
X(l)
.∎

In general, there might be multiple
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered objects in
\mathcal{D}
. To obtain uniqueness, as in
Theorem

5.1
, we focus on rigid objects. Recall that an object
X\in\mathcal{D}
is called rigid if
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(X,X)\simeq 0
.

Example 5.29
.

Let
\beta
be a braid word of length
l+1
and
{\bf a}=(0,\dots,1,\dots,0)
the weight with a unique non-zero entry
1
in position
i\in[0,l]
. Then there exists a unique
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object in
\mathcal{D}
up to equivalence: it is given by
S_{\beta_{i}}
equipped with the filtration

0\to\dots\to 0\to S_{\beta_{i}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}}S_{\beta_{i}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}}S_{\beta_{i}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}}\dots\,,

where the first appearance of
S_{\beta_{i}}
is at the
i
th entry.∎

Given an object
X
with a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration, we can recover the weight from
X
and
\beta
:

Lemma 5.30
.

Let
\beta
be a positive braid word. For any object
X\in\mathcal{D}
there exists at most one weight
{\bf a}
for
\beta
, such that
X
admits a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration.

Proof.

Suppose that
X
admits a
(\beta,{\bf a})
and a
(\beta,{\bf b})
-filtration with
{\bf a}\not={\bf b}
. Let
l+1
be the length of
\beta
. There exists a maximal
0\leq i\leq l
such that
b_{i}\not=a_{i}
. Switching
{\bf a},{\bf b}
if necessary, we may assume that
b_{i}>a_{i}
. Passing to repeated fibers of morphisms
X\to S_{\beta_{j}}^{\oplus a_{j}}
,
j\geq i
, we may assume that
a_{j}=b_{j}=0
for
j>i
and
a_{i}=0
but
b_{i}>0
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\ast}
be the coherent chain complex corresponding to the
(\beta,{\bf b})
-filtration of
X
. We choose any non-zero morphism
S_{\beta_{i}}[-2]\to S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus b_{i}}
and extend it using
Lemma

5.4
to a morphism between coherent chain complexes
(S_{\beta_{i}}[-2-i])_{\ast}\to\mathcal{C}_{\ast}
, where
(S_{\beta_{i}}[-2-i])_{\ast}
is considered in degree
i
with value
S_{\beta_{i}}[-2-i]
. Passing to the cofiber totalization defines a non-zero morphism
S_{\beta_{i}}[-2]\to X
. Using that
X
admits a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration with
a_{j}=0
for
j\geq i
and
Lemma

5.4
, we find that
\operatorname{Ext}^{2}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{\beta_{i}},X)\simeq 0
, which is a contradiction. We must thus have
{\bf a}={\bf b}
.
∎

5.3.1
Preparatory lemmas

We use the following two lemmas,
Lemma

5.31
and
Lemma

5.32
, in the proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

Lemma 5.31
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{D}
be a
2
-spherical object and
A\in\mathcal{D}
be such that
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{D}}(S,A)\simeq 0
for all
i\in[2,\infty)
. Then, for any non-zero morphism
f:S[-1]\to A
,

\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(\operatorname{cof}(f),\operatorname{cof}(f))\geq\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(A,A)\,.

In particular, if
A
is not rigid, then the cofiber
\operatorname{cof}(f)
is not rigid.

Proof.

Let us denote
A^{\prime}\coloneqq\operatorname{cof}(f)
. Since the morphism object functor is exact in both entries, the morphism object
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(A^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is equivalent to the horizontal cofiber and vertical fiber of the following square in
\mathcal{D}(k)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(A,S[-1])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(A,A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underbrace{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S[-1],S[-1])}_{\simeq k\oplus k[-2]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}(S[-1],A)}}

where
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S[-1],S[-1])\simeq k\oplus k[-2]
follows from the assumption that
S
is 2-spherical.
By hypothesis, the bottom right entry
\operatorname{Mor}(S[-1],A)
is connective, and by the
2
-Calabi–Yau property on
\mathcal{D}
the top left entry
\operatorname{Mor}(A,S[-1])
is concentrated in homological degrees
i\in(-\infty,-2]
. It thus follows that the above square contains a direct summand in the stable
\infty
-category of squares in
\mathcal{D}(k)
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(A,A)[-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

This implies that there is a direct summand
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{D}(A,A)\subset\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(A^{\prime},A^{\prime})
, and the statement follows.
∎

For the next lemma and its proof, cf.
Definition

5.15
for the subcategory
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
additively spanned by matching objects, and
Section

5.2.2
for the degrees between crossings of the associated
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths.

Lemma 5.32
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{M}
be a
2
-spherical object and
X\in\mathcal{D}
an object that does not lie in
\mathcal{M}
. Suppose that
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S,X)
is concentrated in degrees
i\in[-2,\infty)
. Then, for any morphism
f:S[-1]\to X
, the cofiber
\operatorname{cof}(f)
does not lie in
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

By contradiction, suppose that the cofiber
Y\coloneqq\operatorname{cof}(f)
of the morphism
S[-1]\to X
lies in
\mathcal{M}
. Then the cofiber
X
of the morphism
Y[-1]\to S[-1]
is not matching and it thus has a contribution from a crossing from
Y
to
S
of degrees
-1
and
0
, cf.
Figure

19
. By the
2
-Calabi–Yau property of
\mathcal{D}
, there is also a crossing from
S
to
Y
with degrees
2
and
3
. This is in contradiction with the fact that, by hypothesis, the morphism object

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S,Y)\simeq\operatorname{cof}(\underbrace{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S,S[-1])}_{\simeq k[-1]\oplus k[-3]}\to\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S,X))

is concentrated in degrees
i\in[-2,\infty)
.
∎

Corollary 5.33
.

Let
(\beta,{\bf a})
be a weighted braid word,
\mathcal{L}(\mathchar 45\relax)\colon N(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\to\mathcal{D}
a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object with geometric realization
L
.

(1)

If
L
is rigid, then
\mathcal{L}(i)
is also rigid for all
i\in[0,\infty)

(2)

If
L\in\mathcal{M}
, then also
\mathcal{L}(i)\in\mathcal{M}
for all
i\in[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Using
Lemma

5.4
, part (1) follows from
Lemma

5.31
, and part (2) follows from
Lemma

5.32
.
∎

5.3.2
Resolving endpoint intersections

The remaining ingredient before proving
Theorem

5.1
is used to construct the rigid filtered objects
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
: it is the construction of certain morphisms whose cofibers are rigid, as established in
Proposition

5.36
below. For that, we start with the cyclic order on endpoint intersections at a vertex of
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
, defined as follows:

Definition 5.34

(Cyclic order at vertices)
.

Let
\mathsf{v}\in{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
be a vertex and
C\coloneqq\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\}
be a collection of distinct
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths all of which have an endpoint at
\mathsf{v}\in{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
, arriving at
\mathsf{v}
with different slopes. By definition, the intersection order on
C
is the cyclic order on
C
obtained by choosing representatives of the
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths in
C
which have minimal numbers of intersections with each other, and declaring the cyclic order to be induced by the clockwise orientation. That is, in the intersection order,
\gamma_{i}<\gamma_{j}<\gamma_{k}
if and only if the angle with which
\gamma_{j}
approaches
\mathsf{v}
lies between the angles with which
\gamma_{k}
and
\gamma_{i}
approach
\mathsf{v}
.∎

In
Definition

5.34
, angles are understood to lie in
[0,2\pi)
, and cf.
[
KS02
, Section 3a]
for a discussion on representatives of paths with minimal number of intersections.

Remark 5.35
.

An equivalent and more algebraic version of
Definition

5.34
is that
\gamma_{i}<\gamma_{j}<\gamma_{k}
if and only if the composite of the non-zero morphisms
X_{\gamma_{i}}\to X_{\gamma_{j}}[a]
and the
[a]
shift of
X_{\gamma_{j}}\to X_{\gamma_{k}}[b]
arising from the endpoint intersections of
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
, and
\gamma_{j}
and
\gamma_{k}
, yields a non-zero morphism
X_{\gamma_{i}}\to X_{\gamma_{k}}[a+b]
.∎

Proposition 5.36
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{M}
be rigid object and
S\in\mathcal{M}
a 2-spherical object. Suppose that
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{D}}(S,X)\simeq 0
,
i\in[2,\infty)
, and that there are no degree
1
crossings from
S
to
X
. Then there exists a unique morphism
f:S[-1]\to X
, unique up to postcomposition with automorphisms of
X
, whose cofiber
\operatorname{cof}(f)
is rigid. Furthermore, this cofiber is matching, i.e.
\operatorname{cof}(f)\in\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

Let us write
Y\coloneqq\operatorname{cof}(f)
, and express
S=X_{\gamma}
for some
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path
\gamma
. Let
\mathsf{v},\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\in{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
be the two vertices of where
\gamma
ends. Consider the induced intersection order, cf.
Definition

5.34
, on the set
C
, resp.
C^{\prime}
, consisting of those
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths describing
X\in\mathcal{M}
with an endpoint at
\mathsf{v}
, resp.
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}
, and
\gamma
itself. We turn this cyclic orders on
C
and
C^{\prime}
into total orders by declaring
\gamma
to be the largest element in this total order. In each of these two total orders, consider the smallest element such that the corresponding endpoint intersection with
\gamma
is of degree
1
. These yield two morphisms
a,a^{\prime}\colon S[-1]\to X
, one from such smallest element in
C
and the corresponding one from
C^{\prime}
.

Now, we claim that the required morphism is their sum
f\coloneqq a+a^{\prime}
,
f:S[-1]\to X
. Let us argue that
\operatorname{cof}(f)
must be rigid. First, note that the cofiber of any morphism
S[-1]\to X
not containing contributions from both
a
and
a^{\prime}
cannot itself be rigid. Indeed, passing to the cofiber amounts to resolving the endpoint intersections, cf.
Figure

20
, and resolving degree
1
endpoint intersections different than the minimal degree
1
-ones leads to a self-crossing of the cofiber near
\mathsf{v}
, or
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}
, of degrees
0
and
1
, which contradicts rigidity. Now, to show that
\operatorname{cof}(f)
is rigid, note that if
a
or
a^{\prime}
corresponds to an endpoint intersection with non-minimal elements (though minimal of degree
1
), the assumption
\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\mathcal{D}}(S,X)\simeq 0
,
j\in[2,\infty)
, and the rigidity
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(X,X)\simeq 0
imply that either
a
or
a^{\prime}
must factor as
S[-1]\to X[-i]\to X
for some
i\in(-\infty,-2]
. That said, the degree
(-i+1)
-endpoint intersection leads to self-crossings of
\operatorname{cof}(a+a^{\prime})
in degrees
(-i+1),(-i+2)
and
i,(i+1)
, and since these are not degree
1
, we conclude
\operatorname{cof}(f)
is rigid.

Finally, for uniqueness, note that the degree
0
endpoint intersections of
X
with itself at
\mathsf{v}
and
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}
give rise to automorphisms of
X
which allow to remove all contributions from degree
1
endpoint intersections that are larger in the total order, as follows from
Remark

5.35
. Thus the morphism is uniquely determined up to such automorphisms, as required.
∎

Figure 22:
(Left) Pieces of
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths for
Example

5.37
, with
\gamma_{1}
indicating only a piece that continues to other parts of the graph
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
, and
\mathsf{e}_{1},\mathsf{e}_{2}
as
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths inside a neighborhood containing
\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2}
and
\mathsf{v}_{3}
. The morphism
\alpha:X_{\mathsf{e}_{1}}[-1]\longrightarrow X_{\mathsf{e}_{2}}
of degree 1 is indicated in green. (Right) The endpoint resolution of the morphism
\alpha
, which yields a crossing intersection with the matching object
X_{\gamma_{1}}
. Note that the
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path for
\operatorname{cof}(\alpha)
is given by the image of the inverse half-twist
f_{2}^{-1}(\mathsf{e}_{1})
, cf.
Lemma

5.18
.

Example 5.37
.

(1) Consider the case
n=3
, the two
\pi_{D}
-matching paths
\mathsf{e}_{1}
and
\mathsf{e}_{2}
, and choose
S\coloneqq X_{\mathsf{e}_{1}}\simeq S_{1}
and
X\coloneqq X_{\mathsf{e}_{2}}\simeq S_{2}
, cf.
Example

5.16
. The hypothesis of
Proposition

5.36
are satisfied, and the conclusion is straightforward in this example. Indeed, there is a unique degree 1 morphism
f:S[-1]\longrightarrow X
, see e.g.
Example

5.21
, and its cofiber
\operatorname{cof}(f)\simeq T_{X}^{-1}(S)
is indeed rigid and, by
Lemma

5.18
, matching, see e.g.
Figure

22
(right).

(2)
Figure

22
depicts an instance illustrating that resolving an endpoint intersection differently than in the proof of
Proposition

5.36
yields a non-rigid object. Consider
S\coloneqq X_{\mathsf{e}_{1}}
and
X=X_{\gamma_{1}}\oplus X_{\mathsf{e}_{2}}
, such that the two
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths for
X
are
\gamma_{1}
and
\mathsf{e}_{2}
, as in
Figure

22
(left). Suppose that
X
is rigid, which is for instance the case if the
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching path
\gamma_{1}
only intersects
\mathsf{e}_{2}
at
\mathsf{v}_{2}
, by
Figure

20
. In this situation, reading clockwise, we have a degree 1 morphism from
S
to
X_{\gamma_{1}}
, a degree 0 morphism from
X_{\gamma_{1}}
to
X_{\mathsf{e}_{2}}
, and a degree 1 morphism from
X_{\mathsf{e}_{2}}
to
S
. Their intersection order at
\mathsf{v}_{2}
is
X_{\gamma_{1}}<X_{\mathsf{e}_{2}}<S
and according to the proof of
Proposition

5.36
we should resolve the degree 1 morphism from
S
to
X_{\gamma_{1}}
if we want to obtain a rigid object.

If, for illustrative purposes, we instead resolved the degree 1 morphism
\alpha
from
S
to
X_{\mathsf{e}_{2}}
, depicted in green in
Figure

22
(left), then its resolution would lead to an object
X_{\gamma_{1}}\oplus\operatorname{cof}(\alpha)
. That said, the direct sum
X_{\gamma_{1}}\oplus\operatorname{cof}(\alpha)
would not be rigid due to the presence of the crossing between
\operatorname{cof}(\alpha)
and
X_{\gamma_{1}}
, which is circled in pink in
Figure

22
(right). Indeed, this crossing contributes to
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\operatorname{cof}(\alpha),X_{\gamma_{1}})
by
Figure

20
, and thus the direct sum
X_{\gamma_{1}}\oplus\operatorname{cof}(\alpha)
is not rigid.∎

5.3.3
Proof of
Theorem

5.1

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

Let us suppose that the
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
exists and prove its uniqueness, up to equivalence. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}^{\prime}
be another
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object with rigid geometric realization. We now prove that the restrictions of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}^{\prime}
to
N(\mathbb{Z}_{[0,i]})
are equivalent, by induction on
i\in[0,\infty)
. The base case
i=0
holds by construction. For the inductive step, we suppose that the assertion holds for
i-1
, and show it for
i
, as follows. By definition, the
i
th object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i)
of the filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
arises as the cofiber of a morphism
S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus a_{i}}[-1]\to\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)
or, equivalently, as the
a_{i}
-many iterated cofibers along morphisms with domain
S_{\beta_{i}}[-1]
. By
Corollary

5.33
,
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i)
is rigid and lies in
\mathcal{M}
, and thus
S_{\beta_{i}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)\simeq\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}^{\prime}(i-1)
cannot have a crossing contributing to degrees
0
and
1
.
Proposition

5.36
thus shows that there exists a unique morphism
f\colon S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus a_{i}}[-1]\to\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)
, up to equivalence, whose cofiber is rigid. Therefore, the morphisms
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)\to\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i)
and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)^{\prime}\to\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i)^{\prime}
must both be equivalent to the cofiber morphism
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)\to\operatorname{cof}(f)
of
f
. We thus obtain that the restrictions of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}^{\prime}
to
N(\mathbb{Z}_{[0,i]})
are also equivalent, concluding the induction step. This establishes the uniqueness of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, up to equivalence.∎

For context, thought it will not be used in this article, we record our expectation that the following variation of
Theorem

5.1
might hold:

Conjecture 5.38
.

Let
(\beta,{\bf a})
be a weighted braid word. Then there exists a unique
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{D}
whose geometric realization is rigid.∎

5.38
makes no assertion that
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{M}
, cf.
Theorem

5.1
. An approach to such conjecture via our argument for
Theorem

5.1
would require understanding how to generalize the endpoint resolutions, cf. the proof of
Proposition

5.36
, to interact with degree 1 crossings while preserving rigidity, cf.
Example

5.37
.

5.3.4
A few comments on
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}

(A) The recursive form for
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
.
The proof of
Theorem

5.1
provides an explicit construction of the filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, if it exists, as follows. It is given by the
\mathcal{D}
-valued
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-filtration

\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(l-1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{l}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(l)

(50)

recursively defined by

\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i)\coloneqq\operatorname{cof}(S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus a_{i}}[-1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartheta_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)),\quad i\in[1,l],\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(0)\coloneqq S_{\beta_{0}}^{\oplus a_{0}},

(51)

and constant equal to
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i)\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(l)
for all
i\in[l,\infty)
. By
Proposition

5.36
, the morphism

\vartheta_{i}\colon S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus a_{i}}[-1]\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)

corresponds to the (up to)
a_{i}
endpoint intersections of degree
1
, at each of the endpoints of
S_{\beta_{i}}
, which are minimal in the order described in
Definition

5.34
. The morphisms
f_{i}
of the filtration (
50
) are given by the canonical inclusion of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)
into the cofiber of
\vartheta_{i}
.

Remark 5.39
.

By the proof of
Proposition

5.36
, the cofiber

\operatorname{cof}(S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus a_{i}}[-1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartheta_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1))

can be geometrically understood as a resolution of endpoint intersections of the matching paths describing
S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus a_{i}}
, which are readily obtained by using
Lemma

5.18
, and the matching paths defining
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i-1)
. In this sense,
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
can be understood as an iterated resolution of endpoint intersections of matching paths.∎

(B) The coherent chain complex
\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
corresponding to
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
.
The
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
can be alternatively described in terms of a coherent chain complex, as follows from the
\infty
-categorical Dold–Kan correspondence, see also
Remark

B.3
. Appendix
B
provides a summary on coherent chain complexes. Intuitively, the role of coherent chain complexes in a stable
\infty
-category is akin to that of twisted complexes of
A_{\infty}
-modules in a
A_{\infty}
-category. In a nutshell, the graded pieces of any
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-filtered object
X(\mathchar 45\relax)
in
\mathcal{D}
determine a
\mathcal{D}
-valued coherent chain complex. Specifically, the
i
-th term of such a coherent chain complex is the shift of the corresponding piece of the associated graded, i.e. the cofiber
\operatorname{cof}(f_{i})[-i]
, where
f_{i}\colon X(i-1)\to X(i)
is the morphism defining the filtered object.In our case this coherent chain complex is described as follows.

Let
(\beta,{\bf a})
be a weighted braid word arising from a horizontal slice of a Lusztig cycle. We denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\operatorname{Ch}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{D})
the coherent chain complex determined by the filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
. By construction, cf.
Equation

51
, the
i
th graded piece
\operatorname{cof}(f_{i})
of the filtration (
50
) is equivalent to
S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus a_{i}}
, and thus
\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
is concentrated in degrees
0
to
l
and has the form

\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}=\left(S_{\beta_{l}}^{\oplus a_{l}}[-l]\longrightarrow S_{\beta_{l-1}}^{\oplus a_{l-1}}[-l+1]\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow S_{\beta_{2}}^{\oplus a_{2}}[-2]\longrightarrow S_{\beta_{1}}^{\oplus a_{1}}[-1]\longrightarrow S_{\beta_{0}}^{\oplus a_{0}}\right)

(52)

where we have drawn arrows for the differentials of degree
(-1)
, but we did not draw those corresponding to the higher coherence data. The geometric realization
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}=\operatorname{colim}\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i)
is equivalent to the total cofiber of
\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, which can be computed as an iterated cofiber, see
Equation

51
above and
Remark

B.6
. In particular,
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
is given by an iterated extension between the
S_{\beta_{i}}^{\oplus a_{i}}
’s.

Remark 5.40
.

Even though the coherent chain complexes
\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
contains the same data as the filtered objects
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, it is sometimes convenient to use them when establishing results related to
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
. In particular, we use this presentation in terms of coherent chain complexes in the proofs of some of the results on categorical Lusztig cycles, cf. the proofs of
Proposition

6.2
and
Proposition

6.5
.∎

(C) The role of rigidity.
We conclude this section with an example illustrating the behavior of non-rigid
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered objects under the tropical propagation rules for weave cycles. These weighted braid words do arise as slices of weave cycles, though not as slices of Lusztig cycles. We hope this example highlights the importance of rigidity in our constructions.

Example 5.41
.

To ease notation in this example, a weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
where
{\bf a}
takes only the values
0
and
1
will be denoted by writing the braid word
\beta
with dots above the letters where
{\bf a}
evaluates to
1
. For instance,
s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}
denotes
(\beta,{\bf a})=(s_{1}s_{2}s_{1},(0,1,0))
.

Let us consider the follow three weighted braid words:

(\beta,{\bf a})=\sigma_{1}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{1}\sim(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})=\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{1}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{3}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{1}\sim(\beta^{\prime\prime},{\bf a^{\prime\prime}})=\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}.

Note that the corresponding braid words are braid equivalent, differing only via braid moves, and the weights have been propagated according to the tropical propagation rules from
Section

2.1.2
, cf.
Equation

11
. For the first weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})=\sigma_{1}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{1}
, the two spherical objects corresponding to the first and second
\dot{\sigma_{2}}
are
T_{S_{1}}^{-1}T_{S_{2}}^{-1}T_{S_{3}}^{-1}T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
and
T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2}))
, respectively. Note that the former is equivalent to
T_{S_{3}}(S_{2})
, and thus the
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
-matching paths associated to
T_{S_{3}}(S_{2})
and
T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2}))
have a degree
1
crossing, but no endpoint intersections. On the one hand, resolving all endpoint intersections yields the direct sum
T_{S_{3}}(S_{2})\oplus T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
, which is a non-rigid object with a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration. On the other hand, the cofiber of the crossing morphism
T_{S_{3}}(S_{2})[-1]\to T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
is a rigid but non-matching object with a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration.

For the second weighted braid word
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
,
Equation

11
and the construction of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
imply that applying the (weighted) braid move
\sigma_{1}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{1}\to\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{1}\dot{\sigma_{2}}
from
(\beta,{\bf a})
has the effect of expressing
T_{S_{3}}(S_{2})
as
\operatorname{cof}(S_{3}[-1]\to S_{2})
, see e.g.
Example

6.4
below. Similarly, for the third weighted braid word
(\beta^{\prime\prime},{\bf a^{\prime\prime}})
, the corresponding weighted braid move
\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{3}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\to\sigma_{3}\dot{\sigma_{2}}\sigma_{3}
from
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
has the effect of expressing
T_{S_{1}}^{-1}T_{S_{3}}^{-1}(S_{2})
as
\operatorname{cof}(T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})[-1]\to S_{3})
. The spherical objects associated to the first and second
\dot{\sigma_{2}}
in
(\beta^{\prime\prime},{\bf a^{\prime\prime}})
are

T_{S_{1}}^{-1}T_{S_{3}}^{-1}T_{S_{2}}^{-1}T_{S_{3}}^{-1}T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})\simeq S_{2},\quad\mbox{ and }\quad T_{S_{1}}^{-1}T_{S_{3}}^{-1}(S_{2}),

respectively. The former again has a degree
1
crossing with the latter, but no endpoint intersection. Therefore
S_{2}\oplus T_{S_{1}}^{-1}T_{S_{3}}^{-1}(S_{2})
is a non-rigid matching
(\beta^{\prime\prime},{\bf a^{\prime\prime}})
-filtered object, whereas
\operatorname{cof}(S_{2}[-1]\to T_{S_{1}}^{-1}T_{S_{3}}^{-1}(S_{2}))
is a rigid non-matching
(\beta^{\prime\prime},{\bf a^{\prime\prime}})
-filtered object. Finally, note that the two rigid objects arising from
(\beta,{\bf a})
and
(\beta^{\prime\prime},{\bf a^{\prime\prime}})
are

\operatorname{cof}(T_{S_{3}}(S_{2})[-1]\to T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2}))\simeq\operatorname{cof}(S_{2}[-1]\to T_{S_{1}}^{-1}T_{S_{3}}^{-1}(S_{2})),

which are equivalent. In contrast, the non-rigid objects we obtained from
(\beta,{\bf a})
and
(\beta^{\prime\prime},{\bf a^{\prime\prime}})
are

T_{S_{3}}(S_{2})\oplus T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})\not\simeq S_{2}\oplus T_{S_{1}}^{-1}T_{S_{3}}^{-1}(S_{2})\,,

which are not equivalent. Thus in the non-rigid case, the objects themselves change, and not just their filtrations, if one uses the tropical propagation rules from
Equation

11
.∎

6
Weave propagation of rigid filtered objects

The goal of this section is to establish how a rigid
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, in the sense of
Definition

5.27
, changes when either a weighted braid move, or a weighted
s_{i}^{2}\to s_{i}
transformation, are applied to
(\beta,{\bf a})
, cf.
Section

2.1.2
. Specifically, we prove the equivalence in Statement (
30
) and prove the cofiber sequence from
Equation

28
, in
Proposition

6.2
and
Proposition

6.5
respectively. The former implies that the geometric realization of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
is invariant under a weighted braid move, whereas the latter exhibits the homological relation between
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
and the filtered object associated to the weighted braid word obtained from
(\beta,{\bf a})
via a weighted
s_{i}^{2}\to s_{i}
transformation.

Given a weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
such that a rigid
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
exists, we denote by

L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\coloneqq\mbox{colim }\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}

the geometric realization of
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, cf.
Remark

5.28
.

6.1
Propagation via weighted braid moves

The weighted analogue of the braid moves is as follows:

Definition 6.1

(Weighted braid moves)
.

Let
(\beta,{\bf a})
be a weighted braid word. By definition, a weighted braid move
(\beta,{\bf a})\to(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
is a braid move
\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
such that:

1.

If
\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
is of the form
s_{i}s_{i+1}s_{i}\leftrightarrow s_{i+1}s_{i}s_{i+1}
, applied to the
j,(j+1)
and
(j+2)
crossings of
\beta
, then the weights satisfy

(a^{\prime}_{j},a^{\prime}_{j+1},a^{\prime}_{j+2})=\left(a_{j+1}+a_{j+2}-\min(a_{j},a_{j+2}),\min(a_{j},a_{j+2}),a_{j}+a_{j+1}-\min(a_{j},a_{j+2})\right),

and else
a^{\prime}_{k}=a_{k}
if
k\not\in\{j,j+1,j+2\}
.

2.

If
\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
is of the form
s_{i}s_{q}\leftrightarrow s_{q}s_{i}
,
|q-i|\geq 2
, applied to the
j,(j+1)
st crossings of
\beta
, then the weights satisfy

(a^{\prime}_{j},a^{\prime}_{j+1})=\left(a_{j+1},a_{j}\right),

and else
a^{\prime}_{k}=a_{k}
if
k\not\in\{j,j+1\}
.

In short, a weighted braid move is a braid move along with a change of the associated weights according to the tropical propagation rules in
Equation

11
.∎

By the construction of Lusztig cycles, cf.
Section

2.1.2
, the weighted braid words associated to a Lusztig cycle before and after a 4-valent or 6-valent vertex of a weave
\mathfrak{w}
are related by a single weighted braid move as in
Definition

6.1
. Consequently, the move in
Definition

6.1
.(1) is referred to as a 6-valent move, or a weighted Reidemeister 3 move, and the move in
Definition

6.1
.(2) is said to be 4-valent move, or a weighted commutation move.

Let us now show that, under the assumption of rigidity, being filtered by a weighted braid word is preserved under weighted braid moves. This provides a categorical interpretation of the tropical propagation rules for Lusztig cycles, as described in
Section

2.1.2
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
(\beta,{\bf a})
be weighted braid word and
X\in\mathcal{D}
a rigid
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object. Suppose that
(\beta,{\bf a})\to(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a}^{\prime})
is a weighted braid move. Then
X
also admits a
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
-filtration.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be the
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object with geometric realization
X
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the coherent chain complex corresponding to
\mathcal{L}
, as in
Section

5.3.4
.(B), so that the totalization
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C})\simeq X
is equivalent to the geometric realization
X
of
\mathcal{L}
. Let us prove the statement for the two weighted braid moves
(\beta,{\bf a})\to(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
from
Definition

6.1
.

6-valent move.
Let us suppose that the braid
\beta
is of the form
\beta=\beta_{A}(\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1})\beta_{B}
, where
\beta_{A},\beta_{B}
are arbitrary positive braid words, and denote by
\kappa\coloneqq\ell(\beta_{A})
the length of
\beta_{A}
. Thus the resulting braid
\beta^{\prime}
reads
\beta^{\prime}=\beta_{A}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\beta_{B}
, with its weights
{\bf a^{\prime}}
depending on the weights
{\bf a}
according to
Definition

6.1
.(1).

First, we can reduce to the case where the suffix
\beta_{B}=1
is the trivial braid word as follows. For
\beta_{B}=\sigma_{i_{0}}\dots\sigma_{i_{k}}
, we write
T_{\beta_{B}}=T_{S_{i_{k}}}\dots T_{S_{i_{0}}}
. By applying the iterated inverse twist
T_{\beta_{B}}^{-1}[-\kappa]
to the coherent chain complex
\mathcal{C}
, we obtain a coherent chain complex of the form

T_{\beta_{B}}^{-1}(\mathcal{C})[-\kappa]\simeq(\dots\rightarrow S_{i+1}^{\oplus a_{\kappa+2}}[-2]\xrightarrow{\partial_{\kappa+2}}\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to S_{i+1}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}[-1]\xrightarrow{\partial_{\kappa+1}}S_{i}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}\rightarrow\dots)\,.

In fact, both depicted differentials vanish, i.e.
\partial_{\kappa+1}=0
and
\partial_{\kappa+2}=0
, because of the vanishing of the corresponding
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}
-groups. It thus suffices to work directly with the arising
3
-term coherent chain complex

S_{i+1}^{\oplus a_{\kappa+2}}[-2]\xrightarrow{0}\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to S_{i+1}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}[-1]\xrightarrow{0}S_{i}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}},

(53)

which is effectively setting
\beta_{B}=1
and focusing on the crossings where the braid move occurs. Note that the presence of the prefix
\beta_{A}
only shifts the subindices by its length
\kappa
.
Lemmas

5.4
and
5.31
together with the rigidity of
X
imply that the totalization of (
53
) is rigid.

Second, our task is to compare the totalization of (
53
) and the totalization of the following coherent chain complex

S_{i}^{\oplus a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime}}[-2]\xrightarrow{0}\operatorname{fib}(S_{i+1}\to S_{i}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}^{\prime}}[-1]\xrightarrow{0}S_{i+1}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}^{\prime}}

(54)

given by specifying the morphism

S_{i}^{\oplus a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime}}[-1]\subset\operatorname{cof}(S_{i}^{\oplus a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime}}[-2]\xrightarrow{0}\operatorname{fib}(S_{i+1}\to S_{i}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}^{\prime}}[-1])\to S_{i+1}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}^{\prime}}

to be any morphism of maximal rank, when considered as a
a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime}\times a_{\kappa}^{\prime}
-matrix via the equivalence
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{i}^{a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime}},S_{i+1}^{a_{\kappa}})\simeq k^{a_{\kappa}^{\prime}\cdot a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime}}
.

Third, we compute the totalization of (
53
) as follows. Since the cofiber of the morphism
S_{i+1}^{\oplus a_{\kappa+2}}[-2]\xrightarrow{0}\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to S_{i+1}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}[-1]
is equivalent to
S_{i+1}^{\oplus a_{\kappa+2}}[-1]\oplus\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to S_{i+1}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}[-1]
, the totalization of (
53
) is thus given by the cofiber of the arising morphism

S_{i+1}^{\oplus a_{\kappa+2}}[-1]\oplus\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to S_{i+1}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}[-1]\xlongrightarrow{(\alpha,0)}S_{i}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}\,.

We present
\alpha
as a
(a_{\kappa+2}\times a_{\kappa})
-matrix and observe that the totalization of (
53
) can only be rigid only if this matrix is of maximal rank, and so it is. Therefore, the totalization of (
53
) is given by the direct sum:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to S_{i+1}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{cof}(S_{i+1}[-1]\to S_{i})^{\oplus\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa+2},a_{\kappa})}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}S_{i}^{\oplus[a_{\kappa}-a_{\kappa+2}]_{+}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}S_{i+1}^{\oplus[-a_{\kappa}+a_{\kappa+2}]_{+}}}

(55)

where we use the notation
[a]_{+}\coloneqq\max(a,0)
. Note that either
[a_{\kappa}-a_{\kappa+2}]_{+}=0
or
[-a_{\kappa}+a_{\kappa+2}]_{+}=0
, and thus either the third term (in cyan) or the fourth one (in orange) vanish, which is essential for rigidity to hold, as
S_{i}\oplus S_{i+1}
is not rigid.

Similarly, the totalization of the coherent complex (
54
) reads

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{cof}(S_{i}[-1]\to S_{i+1})^{\oplus\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime},a_{\kappa}^{\prime})}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{fib}(S_{i+1}\to S_{i}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}^{\prime}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}S_{i}^{\oplus[-a_{\kappa}^{\prime}+a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime}]_{+}}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}S_{i+1}^{\oplus[a_{\kappa}^{\prime}-a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime}]_{+}}}

(56)

It now suffices to compare the summands of (
55
) and (
56
). For that, let us use that for this weighted braid move the weights
{\bf a^{\prime}}
, as in
Definition

6.1
.(1), indeed satisfy:

\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime},a_{\kappa}^{\prime})=a_{\kappa+1}\,,\quad a_{\kappa+1}^{\prime}=\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa+2},a_{\kappa})\,,

\operatorname{max}(0,a_{\kappa}^{\prime}-a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime})=\operatorname{max}(0,-a_{\kappa}+a_{\kappa+2})\,,

\operatorname{max}(0,-a_{\kappa}^{\prime}+a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime})=\operatorname{max}(0,a_{\kappa}-a_{\kappa+2})\,.

Therefore the third terms of (
55
) and (
56
), in cyan, match, as do their fourth terms, in orange. The first
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to S_{i+1}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}}
of (
55
), in blue, is equivalent to

\operatorname{fib}(S_{i}\to S_{i+1}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}\simeq\operatorname{cof}(S_{i}[-1]\to S_{i+1})^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}\simeq\operatorname{cof}(S_{i}[-1]\to S_{i+1})^{\oplus\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa+2}^{\prime},a_{\kappa}^{\prime})},

and thus it is indeed equivalent to the first term of (
56
). Similarly, the second term
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\operatorname{fib}(S_{i+1}\to S_{i}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}^{\prime}}}
of (
56
), in red, is equivalent to

\operatorname{fib}(S_{i+1}\to S_{i}[1])^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}^{\prime}}\simeq\operatorname{cof}(S_{i+1}[-1]\to S_{i})^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}^{\prime}}\simeq\operatorname{cof}(S_{i+1}[-1]\to S_{i})^{\oplus\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa+2},a_{\kappa})}

and hence it is equivalent to the second term of (
55
). In conclusion, the objects (
55
) and (
56
) are equivalent. By grafting twice, using
Lemma

B.8
, we conclude that the rigid object
X
is also
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a}^{\prime})
-filtered.

4-valent move.
Let us now suppose that
\beta=\beta_{A}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\beta_{B}
,
|i-j|\geq 2
. The resulting word is
\beta^{\prime}=\beta_{A}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\beta_{B}
, with the weights
{\bf a}
to
{\bf a^{\prime}}
changing according to
Definition

6.1
.(2), i.e.
a_{\kappa}^{\prime}=a_{\kappa+1}
and
a_{\kappa+1}^{\prime}=a_{\kappa}
where, as before,
\kappa\coloneqq\ell(\beta_{A})
is the length of
\beta_{A}
. In this case we must compare the totalizations of the original coherent complex

\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\qquad\dots\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+2}\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}\xrightarrow{\partial_{\kappa+1}}\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa-1}\to\dots

(57)

for
\beta
, and the totalization of the, yet to be defined, complex
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
for
\beta^{\prime}
, which reads

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\qquad\dots\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+2}\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}[-1]\xrightarrow{\partial^{\prime}_{\kappa+1}}\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}[1]\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa-1}\to\dots

(58)

The key fact is that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{\sigma_{j}\beta_{B}},S_{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\beta_{B}})\simeq\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{\sigma_{j}},S_{\sigma_{i}})\simeq 0
vanishes, since
|i-j|\geq 2
, and therefore the differential
\partial_{\kappa+1}:\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
in the coherent complex
\mathcal{C}
vanishes, so that we have

S_{\sigma_{j}\beta_{B}}^{\oplus a_{\kappa+1}}[-\kappa-1]\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}\xrightarrow{0}\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}\simeq S_{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\beta_{B}}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}[-\kappa]

in degrees
\kappa
and
\kappa+1
of
\mathcal{C}
. Similarly
\partial^{\prime}_{\kappa+1}\simeq 0
for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in (
58
). The cofibers of
\partial_{\kappa+1}
and
{\partial^{\prime}_{\kappa+1}}
are thus equivalent to the direct sum
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}[1]\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
. Therefore, by grafting via
Lemma

B.8
, the totalization
X
of
\mathcal{C}
in (
57
) is equivalent to the totalization of the complex

\dots\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+3}[1]\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+2}[1]\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}[1]\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}}\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa-1}\to\dots

(59)

where the grafting part is indicated in blue in (
59
). The chain complex
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is obtained from (
59
) using grafting as well, and hence their totalizations coincide. Thus
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\simeq\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C})\simeq X
. In consequence,
X\simeq\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\simeq\mbox{colim}\mathcal{L}_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}
, which shows that
X
is also
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a})
-filtered, as required.
∎

Corollary 6.3

(Invariance of
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
under weighted braid moves)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave and
\gamma_{i}
a Lusztig cycle. Suppose that
(\beta,{\bf a})
and
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a}^{\prime})
are two weighted braid words obtained from two different horizontal slices of
\gamma_{i}
such that there are no trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
between them. Then there exists an equivalence
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\simeq L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a}^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

5.1
,
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered objects are unique. By hypothesis,
(\beta,{\bf a})
and
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a}^{\prime})
are related by weighted braid moves, and thus
Theorem

5.1
and
Proposition

6.2
imply that
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\simeq L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a}^{\prime}}
.
∎

Example 6.4
.

Let us use the dot notation for weighted braid words with binary weights
0
or
1
, as in
Example

5.41
, so
s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}
denotes
(\beta,{\bf a})=(s_{1}s_{2}s_{1},(0,1,0))
and
\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}
denotes
(\beta,{\bf a})=(s_{2}s_{1}s_{2},(1,0,1))
.

(i)
Consider the weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})=s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}
, so that
\beta_{0}=s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}
,
\beta_{1}=s_{2}s_{1}
and
\beta_{2}=s_{1}
. Then the associated filtered object has
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(0)=0
, as
a_{0}=0
,
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(1)=S_{\beta_{1}}=T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
, since
a_{1}=1
, and
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(i)\simeq\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(1)
for all
i\in[1,\infty)
, as
a_{2}=0
. Thus the filtration in
Theorem

5.1
, cf.
Equation

50
, reads

\{0\}\longrightarrow T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{id}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2}).

(60)

From the viewpoint of
Section

5.3.4
.(B), the corresponding coherent chain complex
\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
is given by

\mathcal{C}_{s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}}=\left(0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})[-1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0\right),

(61)

where the only non-zero entry is in homological degree 1. Note that the cofiber totalization of
\mathcal{C}_{s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}}
in
Equation

61
is equivalent to the cofiber of the zero map
d_{1}:T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})[-1]\longrightarrow 0
, which indeed coincides with
L_{s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}}=T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
from
Equation

60
.

(ii)
Consider the weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})=\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}
, so that
\beta_{0}=s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}
,
\beta_{1}=s_{1}s_{2}
and
\beta_{2}=s_{2}
. Then
S_{\beta_{0}}=T_{S_{2}}^{-1}T_{S_{1}}^{-1}S_{2}\simeq S_{1}
and
S_{\beta_{2}}=S_{2}
and the associated filtered object reads

S_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{id}}}{{\longrightarrow}}S_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2}),

(62)

where
\theta:S_{1}\longrightarrow T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
is identified via
T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})\simeq T_{S_{2}}S_{1}
with the canonical map
S_{1}\longrightarrow T_{S_{2}}S_{1}
to the cofiber of the evaluation map from
S_{2}
to
S_{1}
. The morphism also
\theta
corresponds to an endpoint intersection.

In this case, the coherent chain complex
\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
is given by

\mathcal{C}_{\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}}=\hbox to151.78pt{\vbox to40.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 75.88876pt\lower-8.65973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{27.64581pt}{2.35277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
