\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(63)

where
\iota:S_{2}[-1]\longrightarrow S_{1}
is a non-zero morphism in
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{2},S_{1})\simeq k[-1]
. The cofiber totalization of
\mathcal{C}_{\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}}
in
Equation

63
is

\operatorname{cof}(S_{2}[-1]\longrightarrow\operatorname{cof}(0\longrightarrow S_{1}))\simeq\operatorname{cof}(S_{2}[-1]\longrightarrow S_{1})\simeq\operatorname{cof}(\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{2},S_{1})\otimes S_{2}\longrightarrow S_{1})\simeq T_{S_{2}}S_{1}.

Since
T_{S_{2}}S_{1}\simeq T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
, this coincides with
L_{\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}}=T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
from
Equation

62
.

(iii)
From
(i)
and
(ii)
above, we directly deduce that
L_{s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}}\simeq L_{\dot{s}_{2}s_{1}\dot{s}_{2}}
, as they are both equivalent to
T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})
. This is indeed in accordance with
Proposition

6.2
and
Corollary

6.3
. Note that the induced filtrations are nevertheless different, cf. filtrations (
60
) and (
62
).∎

6.2
Propagation across trivalent vertices

The goal of this subsection is to prove the cofiber sequence (
28
), which we establish in
Proposition

6.5
. Specifically, given a weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
with
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
rigid, we want to relate
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
and
L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}
where
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
is obtained from
(\beta,{\bf a})
via the
3
-valent move, meaning the weighted braid operation
s_{i}^{2}\to s_{i}
applied to two consecutive and equal crossings of
\beta
, where the weights change according to
Equation

11
. That is, all weights remain equal except the weight of the new crossing, which must be the minimum of the two weights associated to the two crossings to which we applied the operation.

For context, we recall from
Section

4
that the cofiber sequence (
28
) is a central ingredient in the construction of the categorical Lusztig cycles. In particular, by
Remark

2.11
, it supplies a collection of relative triangles which are used in
Definition

4.1
to specify the required global sections of the weave schober. The statement we need is as follows:

Proposition 6.5

(Filtered objects across trivalents)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave,
p\in\mathfrak{w}
a trivalent vertex and
\gamma
the Lusztig cycle of a trivalent vertex above
p
. Consider the weighted braid words
(\beta,{\bf a})
and
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
associated to
\gamma
from horizontal slices right above and right below
p
, respectively, and suppose that a rigd
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
exists. Then a rigid
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
-filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}
exists and there exists a cofiber sequence in
\mathcal{D}

\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},L_{\beta,{\bf a}})\otimes S_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ev}}{{\longrightarrow}}L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\longrightarrow L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}\,

(64)

where
\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},L_{\beta,{\bf a}})
denotes the maximal summand of the morphism object contained in homologically positive degrees and the left morphism
ev
indicates the corresponding evaluation morphism.

Proof.

Let us use the coherent chain complex
\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
in
\mathcal{D}
associated to the filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, cf.
Section

5.3.4
.(B). To ease notation we set
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
, which reads

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\ast}\coloneqq\qquad\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}[-\kappa]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa-1}}[2-\kappa]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa-2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}

(65)

where
\kappa-1
and
\kappa
denote the indices of the two equal crossings
s_{i_{\kappa-1}}=s_{i_{\kappa}}
of
\beta
to which the operation
s_{i_{\kappa}}^{2}\to s_{i_{\kappa}}
is being applied. Note that in (
65
) we have used that the
\kappa-1
and
\kappa
crossings are equal to express the degree
(\kappa-1)
term of
\mathcal{C}_{\ast}
as

S_{\beta_{\kappa-1}}[-(\kappa-1)]\simeq(T_{s_{i_{\kappa}}}^{-1}S_{\beta_{\kappa}})([-(\kappa-1)])\simeq S_{\beta_{\kappa}}[2-\kappa]\simeq S_{p}[2-\kappa],

since we have
S_{p}\simeq S_{\beta_{\kappa}}
by construction.

First, we consider a coherent chain complex
\mathcal{E}_{\ast}
endowed with a coherent chain map
\mathcal{E}_{\ast}\to\mathcal{C}_{\ast}
, as follows. In degrees
0
to
(\kappa-1)
, we define

\mathcal{E}_{\leq\kappa-1}\coloneqq\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p}[2-\kappa],\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa-1})\otimes S_{p}[2-\kappa].

In degree
\kappa
, we set
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}\coloneqq S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}-\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa},a_{\kappa-1})}[-\kappa]
and use the zero morphism
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa}\to\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{E}_{\leq\kappa-1})
to extend
\mathcal{E}_{\leq\kappa-1}
to
\mathcal{E}_{\leq\kappa}
. In degrees strictly larger than
\kappa
we declare
\mathcal{E}_{j}\simeq 0
, for all
j\in[\kappa+1,\infty)
. By using evaluation in degrees
0
to
\kappa-1
, and a monomorphism in degree
\kappa
, there is a canonical chain map

f:\mathcal{E}_{\ast}\to\mathcal{C}_{\ast}.

(66)

By construction, the chain map
f_{\leq\kappa-1}:\mathcal{E}_{\leq\kappa-1}\to\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa-1}
induces after totalization the evaluation morphism

\operatorname{tot}(f_{\leq\kappa-1})\simeq ev:\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa-1}))\otimes S_{p}\to\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa-1}).

(67)

Now, we claim that
\mathcal{E}_{\ast}
is equivalent to the coherent chain complex

\mathcal{E}_{\ast}\simeq\quad\dots\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow S_{p}^{\oplus a_{b}-\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa},a_{\kappa-1})}[-\kappa]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}{S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa-1}}[2-\kappa]}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\mathcal{C}_{\kappa-2})\otimes S_{p}\longrightarrow\dots

(68)

Indeed,
Lemma

5.4
implies that the morphism object
\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p}[2-\kappa],\mathcal{C}_{j})
is connective for all
j<\kappa-1
from which (
68
) follows.

Second, let us prove that
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{E}_{\ast})\simeq\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},L_{\beta,{\bf a}})\otimes S_{p}
, as follows.
Lemma

5.4
implies that
\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},S_{\beta_{j}})
is concentrated in strictly negative degree for all
j\in(\kappa,\infty)
, and thus we have an equivalence

\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},L_{\beta,{\bf a}})\simeq\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa})).

(69)

By the construction of
\mathcal{C}
, cf. (
65
), we have a cofiber sequence

\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq k-1})\to\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa})\to S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}.

(70)

Applying the functor
\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\mathchar 45\relax)
to (
70
) leads to the cofiber sequence

\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa-1}))\to\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa}))\to\underbrace{\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},S_{p})^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}}_{\simeq(k\oplus k[-2])^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}}\,.

(71)

By
Lemma

5.4
, and the fact that the differential
\partial_{\kappa+1}:S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}[-\kappa]\to S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa-1}}[2-\kappa]
in (
65
) has maximal rank, since
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
is rigid, we obtain that

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa-1}))\simeq\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p},S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa-1}}[1])\simeq k^{\oplus a_{\kappa-1}}

and that the connecting homomorphism

k^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa-1}))\simeq k^{\oplus a_{\kappa-1}}

for (
71
) is of maximal rank, i.e. of rank
\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa},a_{\kappa-1})
. In consequence, we obtain from (
71
) the (split) cofiber sequence

\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa-1}))\to\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\leq\kappa}))\to k^{\oplus a_{\kappa}-\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa},a_{\kappa_{1}})}\,.

(72)

Together with the equivalence (
69
), (
72
) implies that

\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{E}_{\ast})\simeq\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},L_{\beta,{\bf a}})\otimes S_{p}\,.

(73)

Third, by considering the cofiber of the map
f:\mathcal{E}_{\ast}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\ast}
in (
66
), we obtain the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{E}_{\ast}\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}-\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa},a_{\kappa-1})}[-\kappa]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa-1}}[2-\kappa]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\mathcal{C}_{\kappa-2})\otimes S_{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathcal{C}_{\ast}\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa}}[-\kappa]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{S_{p}^{\oplus a_{\kappa-1}}[2-\kappa]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa-2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\operatorname{cof}(f)\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{S_{p}^{\oplus\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa},a_{\kappa-1})}[-\kappa]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T_{S_{p}}\mathcal{C}_{\kappa-2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

By (
67
) and the equivalence (
73
), the totalization
\operatorname{tot}(f):\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{E}_{\ast})\longrightarrow\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast})
is equivalent to the evaluation map

ev:\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},L_{\beta,{\bf a}})\otimes S_{p}\to L_{\beta,{\bf a}}.

In order to conclude the statement of the proposition, it therefore suffices to show that the totalization
\operatorname{tot}{\operatorname{cof}(f)}
of the bottom row
\operatorname{cof}(f)
of the diagram above, in purple, is indeed equivalent to
L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}
. Indeed, it follows from grafting, see
Lemma

B.8
, that there is a coherent chain complex

\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a}^{\prime}}\simeq(\dots\to\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+1}[1]\to S_{p}^{\operatorname{min}(a_{\kappa},a_{\kappa-1})}[-\kappa+1]\to T_{S_{p}}\mathcal{C}_{\kappa-2}\to\dots),

whose totalization equivalent to
\operatorname{tot}(\operatorname{cof}(f))
. Thus

\operatorname{cof}(ev)\simeq\operatorname{tot}(\operatorname{cof}(f))\simeq\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a}^{\prime}})\,.

The desired filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}
is the one corresponding to the coherent chain complex
\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a}^{\prime}}
.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

In terms of semi-twists, cf.
Definition

A.10
,
Proposition

6.5
shows that

L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}\simeq T^{+,\geq 0}_{S_{p}}(L_{\beta,{\bf a}})

(74)

under the given hypotheses. By
Lemma

5.6
, semi-twists preserve rigidity and thus
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
being rigid implies that
L_{\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}}}
must be rigid as well.∎

Example 6.7
.

Let us provide a key instance of the cofiber sequence (
64
). For that, we consider the Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:s_{1}^{2}\longrightarrow s_{1}
consisting of a trivalent vertex
p\in\mathfrak{w}
. Consider an arbitrary weighted braid word
(\beta,{\bf a})
above the trivalent vertex, so that
\beta=s_{1}^{2}
and
(a_{0},a_{1})=(b,a)
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since
S_{p}\simeq S
,
S_{\beta_{0}}=S[1]
and
S_{\beta_{1}}=S
, and the associated filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}
is

\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(0)=S[1]^{a},\quad L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\simeq\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}(1)=\operatorname{cof}(S[-1]^{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}S[1]^{a})

(75)

where
f:S[-1]^{b}\longrightarrow S[1]^{a}
can be described by a linear map
M_{f}\in\operatorname{Mat}_{a\times b}(k)
tensored with
S
, since
\operatorname{Mor}(S,S)\simeq k\oplus k[-2]
as
S
is 2-spherical. Here we used the notation
S^{a}\coloneqq S^{\oplus a}
for ease. Due to its
construction in terms of endpoint resolutions,
M_{f}
is of maximal rank
\operatorname{rk}(M_{f})=\min(a,b)
. By using the cofiber sequence describing
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
in
Equation

75
, we obtain

\operatorname{Mor}(S,L_{\beta,{\bf a}})\simeq k[-2]^{b}\oplus k[-1]^{a-\min(a,b)}\oplus k^{b-\min(a,b)}\oplus k[1]^{a}

and thus

\tau_{\geq 0}\operatorname{Mor}(S,L_{\beta,{\bf a}})\otimes S\simeq S^{b-\min(a,b)}\oplus S[1]^{a}.

(76)

Consider the weighted braid word
(\beta^{\prime},a^{\prime})=(s_{1},\min(a,b))
below the trivalent vertex, as specified by
Equation

10
. The associated filtered object is
L_{\beta^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\simeq S^{\min(a,b)}
. The cofiber sequence in
Equation

64
therefore reads

S^{b-\min(a,b)}\oplus S[1]^{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{cof}(S[-1]^{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}S[1]^{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\longrightarrow}}S^{\min(a,b)}\,.

(77)

Specifically, if
b=\min(a,b)
, then the cofiber sequence (
64
) coincides with the cofiber sequence

S[1]^{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{cof}(S[-1]^{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}S[1]^{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\longrightarrow}}S^{b}\,

of the map
f
, with
h=\delta
being the natural connecting morphism
\delta:\operatorname{cof}(S[-1]^{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}S[1]^{a})\longrightarrow S^{b}
. Since
\delta
is a connecting morphism, we have
\operatorname{fib}(h)=S[1]^{a}
, and this is indeed a cofiber sequence. If
a=\min(a,b)
, then the cofiber sequence (
64
) reads

S^{b-\min(a,b)}\oplus S[1]^{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{cof}(S[-1]^{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}S[1]^{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\longrightarrow}}S^{\min(a,b)}\,,

with
h=(p\otimes S)\circ\delta
being the composition of the connecting morphism
\delta
and any surjective map
p:k^{b}\longrightarrow k^{a}
tensored with
S
. In this case,
\operatorname{fib}(h)
is obtained as the trivial extension of
\operatorname{fib}(p\otimes S)\simeq\ker(p)\otimes S\simeq S^{b-\min(a,b)}
by
\operatorname{fib}(\delta)\simeq S[1]^{a}
, and the above is indeed a cofiber sequence.∎

Corollary 6.8
.

Let
(\beta,{\bf a})
be a weighted braid word arising from a horizontal slice of a Lusztig cycle of a Demazure weave. Then, up to equivalence, there exists a unique
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{D}
whose geometric realization
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}
is rigid. In addition,
L_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

Let
p\in\mathfrak{w}
be the vertex associated to the Lusztig cycle
\gamma=\gamma_{p}
, so that
\gamma
is born at
p
and propagates downwards, cf.
Definition

2.1
. By construction, the weights of the Lusztig cycle above
p
vanish, and therefore the statement trivially holds for horizontal slices above
p
. For the horizontal slice right below
p
and the Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{p}
, the associated weighted braid word
(\beta_{p},{\bf a}_{p})
has a unique non-zero weight
a_{j}
, for some
j\in[0,\ell(\beta)]
, and this weight has value
a_{j}=1
. Thus
L_{\beta_{p},{\bf a}_{p}}\simeq S^{\oplus a_{j}}_{\beta_{j}}\simeq S_{\beta_{j}}
, which is indeed rigid and belongs to
\mathcal{M}
, since
S_{\beta_{j}}\in\mathcal{M}
by
Lemma

5.18
. The object
L_{\beta_{p},{\bf a}_{p}}
is trivially
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered and
Theorem

5.1
implies it is unique. The statement thus holds for the horizontal slice right below
p
by taking
\mathcal{L}_{\beta_{p},{\bf a}_{p}}
with the trivial filtration and colimit
L_{\beta_{p},{\bf a}_{p}}
.

Let us now argue that for any horizontal slice
h
of the Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
below
p
. By construction, the weighted braid word
(\beta_{h},{\bf a}_{h})
associated to
\gamma_{p}
and such horizontal slice
h
is obtained from
(\beta_{p},{\bf a}_{p})
by a combination of weighted braid moves and (weighted) trivalent moves
s_{i}^{2}\to s_{i}
, typically intertwined. For the weighted braid moves,
Proposition

6.2
and
Corollary

6.3
imply the required statement. For the weighted trivalent moves, i.e. propagating downwards through a 3-valent vertex,
Proposition

6.5
shows that the resulting filtered object below exists and it is obtained by applying a positive semi-twist to the filtered object above, cf. equivalence (
74
).
Lemma

5.6
shows that rigidity is therefore preserved when scanning downward past a trivalent vertex, and
Lemma

5.25
proves that being matching is also preserved, i.e. the result filtered object has colimit in
\mathcal{M}
. This establishes the existence of the required
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtered rigid object
\mathcal{L}_{\beta,{\bf a}}\in\mathcal{D}
, with geometric realization in
\mathcal{M}
. Its uniqueness follows from
Theorem

5.1
.
∎

This concludes the proofs for all the ingredients needed in the construction of the categorical Lusztig cycles in
Section

4
. In particular, the results thus far prove Theorem
A
. For reference, we record the proof as follows.

Proof of Theorem
A
.

Part (1) is stated and proven in
Corollary

6.8
. The proof for Part (2) follows from combining
Corollary

6.3
and
Proposition

6.5
, cf. also
Remark

4.3
. Part (3) is obtained from the construction of the categorical Lusztig cycles in
Section

4.1
, which is detailed and shown to be well-defined in the results of
Section

5
and this section.
∎

7
Matching sections for weave schobers and weave thimbles

The goal of this section is to construct a full exceptional collection
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}}\coloneqq\{{\Delta_{1}}^{\mathfrak{w}},\ldots,\Delta_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}}\}
in the
k
-linear stable
\infty
-category of global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, with
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
the weave schober of a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
. The exceptional collection
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}}
will be used to prove homological properties of the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
. In particular, we use them to prove Parts (2), (3) and (4) of Theorem
B
and Theorem
C
.

It is important to emphasize that the exceptional collection
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}}
is
not
invariant under weave equivalences: there exist weave equivalences
\mathfrak{w}\simeq\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}}\neq\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
. Thus these exceptional collections
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}}
are not intrinsically well-defined within the context of weave calculus. That said, the categorial Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
can be expressed in terms of the collection
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}}
in a manner that
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
are invariant under weave equivalences applied to
\mathfrak{w}
.

This section is organized as follows:

1.

Section

7.1
provides a general construction, in line with
Section

5.2.1
, to obtain global sections in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
from a certain type of matching paths on the plane of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
. Note that, in contrast with
Section

5.2
, the matching paths in
Section

7.1
are drawn on the plane where the weave
\mathfrak{w}
and the graph
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
are themselves drawn, and not in a different plane and with a different graph
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
as it occurred in
Section

5.2
. The resulting objects in this section belong to
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
and are, in a sense, 3-dimensional, cf.
Lemma

7.9
; the objects built in
Section

5.2.1
belonged to
\mathcal{D}
and were, in a sense, 2-dimensional, cf.
Section

5.2.2
.

2.

Section

7.2
provides a specific type of paths associated to a weave and discusses the notion and properties of weave parallel transport. The key geometric idea is to think of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
as a set of branch cuts. Even if it is a highly non-minimal branch cut, its rigid algebraic structure leads to remarkable relations between the different objects associated to the different types of weave paths.

3.

Section

7.3
uses the general construction from
Section

7.1
applied to the weave paths in
Section

7.2
to define the weave thimbles
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}}
of a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
, and their duals. A significant observation will be that these weave thimbles identify with the inductions of the vanishing cycles in the sense of
[
Chr25b
]
.
Section

7.3.1
proves that the weave thimbles
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}}
form an exceptional collection, and establishes their behavior under weave equivalences and weave mutations.

7.1
Global sections of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
from matching paths

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave and
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
be its ordered trivalent vertices, ordered from bottom to top. Following
Section

3.1
, we have the associated ribbon graph
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
with vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
and edges
e_{,}\ldots,e_{m}
, see
Figure

10
. As before, we use
v_{-\infty}\in e_{1}
to denote a generic point in the unique non-compact edge
e_{1}
. To ease notation, we will often denote the trivalent vertices
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
by
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
, identifying
p_{i}=v_{i}
if the context allows. Note that the graph
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
is always drawn vertically, in contrast to
{\bf G}_{\mathcal{D}}
in
Section

5.2
, which was drawn horizontally, cf.
Section

5.2.1
. Both
\mathfrak{w}
and
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
are drawn in a plane, equivalently a disk, that we denote by
\mathbb{D}
.

Definition 7.1

(
\mathfrak{w}
-matching paths)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave with trivalent vertices
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
. By definition, a
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path
\gamma
in
\mathbb{D}
is an embedded curve
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{D}
, such that:

(1)

\gamma(0)=v_{i}
and
\gamma(1)=v_{k}
, for some
k\in\{-\infty,1,\dots,m-1\}
and
i\in(k,m]
.

(2)

Near
\gamma(0)
the image of
\gamma
must coincide with either
Figure

25
.(1) or
Figure

25
.(2). That is, either point straight to the right, or point immediately to the left after having started with a velocity vector pointing upwards.

(3)

\gamma
goes only downwards after its start, i.e. with the exception of the local model near
\gamma(0)
in the previous item, the path
\gamma
goes downwards.

(4)

The interior of its image
\gamma(0,1)
is disjoint from
\{v_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}
,

(5)

If
j\not=-\infty
, there must exist an equivalence

S_{b}\simeq\mathcal{F}^{\rightarrow}(\gamma|_{(0,1)})(S_{a})[l]

in
\mathcal{D}
for some
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
a,b
are the indices such that the weave edges incident to
p_{i}
are labeled by
a
, i.e. by the permutation
s_{a}
, and the weave edges incident to
p_{k}
are labeled by
b
. Here we also denoted
\mathcal{F}^{\rightarrow}(\gamma|_{(0,1)})\colon\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(e_{i})\simeq\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(e_{k+1})
as the transport of
\mathcal{F}
along
\gamma
, cf.
[
Chr25b
, Section 4.3]
.

Such
\mathfrak{w}
-matching paths are considered up to ambient compactly supported smooth isotopy of
\mathbb{D}
relative to
\{v_{-\infty},v_{1},\dots,v_{n},\}
.∎

Note that
Definition

7.1
is purposefully ambiguous given the abuse of notation
p_{i}=v_{i}
identifying trivalents of
\mathfrak{w}
with vertices of
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
. Indeed, it could either suggest that we are drawing a path
\gamma
with endpoints on the trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
, or with endpoints on the vertices of
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
. Both viewpoints are valuable: the former is more intuitively geometric and matches the classical theory of matching paths, where the endpoints are branch points, whereas the latter is technically more accurate in the context of building sections for the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, which is actually defined on
{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
.
Figure

23
is an instance of the former viewpoint, drawing endpoints at the trivalents of weave
\mathfrak{w}
itself.

Remark 7.2
.

The condition
Definition

7.1
.(3) exists to fix the correct shift: e.g. the different angles in which a path leaves a trivalent of
\mathfrak{w}
only affect the resulting object by a shift. Also, in contrast to
Section

5.2.2
, we do not need to consider gradings for the
\mathfrak{w}
-matching paths in
Definition

7.1
. The condition
Definition

7.1
.(5) is an algebraic version of the requirement that parallel transport along
\gamma
connects the two vanishing spheres at the endpoints of
\gamma
, see
Section

7.1.2
.(iii).∎

Figure 23:
Four types of
\mathfrak{w}
-matching segments, cf.
Definition

7.3
and
Remark

7.4
.

Definition 7.3
.

Given
i\in[1,m]
, let
\mathbb{D}_{v_{i}}\subset\mathbb{D}
be a closed horizontal band whose upper boundary, resp. lower boundary, coincides with a horizontal slices immediately above, resp. below, the vertex
v_{i}
.

(1)

For
i\in[1,m-1]
, a
\mathfrak{w}
-matching segment in
\mathbb{D}_{v_{i}}
is defined to be an embedded curve
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{D}_{v_{i}}
such that

•

\gamma
is smoothly isotopic to a path that only goes downwards, relative to its endpoints.

•

\gamma
does not intersect
\{v_{i}\}
except for at most at one endpoint.

•

All endpoints of
\gamma
lie in
(\partial\mathbb{D}_{v_{i}}\cap\mathbb{D}^{\circ})\cup\{v_{i}\}
, i.e. on the top or bottom bounding slice or at
v_{i}
.

Such
\mathfrak{w}
-matching segments are considered up to compactly supported smooth isotopy of
\mathbb{D}_{v_{i}}
relative
\mathbb{D}_{v_{i}}\cup\{v_{i}\}
.

(2)

If
i=m
, a matching segment
\gamma
in
\mathbb{D}_{v_{m}}
is defined similarly, now imposing that
\gamma(0)=v_{m}
and that
\gamma(1)
lies on the lower boundary slice of
\mathbb{D}_{v_{m}}
.∎

Figure

23
depicts four
\mathfrak{w}
-matching segments illustrating
Definition

7.3
.

Remark 7.4
.

There is a unique matching segment in
\mathbb{D}_{v_{m}}
, denoted
\delta_{v_{m}}^{\downarrow}
. For
i\in[2,n]
, there are exactly four matching segments
\delta_{v_{i}}^{\dagger}
in
\mathbb{D}_{v_{i}}
up to compactly supported isotopy, where
\dagger\in\{\uparrow,\downarrow,\circlearrowright,\circlearrowleft\}
, cf.
Figure

23
. Indeed, there are two such segments with an endpoint at
v_{i}
, starting either at the top boundary, denoted
\delta_{v_{i}}^{\uparrow}
, or ending at the bottom boundary, denoted
\delta_{v_{i}}^{\downarrow}
. There are two
\mathfrak{w}
-matching segments starting at the top boundary and ending at the bottom boundary: going around
v_{i}
either clockwise
11
11

11

Note that clockwise segments turn right at a
2
-valent vertex, and are thus isotopic to local clockwise trajectories considered in
[
Chr25b
]
, since our orientations of the plane here are reversed from
[
Chr25b
]
.
, denoted
\delta_{v_{i}}^{\circlearrowright}
, or counterclockwise, denoted
\delta_{v_{i}}^{\circlearrowleft}
.∎

Note also that every
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path in
Definition

7.1
arises uniquely as the composite of matching segments.

7.1.1
Local lax sections from matching segments

Let
v_{i}
be a vertex of
{\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}
and
\delta
a
\mathfrak{w}
-matching segment in
\mathbb{D}_{v_{i}}
, as in
Definition

7.3
. We suppose further that we are given the following data, denoted
L
(also called a local value):

•

If
\delta=\delta_{v_{i}}^{\downarrow},\delta_{v_{i}}^{\uparrow}
, an element
L=k[l]\in\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)
, i.e. an integer
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•

If
\delta=\delta_{v_{i}}^{\circlearrowleft},\delta_{v_{i}}^{\circlearrowright}
, an element
L\in\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{F}(e_{i+1})
.

We now proceed with defining an associated local lax section
M^{L}_{\delta}\in\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, in the sense of
Definition

2.6
, supported at
v_{i}
and its incident edges
e_{i+1},e_{i}
, cf.
Definition

7.5
below. For that, let
p\colon\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\to\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}})
be the Grothendieck construction of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
. As in
Section

5.2.1
, we describe objects in
\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
as pairs
(v;O_{v})\in\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, where
v\in\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}})
and
O_{v}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v)
are objects. Note that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{i})=\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)\overset{\rightarrow}{\times}_{(\mathchar 45\relax)\otimes S_{p_{i}}}\mathcal{D}
if
i\in[1,m-1]
, whose elements we write as tuples as in (
12
), and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{m})=\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)
. Consider the following five partial sections of
p
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\downarrow}_{L,i}:\Delta^{0}=\{v_{i}\}\longrightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{w}),\quad\mathcal{M}^{\downarrow}_{L,i}(v_{i})\coloneqq(v_{i};k[l-1],0,0)\,,

(78)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\uparrow}_{L,i}:\Delta^{0}=\{v_{i}\}\longrightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{w}),\quad\mathcal{M}^{\uparrow}_{L,i}(v_{i})\coloneqq(v_{i};k[l],S_{p_{i}}[l],\operatorname{id}_{S_{p_{i}}[l]})\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\circlearrowright}_{L,i}:\Delta^{0}=\{v_{i}\}\longrightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{w}),\quad\mathcal{M}^{\circlearrowright}_{L,i}(v_{i})\coloneqq(v_{i};0,L,0)\,,

(79)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\circlearrowleft}_{L,i}:\Delta^{0}=\{v_{i}\}\longrightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{w}),\quad\mathcal{M}^{\circlearrowleft}_{L,i}(v_{i})\coloneqq(v_{i};\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p_{i}},L),L,\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p_{i}},L)\otimes S_{p_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{ev}}}{{\shortrightarrow}}L)\,,

(80)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\downarrow}_{L,m}:\Delta^{0}=\{v_{m}\}\longrightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{w}),\quad\mathcal{M}^{\downarrow}_{L,m}(v_{m})\coloneqq(v_{m};L)=(v_{m};k[l]).

The index
i
for the first four functors ranges in
i\in[1,m-1]
. These functors effectively assign the value of a yet to be constructed lax section at the corresponding vertex
v_{i}
or
v_{m}
, which depends on the nature of the matching segment. Intuitively, each of these can be made into a lax section of the weave schober by extending with zeros. However, for gluing an alternative extension to a lax section will be more convenient, given universally via the left Kan extension.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{L,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mbox{Lan}^{p}_{\iota}(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{L,i})}

(81)

Definition 7.5

(Local lax sections)
.

Let
\iota:\Delta^{0}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}})
denote the inclusion of a vertex
v_{i}\in{\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}
. By definition, the local lax section

M^{L}_{\delta^{\dagger}_{v_{i}}}\coloneqq\operatorname{Lan}^{p}_{\iota}(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{L,i})\in\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})

for the segment
\delta^{\dagger}_{v_{i}}
with value
L
is the
p
-relative left Kan extension
\operatorname{Lan}^{p}_{\iota}(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{L,i})
of
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{L,i}
along
\iota
.∎

In
Definition

7.5
, the superscript
\dagger
denotes
\dagger\in\{\uparrow,\downarrow,\circlearrowright,\circlearrowleft\}
and the lax sections are local in that they are supported at
v_{i}
and their adjacent edges
e_{i},e_{i+1}
, meaning that
M^{L}_{\delta^{\dagger}_{v_{i}}}(x)\simeq(x;0)
for all
x\not=v_{i},e_{i},e_{i+1}
. The diagram in
Definition

7.5
depicts such lax sections
M^{L}_{\delta^{\dagger}_{v_{i}}}
from
Definition

7.5
as relative left Kan extensions, emphasized in blue. For instance, in the case of
v_{m}
, this implies that

M_{\delta_{v_{m}}^{\downarrow}}^{L}(v_{m}\to e_{m})\colon(v_{m};k[l])\to(e_{m};S_{p_{m}}[l])

is the coCartesian edge in
\Gamma(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
encoding the application of the functor
(\mathchar 45\relax)\otimes S_{p_{m}}
. In general, we compute the values of
M^{L}_{\delta}
at the adjacent edges
e_{i+1}
and
e_{i}
by applying the functors
\varrho_{1}
, respectively
\varrho_{2}
, from
Lemma

2.9
. This leads to the following values at the adjacent edges:

M^{L}_{\delta_{v_{i}}^{\uparrow}}(e_{i+1})\simeq S_{p_{i}}[l]\,,\quad\quad\quad M^{L}_{\delta_{v_{i}}^{\uparrow}}(e_{i})\simeq 0

M^{L}_{\delta_{v_{i}}^{\downarrow}}(e_{i+1})\simeq 0\,,\quad\quad\quad M^{L}_{\delta_{v_{i}}^{\downarrow}}(e_{i})\simeq S_{p_{i}}[l]

(82)

M^{L}_{\delta_{v_{i}}^{\circlearrowright}}(e_{i+1})\simeq L\,,\quad\quad\quad M^{L}_{\delta_{v_{i}}^{\circlearrowright}}(e_{i})\simeq L

(83)

M^{L}_{\delta_{v_{i}}^{\circlearrowleft}}(e_{i+1})\simeq L\,,\quad\quad\quad M^{L}_{\delta_{v_{i}}^{\circlearrowleft}}(e_{i})\simeq T_{S_{p_{i}}}(L)\,.

(84)

Remark 7.6
.

These are the schober local models categorifying the appearances of Dehn twists for parallel transports in Picard-Lefschetz theory, which in the case of weave schobers are combinatorialized in
Section

7.3
. To wit, Equations (
83
) and (
84
) encode the fact that, for a regular fiber fixed east of the critical points, counterclockwise encircling a critical value leads to a positive twist along the associated vanishing cycle. Specifically, the parametrized schober refines this information by declaring that passing down to the right of a critical value is trivialized to be the identity, as in
Equation

83
, and passing down to left of it results in the application of the positive twist associated to its vanishing cycle, as in
Equation

84
.∎

7.1.2
Global sections from
\mathfrak{w}
-matching paths

Let us now glue the local lax sections from
Definition

7.5
to obtain a global section of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
. For that, let
\gamma
be a
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path in
\mathbb{D}
, as in
Definition

7.1
, starting at a vertex
v_{i}
and ending at a vertex
v_{i^{\prime}}
, for some
i,i^{\prime}\in[1,m]\cup\{-\infty\}
with
i^{\prime}<i
. Let us express
\gamma
as a composition of
\mathfrak{w}
-matching segments
\delta_{i},\dots,\delta_{i^{\prime}}
, cf.
Definition

7.3
, or until
\delta_{1}
if
i^{\prime}=-\infty
. We decorate each such a segment
\delta_{i}
with a local object
L_{i}
as follows:

(i)

For the initial segment
\delta_{i}=\delta_{v_{i}}^{\downarrow}
, we set
L_{i}\coloneqq k\in\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)
.

(ii)

For
j\in(i^{\prime},i)
, we recursively declare
L_{j}\coloneqq M_{\delta_{j+1}}^{L_{j+1}}(e_{j})
, using
Definition

7.5
.

(iii)

If
i^{\prime}\not=-\infty
, the condition
Definition

7.1
.(5) is equivalent to the assertion that
M_{\delta_{j+1}}^{L_{j+1}}(e_{j})\simeq S_{p}[l]
for some
l\in\mathbb{Z}
. We then declare
L_{i^{\prime}}\coloneqq k[l]\in\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)
.

In order to obtain the global section
M_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
associated to the
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path
\gamma
, we glue together the compatible family of local sections
\{M_{\delta_{j}}^{L_{j}}\}_{j\in[i^{\prime},i]}
, which is rigorously done as follows.

For an edge
e_{j}
and object
Y\in\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(e_{j})
, let
\iota_{e_{j}}(Y)\in\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
be the lax section supported at
e_{j}
with value
Y
. For all
j\in(i^{\prime},i]
, we choose morphisms

\iota_{j}(L_{j})\longrightarrow M_{\delta_{j}}^{L_{j}},M_{\delta_{j-1}}^{L_{j-1}},\quad j\in(i^{\prime},i]

(85)

in
\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
which restrict at the edge
e_{j}
to an equivalence. Let
Z_{\gamma}\subset\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}})
be the full subcategory spanned by the vertices
v_{i},v_{i+1},\dots,v_{i^{\prime}}
, omitting
v_{i^{\prime}}
if
i^{\prime}=-\infty
, and their connecting edges
e_{i},\dots,e_{i^{\prime}+1}
.

Definition 7.7

(Global sections from
\mathfrak{w}
-matching paths)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
its weave schober and
\gamma
a
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path. By definition, the global section
M_{\gamma}\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
is defined as the colimit

M_{\gamma}\coloneqq\mbox{colim}(D_{\gamma}\colon Z^{\operatorname{op}}_{\gamma}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}))

where the diagram
D_{\gamma}
is given by
D_{\gamma}(v_{j})\coloneqq M_{\delta_{j}}^{L_{j}}
and
D_{\gamma}(e_{j})\coloneqq\iota_{e_{j}}(L_{j})
, where we implicitly use the morphisms from (
85
).∎

By inspecting its construction, we deduce that
M_{\gamma}\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
in
Definition

7.7
is coCartesian and, though the diagram
D_{\gamma}
depends on the morphisms (
85
), its colimit
M_{\gamma}
is independent of these choices.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave with
m
trivalent vertices, and
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{D}
be a
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path. By definition, the object
M_{\gamma}\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
is said to be

•

a
\mathfrak{w}
-matching sphere if the endpoint
\gamma(1)=v_{i}
is a vertex
i\in[1,m]
,

•

a
\mathfrak{w}
-thimble if instead
\gamma(1)=v_{-\infty}
.∎

The notation in
Definition

7.8
is motivated by the following computation:

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{D}
be a matching path.

(1)

If
\gamma(1)=v_{i}
for some
i\geq 1
, then

\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(M_{\gamma},M_{\gamma})\simeq k\oplus k[-3]\,,

meaning that
M_{\gamma}
is a
3
-spherical object.

(2)

If
\gamma(1)=v_{-\infty}
, then

\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(M_{\gamma},M_{\gamma})\simeq k\,,

meaning that
M_{\gamma}
is exceptional.

Proof.

By
Definition

7.7
,
M_{\gamma}\simeq\displaystyle\operatorname{colim}_{Z^{\operatorname{op}}_{\gamma}}D_{\gamma}
and thus

\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(M_{\gamma},M_{\gamma})\simeq\displaystyle\lim_{x\in Z_{\gamma}}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(D_{\gamma}(x),M_{\gamma})\,.

Since
D_{\gamma}(x)
is the
p
-relative left Kan extension of its restriction to
x\in Z_{\gamma}\subset\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, it follows that

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{L}({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(D_{\gamma}(x),M_{\gamma})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(x)}(D_{\gamma}(x)({\bf x}),M_{\gamma}({\bf x}))\,

where here we have used
{\bf x}
to indicate the vertex or edge
{\bf x}\in{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
tautologically corresponding to the object
x\in Z_{\gamma}
.
By unraveling the diagrams involved, we obtain that
\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(M_{\gamma},M_{\gamma})
is the limit of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k\oplus k[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k\oplus k[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k\oplus k[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k\oplus k[-2]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

if
\gamma(1)=v_{k}
with
k\geq 1
, and otherwise, if
\gamma(1)=v_{-\infty}
, it is the limit of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k\oplus k[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k\oplus k[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k\oplus k[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k\oplus k[-2]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

In the former case, we obtain

\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(M_{\gamma},M_{\gamma})\simeq\mbox{lim}\left(\hbox to106.12pt{\vbox to49.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.05891pt\lower-24.79169pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

since both inclusions are to the first factor of

. In the latter case,

\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(M_{\gamma},M_{\gamma})\simeq\mbox{lim}\left(\hbox to106.12pt{\vbox to52.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.05891pt\lower-26.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Figure 24:
A general Demazure weave with its weave vanishing paths, as introduced in
Definition

7.10
. The left vanishing paths
\wp_{l}
are depicted in pink, and the right vanishing paths
\wp_{r}
in yellow.

7.2
Weave vanishing paths and transport

Let
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be a Demazure weave. We now introduce a certain type of
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path, as in
Definition

7.1
, which will be used to construct the exceptional collection
\mathbb{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{w}}
and its dual.

Definition 7.10

(Weave vanishing paths)
.

Let
p\in\mathfrak{w}
be a trivalent vertex. By definition:

1.

The right weave vanishing path
\wp_{r}(p)\subseteq\mathbb{D}
is the embedded path given by concatenating the straight horizontal path from
p
to the right, until
\mathfrak{w}
is entirely to the left of the path, with the straight vertical path heading downwards, as in
Figure

24
(yellow). The path
\wp_{r}
starts as in
Figure

25
.(2).

2.

The left weave vanishing path
\wp_{l}(p)\subseteq\mathbb{D}
is the embedded path given by concatenating the following four paths. First, the horizontal path from
p
to the left starting with the upwards hook, as in
Figure

25
.(1), until
\mathfrak{w}
is entirely to the right of the path. Second, the straight vertical path heading downwards, until we descend to a height where the horizontal weave slice spells
\delta(\beta)
. Third, the straight horizontal path to the right from that point, and fourth the straight vertical path heading downwards. Left weave vanishing paths are depicted as in
Figure

24
(pink).

Weave vanishing paths will typically be considered up to compactly supported planar isotopies in the complement of the trivalent vertices of the weave, and assumed to be transversal to
\mathfrak{w}
.
∎

Example 7.11
.

The
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path starting at
v_{i}
, defined as the composition of the segments
\delta_{v_{i}}^{\downarrow}
and
\delta_{v_{i-1}}^{\circlearrowright},\dots,\delta_{v_{1}}^{\circlearrowright}
, is equivalent to the right weave vanishing path
\wp_{r}(p_{i})
. Similarly, the
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path starting at
v_{i}
given by the composition of
\delta_{v_{i}}^{\downarrow}
and
\delta_{v_{i-1}}^{\circlearrowleft},\dots,\delta_{v_{1}}^{\circlearrowleft}
gives a representative for
\wp_{l}(p_{i})
, which will yield equivalent objects, up to a shift.∎

In order to illustrate the geometric idea that in this article
\mathfrak{w}
is being treated as a set of branch cuts, we introduce the following notion:

Definition 7.12

(Weave transport)
.

Let
\wp
be an oriented embedded segment in the plane transverse to
\mathfrak{w}
. By definition, the
\mathfrak{w}
-transport of an object
L\in\mathcal{D}
along
\wp
is the object

\mbox{Hol}_{\mathfrak{w}}(L;\wp)\in\mathcal{D}

(86)

obtained by starting with
L\in\mathcal{D}
and following the rules of
Figure

25
.(3)-(4) as one travels forward along
\wp
. Namely, if the path
\wp
crosses a weave line decorated by
s_{i}\in W
left-to-right, we apply the twist functor
T_{S_{i}}^{-1}
, and if
\wp
crosses a weave line decorated by
s_{i}\in W
right-to-left, we apply the inverse twist functor
T_{S_{i}}
.∎

Figure 25:
(1) The start of the path for a left vanishing path at the trivalent vertex of a weave. (2) The start of the path for a right vanishing path. (3) and (4) depict the rules for parallel transport in the presence of a weave, cf.
Definition

7.12
.

The combinatorial rules in
Figure

25
, and thus
Definition

7.12
, are consistent with the fact that counter-clockwise winding around a trivalent vertex leads to a positive twist, as in Picard-Lefschetz theory, cf.
Figure

26
.(1), Similarly, they imply that performing a zig-zaging as we cross a weave line, as in
Figure

26
.(2), leads to the same transport as the straight segment in
Figure

25
.(4). In particular, weave parallel transport is invariant under planar isotopies in the complement of the trivalent vertices of the weave, and thus paths
\wp
will typically be considered up to such isotopies, see e.g.
Definition

7.10
.

Figure 26:
(1) An illustration of the fact that
Figure

25
.(3) and (4) imply that circling counter-clockwise around a trivalent vertex decorated by
s_{i}\in W
results in a positive twist by
S_{i}
, as we have
T_{S_{i}}=T^{-1}_{S_{i}}T^{2}_{S_{i}}
. (2) A depiction exemplifying the independence of the planar isotopy representative in the complement of the trivalent vertices, as we have
T^{-1}_{S_{i}}=T^{-1}_{S_{i}}T_{S_{i}}T^{-1}_{S_{i}}
.

For instance, the spherical object
S_{p}
associated to a trivalent
s_{i}
-vertex
p\in\mathfrak{w}
in
Definition

3.1
can be understood in these terms:

S_{p}\simeq\mbox{Hol}_{\mathfrak{w}}(S_{i};\wp_{r}(p)),

(87)

i.e. the object
S_{p}\in\mathcal{D}
is the
\mathfrak{w}
-transport of
S_{i}
along the right vanishing path
\wp_{r}(p)
. The presentation in (
87
) promotes that the objects
S_{p}
are the weave analogues of vanishing cycles, which are the evaluations of the thimbles corresponding to right vanishing paths at the regular fiber at
-\infty
. The
\mathfrak{w}
-transports
\mbox{Hol}_{\mathfrak{w}}(S_{i};\wp_{l}(p))
along left vanishing paths are also of relevance, but they can be computed in terms of the objects in
(
87
)
, coming for the right vanishing paths, due to the following formula:

Lemma 7.13

(Alternative rule for transport along left weave vanishing path)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave,
p_{i}\in\mathfrak{w}
a trivalent vertex decorated by
s_{j}\in W
, and
\wp_{l}(p_{i})
its left vanishing path. Then

\operatorname{Hol}_{\mathfrak{w}}(S_{j};\wp_{l}(p_{i}))\simeq T_{S_{p_{1}}}T_{S_{p_{2}}}\cdots T_{S_{p_{i-1}}}(S_{p_{i}}).

(88)

In particular, the
\mathfrak{w}
-transport of
S_{j}
along the left vanishing path
\wp_{l}(p_{i})
can be computed from certain twists along the right vanishing spheres below of the vanishing sphere of
\wp_{r}(p_{i})
.

Proof.

Equation

88
readily holds for right-inductive weaves. Thus the statement follows from the fact that the validity of
Equation

88
is preserved under weave equivalences and weave mutations.
∎

7.3
Standard and costandard weave thimbles

In this subsection we introduce the collections of standard and costandard thimbles of a Demazure weave. They are proven to be a full exceptional collection in
Proposition

7.21
, and their behavior under weave equivalences and mutations is presented in
Lemma

7.22
.

Definition 7.14

(Weave thimbles)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave,
v_{i}\in\mathfrak{w}
a trivalent vertex and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
its weave schober. By definition:

(1)

The standard weave thimble
\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
is
\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\coloneqq M_{\wp_{r}(v_{i})}
.

(2)

The costandard weave thimble
\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
is
\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\coloneqq M_{\wp_{l}(v_{i})}
.∎

In
Definition

7.14
we are using the global sections from
Definition

7.7
, and the weave vanishing paths from
Definition

7.10
. By
Example

7.11
, if we consider the
\mathfrak{w}
-matching path
\gamma_{i}^{r}
, resp.
\gamma_{i}^{l}
, consisting of
\delta_{v_{i}}^{\downarrow}
composed with
\delta_{v_{i-1}}^{\circlearrowright},\dots,\delta_{v_{1}}^{\circlearrowright}
, resp.
\delta_{v_{i}}^{\downarrow}
composed with
\delta_{v_{i-1}}^{\circlearrowleft},\dots,\delta_{v_{1}}^{\circlearrowleft}
, the thimbles in
Definition

7.14
can be equivalently described as
\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\simeq M_{\gamma_{i}^{r}}
and
\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\simeq M_{\gamma_{i}^{l}}
.

Given a vertex
v_{i}\in{\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, we denote by
\operatorname{ev}_{v_{i}}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{i})
the evaluation functor of global sections to
v_{i}
. By
[
Chr25b
, Lem. 4.11]
, this functor admits left and right adjoints, denoted
\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{i}}
and
\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{i}}
respectively, called the induction functors. By
[
Chr25b
, Ex. 4.22]
, the weave thimbles in
Definition

7.14
can be expressed
12
12

12

The convention difference with
[
Chr25b
]
has the effect that the thimbles go down on the opposite side.
as inductions:

\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\simeq\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{i}}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{v_{i}}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{i}}((k[-1],0,0))\,

(89)

and similarly

\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\simeq\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{i}}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{v_{i}}(\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{i}}((k[-1],0,0))\,.

(90)

Remark 7.15
.

Definition

7.14
, (
89
), and (
90
) can be readily generalized to arbitrary
{\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}
-parametrized perverse schobers, by replacing the vanishing cycle
k\in\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)
with any chosen object in the
\infty
-category of vanishing cycles of the perverse schober at
v_{i}
.
∎

Figure 27:
Two 2-stranded weaves discussed in
Example

7.18
. In both cases, the standards
({\Delta_{1}},{\Delta_{2}})
and costandards
({\nabla_{1}},{\nabla_{2}})
are depicted. (Left) Left inductive weave for
\sigma_{1}^{3}
. (Right) Right inductive weave for
\sigma_{1}^{3}
.

The adjective costandard in
Definition

7.14
is justified by the following fact:

Proposition 7.16

(Duality for standard and costandard thimbles)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave.
Then the standard and costandard thimbles are dual, i.e.

\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i},\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j})\simeq\delta_{ij}\cdot k\qquad\forall i,j\in[1,m].

Proof.

If
i\not=j
, (
89
) and (
90
) imply

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i},\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{i}}((k[-1],0,0)),\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{j}}((k[-1],0,0)))

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{i})}(X,\operatorname{ev}_{v_{i}}(\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j})),&\mbox{ if }i>j\\
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{j})}(\operatorname{ev}_{v_{j}}(\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}),Y),&\mbox{ if }i<j\end{cases}

\displaystyle\simeq 0\,

where
X\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{i})
and
Y\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{j})
are the starting objects for
\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}
and
\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j}
, respectively. If
i=j
, (
89
) and (
90
) give

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{i}}((k[-1],0,0)),\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{j}}((k[-1],0,0)))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{i})}((k[-1],0,0),\operatorname{ev}_{v_{i}}(\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{j}))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{i})}((k[-1],0,0),(k[-1],0,0))

\displaystyle\simeq k\,.

∎

The duality in
Proposition

7.16
is in line with the classical duality between standard and costandard objects in the context of highest weight categories, see the proof of
[
CPS88
, Theorem 3.11]
, or
[
BLPW10
, Prop. 5.3]
.

Let
e_{i}\in{\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}}
be an edge, and
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{i}}:R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
the evaluation functor to such an edge
e_{i}
. Given any standard thimble
\Delta_{k}^{\mathfrak{w}}
, as in
Definition

7.14
.(1), we have

\operatorname{ev}_{e_{i}}(\Delta_{k}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }k<i,\\
S_{p_{k}}&\mbox{ if }i\leq k.\end{cases}

(91)

In particular, the evaluation
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}
, which we can alternatively denote
\operatorname{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}
, always yields the spherical object
\operatorname{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}(\Delta_{k}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq S_{p_{k}}
at
p_{k}
. The following lemma explains how to compute the morphism objects between standard weave thimbles in terms of their evaluations:

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave with
m
trivalent vertices. Then for all
j\in[1,m]
,
i\in[1,j)
, the functor
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{i}}
induces an equivalence

\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{p_{i}},S_{p_{j}})\,.

In addition,
\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq k
.

Proof.

By (
89
),
\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\simeq\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{i}}((k[-1],0,0))
and thus

\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{i})}((k[-1],0,0),\operatorname{ev}_{v_{i}}(\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}}))\simeq

\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{i})}((k[-1],0,0),(0,S_{p_{j}},0))\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{p_{i}},S_{p_{j}})\,.

The equivalence
\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq k
is
Lemma

7.9
.(2).
∎

Example 7.18
.

Let
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{3}
and consider the two 2-stranded weaves in
Figure

27
. The combinatorial operation of exchanging these two weaves is the most fundamental instance of a weave mutation. To ease notation, we omite the superscript
\mathfrak{w}
from the standard and costandard objects.

(1)

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be the left inductive Demazure weave for
\beta
, as in
Figure

27
(left). The evaluations at
v_{-\infty}
of the standard thimbles
({\Delta_{1}},{\Delta_{2}})
are

\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}(\Delta_{2})\simeq S_{1},\quad\quad\quad\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}(\Delta_{1})\simeq S_{1},

and those of the costandard objects
({\nabla_{1}},{\nabla_{2}})
are

\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}(\nabla_{2})\simeq S_{1}[-1]\,,\quad\quad\quad\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}(\nabla_{1})\simeq S_{1}\,.

The weave transport along the right and left vanishing paths
\wp_{r}
and
\wp_{l}
are illustrated in
Figure

27
, with
\wp_{r}
depicted in dashed yellow lines, and
\wp_{l}
in dashed orange lines. By
Lemma

7.17
, these evaluations allow us to compute the relevant morphism groups:

\operatorname{Mor}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\Delta_{1}}),\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\Delta_{2}}))\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{1},S_{1})\simeq k\oplus k[-2],

\operatorname{Mor}(\nabla_{2},\nabla_{1})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\nabla_{2}}),\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\nabla_{1}}))\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{1}[-1],S_{1})\simeq k[1]\oplus k[-1].

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be the right inductive Demazure weave for
\beta
, as in
Figure

27
(right). In this case, see e.g. (
91
), the evaluations at
v_{-\infty}
of the standard objects
({\Delta_{1}},{\Delta_{2}})
are

\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}(\Delta_{2})\simeq S_{1}[1],\quad\quad\quad\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}(\Delta_{1})\simeq S_{1}.

The costandard objects
({\nabla_{1}},{\nabla_{2}})
evaluate to

\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}(\nabla_{2})\simeq S_{1}\,,\quad\quad\quad\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}(\nabla_{1})\simeq S_{1}\,.

Lemma

7.17
implies the following computation for the morphism groups:

\operatorname{Mor}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\Delta_{1}}),\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\Delta_{2}}))\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{1},S_{1}[1])\simeq k[1]\oplus k[-1],

\operatorname{Mor}(\nabla_{2},\nabla_{1})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\nabla_{2}}),\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\nabla_{1}}))\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{1},S_{1})\simeq k\oplus k[-2]\,.

In both instances (1) and (2) in this example the duality from
Proposition

7.16
is readily verified.
∎

Figure 28:
The weave
\mathfrak{w}
from
Example

3.4
and
Example

7.19
, cf. also
Figure

12
. The associated standard objects
(\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{5})
, in yellow, and the costandard objects
(\nabla_{1},\ldots,\nabla_{5})
, in pink, constructed using
Section

7.1
. The dashed black arrow on top indicates the direction of positive wrapping, which is coutner-clockwise. To ease notation, we have omitted the small hook at the beginning of the costandard objects according to
Figure

25
.(1).

Example 7.19
.

The standards and costandards for the weave in
Example

3.4
are depicted in
Figure

28
. The spherical simples
S_{p_{i}}
are computed in
Example

3.4
. In conjunction with (
7.17
) and
Lemma

7.17
, this yields the morphism groups
\operatorname{Mor}(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})
between the standard thimbles. For instance,

\operatorname{Mor}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\Delta_{1}}),\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\Delta_{2}}))\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{1},S_{2})\simeq k[-1],

\operatorname{Mor}(\Delta_{3},\Delta_{5})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\Delta_{3}}),\mbox{ev}_{v_{-\infty}}({\Delta_{5}}))\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(T_{S_{2}}^{-1}(S_{1}[1]),S_{1}[1])\simeq k.

∎

Remark 7.20
.

Using either
Definition

7.14
, or (
89
) and (
90
), a computation shows that the standard weave thimbles
(\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{1},\dots,\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{m})
recover the costandard weave thimbles
(\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{1},\dots,\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{m})
via

\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{l}\simeq T_{\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}}^{+}T_{\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}}^{+}\cdots T_{\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{l-1}}^{+}(\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{l})\,.

Conversely, we also have

\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{l}\simeq T_{\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}}^{-}T_{\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}}^{-}\cdots T_{\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{l-1}}^{-}(\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{l})\,,

(92)

see also
Remark

A.5
and
[
Sei08
, Section 5k]
.
∎

7.3.1
Exceptionality of weave thimbles and behaviour under weave operations

Let us show that the standard weave thimbles form a full exceptional collection in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
:

Proposition 7.21

(Exceptionality of weave thimbles)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave.

(1)

The standard thimbles
(\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{1},\dots,\Delta^{\mathfrak{w}}_{m})
form a full exceptional collection in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
.

(2)

The costandard thimbles
(\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{1},\dots,\nabla^{\mathfrak{w}}_{m})
form a full coexceptional collection in
R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
.

Proof.

Let us denote
\Delta_{i}\coloneqq\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
, as
\mathfrak{w}
is fixed throughout the argument. For Part (1), (
89
) implies

\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta_{i},X)\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}(v_{i})}(\Delta_{i}(v_{i}),X(v_{i}))\,,\quad\forall X\in R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}).

(93)

By considering
X=\Delta_{j}
with
j\in[1,i)
, the vanishing
\Delta_{j}(v_{i})\simeq 0
and (
93
) imply
\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j})\simeq 0
. This shows the required semi-orthogonality. In conjunction with
Lemma

7.17
we obtain that
(\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{m})
is an exceptional collection.

Let us now show that this exceptional collection is full by an induction on the vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
. For that, let
\mathfrak{w}_{\leq m-1}\subset\mathfrak{w}
be the subweave of
\mathfrak{w}
containing the
m-1
first trivalent vertices
p_{1},\dots,p_{m-1}
. Then the subcategory

R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}_{\leq m-1}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}_{\leq m-1}})\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{ev}_{v_{m}})\subset R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})

is equivalent to the full subcategory of global setions which vanish at
v_{m}
, and hence also at
e_{m}
. The induction hypothesis is that
(\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{m-1})
is full. Suppose now that
\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta_{i},X)\simeq 0
for all
i\in[1,m]
: to establish
(\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{m})
is full, we must show that
X\simeq 0
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}\leq n}(v_{m})\simeq\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)
and
\Delta_{m}(v_{m})\simeq k
, the equivalence (
89
) implies

0\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\Delta_{m},X)\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k)}(k,X(v_{m}))\simeq X(v_{m})

and therefore
X\in R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}_{\leq m-1}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}_{\leq m-1}})
. Since
(\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{m-1})
is full, by the induction hypothesis, we conclude find
X\simeq 0
, as required. Part (2) follows from Part (1) applied to the opposite
\infty
-category.
∎

We conclude by recording how weave thimbles behave under weave equivalence and weave mutation. By
Theorem

3.8
, the weave schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
remains invariant under weave equivalences except for the case of a pulley move. Specifically, for a forward pulley move or a forward weave mutation from
\mathfrak{w}
to
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
, the weave schober changes according to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}\mapsto\mu_{i}^{\#}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
for some
i\in[1,m]
, which itself induces an equivalence

\mu_{i}^{\#}:R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\longrightarrow R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}).

Similarly, for a backward pulley move or backward weave mutation from
\mathfrak{w}
to
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
, we have an equivalence

\mu_{i}^{\flat}:R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})\longrightarrow R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}).

Therefore, by construction, the possible changes of weave thimbles under weave equivalences and weave mutations are the perverse schober analogues of Hurwitz moves for distinguished bases, see e.g.
[
Sei08
, Section (16d)]
. Thus, if we have correctly developed the theory of weave schobers, one expects weave thimbles to either remain invariant or change by mutation of exceptional collections. This is indeed the case:

Lemma 7.22

(Weave thimbles under weave equivalence and mutation)
.

In the notation above:

(1)

Let
\mathfrak{w}\simeq\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be a height-preserving weave equivalence. Then

\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\simeq\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\simeq\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\quad\forall i\in[1,m].

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{w}\to\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be a forward pulley move or a forward weave mutation, exchanging the heights of vertices
p_{i-1}
and
p_{i}
. Then
(\Delta_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\ldots,\Delta_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}})
is the image under
\mu_{i}^{\sharp}
of the forward mutation of
(\Delta_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\ldots,\Delta_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}})
at
(\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})
, i.e. the objects

(\Delta_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\dots,\Delta_{i-2}^{\mathfrak{w}},T_{\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}}}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}),\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{i+1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\dots,\Delta_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}})\,,

where
T_{\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}}}^{+}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\coloneqq\operatorname{cof}(\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\otimes\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}}\longrightarrow\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})
.
Similarly, the co-exceptional collection
(\nabla_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\dots,\nabla_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}})
coincides with the image under
\mu_{i}^{\sharp}
of the forward mutation of
(\nabla_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\dots,\nabla_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}})
at
(\nabla_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})
.

(3)

Let
\mathfrak{w}\to\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
be a backward pulley move or a backward weave mutation, exchanging the heights of vertices
p_{i-1}
and
p_{i}
. Then
(\Delta_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\ldots,\Delta_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}})
is the image under
\mu_{i}^{\flat}
of the backward mutaion of
(\Delta_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\ldots,\Delta_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}})
at
(\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})
, i.e. the objects

(\Delta_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\dots,\Delta_{i-2}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},T_{\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}}^{-}(\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}}),\Delta_{i+1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\dots,\Delta_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}})\,,

where
T_{\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}}^{-}(\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}})\coloneqq\operatorname{fib}(\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})^{*}\otimes\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})
. Similarly, the co-exceptional collection
(\nabla_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\dots,\nabla_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}})
coincides with the image under
\mu_{i}^{\flat}
of the backward mutation of
\nabla_{1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\dots,\nabla_{m}^{\mathfrak{w}}
at
(\nabla_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}},\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})
.

Proof.

Part (1) follows directly from
Theorem

3.8
.(1), since
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
in this case. Let us prove Part (2). For
j\in[1,m]
with
j\not\in\{i-1,i\}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma({\bf G_{\mathfrak{w}}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{i}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{v_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{v_{j}}}

commutes. It follows from (
89
) that

\mu_{i}^{\sharp}(\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq\mu_{i}^{\sharp}(\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{j}}((k,0,0)))\simeq\Delta_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\,,\quad\mbox{ if }j\not\in\{i-1,i\}.

The equivalences
\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\simeq\mu_{i}^{\sharp}(T_{\Delta_{i-1}}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}))
and
\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\simeq\mu_{i}^{\sharp}(\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}})
follow from an explicit computation of coCartesian sections, by tracing through the zig-zag of contractions from (
8
) and using the equivalences of global sections from
Proposition

2.8
. Let us illustrate part of such a computation, as follows.

Let us suppose that
i=m
is the top vertex, which allows us to omit the edge
e_{i+1}
connecting to
v_{i}
. The computation of the equivalence
\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\simeq\mu_{i}^{\sharp}(\Delta_{i-1}^{\mathfrak{w}})
amounts to identifying the coCartesian sections under the four equivalences of global sections from the contractions in (
8
). We describe below the images under the first two of these equivalences. For the first one
\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F})
, we use the perverse schober on the left side of (
17
). Similarly, for the second one
(\pi_{2})_{*}\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F})
, we use the perverse schober on the left of (
18
). Let us denote the non-singular central vertex in the top middle-left ribbon graph in (
8
) by
\tilde{v}
. We have
\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F})(\tilde{v})\simeq\operatorname{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{D})
, and thus we can denote its objects by
(\tilde{v};a\to b)
with
a\to b
a morphism in
\mathcal{D}
. Pull-push yields the following coCartesian sections, depicted here partially near
v_{i-1}
:

{{{{{}}{
