By definition, we also set
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}
.∎

Let us now show that the
\infty
-category of downward global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
and the
\infty
-category of upward global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})
both embed into the two-sided global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})
. In fact, these two full subcategories of
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})
differ by a mutation equivalence of semiorthogonal decompositions, in the sense of
[
DKSS21
, Def. 2.4.1]
and are thus equivalent as
\infty
-categories. The precise statement we prove reads as follows:

Lemma 9.2
.

(1)

The
\infty
-category of global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
is equivalent to the full subcategory of
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})
consisting of global sections whose image under
\operatorname{ev}_{\infty}
vanishes. The arising inclusion

\iota^{\downarrow}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})\longrightarrow R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})

admits both left and right adjoints
(\iota^{\downarrow})^{L},(\iota^{\downarrow})^{R}
.

(2)

The
\infty
-category of global sections
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})
is equivalent to the full subcategory of
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})
consisting of global sections whose image under
\operatorname{ev}_{1}
vanishes. The arising inclusion

\iota^{\uparrow}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})\longrightarrow R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})

admits both left and right adjoints
(\iota^{\uparrow})^{L},(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}
.

(3)

The four composite functors

(\iota^{\downarrow})^{\bullet}\circ\iota^{\uparrow}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})\longrightarrow R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad(\iota^{\uparrow})^{\bullet}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})\longrightarrow R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})

are equivalences of
\infty
-categories, where
\bullet\in\{L,R\}
.

Proof.

For Parts (1) & (2),
[
Chr25b
, Theorem 4.6]
implies that the two evaluation functors

\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{1})\simeq\mathcal{D}\,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{\infty})\simeq\mathcal{D}

define a perverse schober parametrized by the
2
-spider. Therefore, they each admit left and right adjoints which are fully faithful. In order to show that
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}})
is equivalent to the full subcategory of global sections whose image under
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}
vanishes, it suffices to note that the passage to the fiber commutes with the passage to global sections, as limits commute with limits, and that the fiber of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(v_{m})\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{\infty})
is equivalent to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow}(v_{m})
. The latter follows from
[
Chr22b
, Lemma 4.26]
. This concludes Part (1). A similar argument shows that
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}})
, thus concluding the proof of Part (2).

Part (3) can be proven as follows. By using the existence of the right adjoints of
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}},\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}
, from Parts (1) and (2),
[
DKSS21
, Prop. 2.3.2]
implies that
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})
admits two semiorthogonal decompositions

(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}^{R}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{1})),\iota^{\uparrow}(R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})))\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad(\iota^{\downarrow}(R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})),\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}^{R}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{1}))\,.

The two essential images
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}^{R}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{1}),\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}^{R}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{1})
agree by the definition of perverse schober on the
2
-spider. Since the functors
(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}
and
(\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\uparrow}
describe the corresponding mutation equivalences, cf.
[
DKSS21
, Def. 2.4.1 & Prop. 2.4.2]
, they are therefore equivalences. Similarly, by using left adjoints (instead of right adjoints as above), the corresponding argument shows that the functors
(\iota^{\downarrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\uparrow},(\iota^{\uparrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}
are also equivalences.
∎

The
\infty
-categories and functors discussed in
Lemma

9.2
are depicted in Diagram (
154
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{\infty})\simeq\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}})\simeq R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\uparrow})^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\downarrow})^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\uparrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\uparrow}}

(154)

Lemma 9.3
.

The functor
(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}},\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}})\colon R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})\to\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}
admits a relative weak right
3
-Calabi–Yau structure.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of
Proposition

3.6
.
∎

Let us denote
\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\downarrow}\coloneqq\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
and
\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\downarrow}\coloneqq\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}
for the downward facing weaves thimbles in
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
, as introduced in
Definition

7.14
. See also Equations (
89
) and (
90
) for their descriptions in terms of inductions from
v_{i}
. The corresponding upwards weave thimbles can be defined directly via induction from
v_{i}
, as follows:

Definition 9.4
.

(1)

The upwards standard thimble
\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}\in R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})
is defined as

\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}\coloneqq\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{i}}((k,S_{p_{i}},\operatorname{id}_{S_{p_{i}}}))\,.

(155)

(2)

The upwards costandard thimble
\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}\in R\Gamma({\bf G}_{w}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})
is defined as

\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}\coloneqq\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{i}}((k,S_{p_{i}},\operatorname{id}_{S_{p_{i}}}))\,.

∎

The following result shows that the equivalences
(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}
and
(\iota^{\uparrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}
in
Lemma

9.2
can be understood as a type of Ringel duality in the context of perverse schobers, exchanging downward standard and upward costandard thimbles, up to a shift.

Proposition 9.5

(Ringel-type duality)
.

There exist equivalences in
R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})

((\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\downarrow})\simeq\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}[-1]

(156)

and

((\iota^{\uparrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\downarrow})\simeq\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}[2]\,.

(157)

Proof.

Let us prove equivalence (
156
). Denote by

T^{\operatorname{gl}}\coloneqq\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{id}_{R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}^{\updownarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}})}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{unit}}(\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{e_{\infty}},\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{e_{1}})\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}},\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}))

the cotwist functor
T^{\operatorname{gl}}:R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}^{\updownarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}})\longrightarrow R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}^{\updownarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}})
of the spherical adjunction

(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}},\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}})\colon R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}^{\updownarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}})\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\updownarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}}(e_{\infty})\times\mathcal{F}^{\updownarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}}(e_{1})\penalty\mspace{6muplus1mu}{:}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu\mathpunct{}\mspace{2mu}(\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{e_{\infty}},\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{e_{1}}).

Similarly, for each
i\in[1,m]
, denote by
T_{v_{i}}
the cotwist functor of the spherical adjunction

(\varrho_{2},\varrho_{1})=(\mathcal{F}(v_{i}\to e_{i}),\mathcal{F}(v_{i}\to e_{i+1}))\colon\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(v_{i})\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{i})\times\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}(e_{i+1})\penalty\mspace{6muplus1mu}{:}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu\mathpunct{}\mspace{2mu}(\mathcal{F}(v_{i}\to e_{i})^{R},\mathcal{F}(v_{i}\to e_{i+1})^{R})\,.

For these functors,
[
Chr25b
, Prop. 4.23.(2)]
implies that

T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{i}}\\
\simeq\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{i}}\circ T_{v_{i}}.

(158)

Now, a direct computation shows that

T_{v_{i}}((k[-1],0,0))\simeq(k[-1],S_{i}[-1],\operatorname{id}_{S_{i}[-1]})\,.

(159)

Therefore, we conclude the equivalences:

\displaystyle(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:thimble_as_induction}}}{{\simeq}}(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{v_{i}})((k[-1],0,0))

(160)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:TindL_is_indTv}}}{{\simeq}}\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{i}}\circ T_{v_{i}}((k[-1],0,0))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ringel_proof_Tvi_vertex}}}{{\simeq}}\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{i}}((k[-1],S_{i}[-1],\operatorname{id}_{S_{i}[-1]}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:upward_cothimble}}}{{\simeq}}\iota^{\uparrow}(\nabla_{i}^{\uparrow})[-1]\,.

Let us compute
(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})
differently, as follows. Since we have
(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}},\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\simeq(0,S_{i})
, we deduce the equivalence

(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\iota^{\downarrow}(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\to\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{e_{1}}(S_{i}))\,.

(161)

Since
(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\in\operatorname{Im}(\iota^{\uparrow})
, by the fully faithfulness of
\iota^{\uparrow}
, we obtain

(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\simeq(\iota^{\uparrow}\circ(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R})(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\,.

(162)

Now, for any upward global section
X\in R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}})
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(X,(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}(\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{e_{1}}(S_{i})))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\updownarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}^{\updownarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}})}(\iota^{\uparrow}(X),\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{e_{1}}(S_{i}))

(163)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}(e_{1})}(\underbrace{\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\iota^{\uparrow}(X))}_{\simeq 0},S_{i})\simeq 0,

where the first equivalence is the adjunction
\iota^{R}\dashv(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}
, and
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\iota^{\uparrow}(X))\simeq 0
follows from
Lemma

9.2
.(2).
The vanishing (
163
) for all
X\in R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}})
implies that
(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}(\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{e_{1}}(S_{i}))\simeq 0
. Therefore (
161
) yields

(\iota^{\uparrow}\circ(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R})(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\simeq(\iota^{\uparrow}\circ(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta^{\downarrow}_{i})\,.

(164)

Now, combining the above equivalences yields

(\iota^{\uparrow}\circ(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ringel_proof_eq2}}}{{\simeq}}(\iota^{\uparrow}\circ(\iota^{\uparrow})^{R})(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ringel_proof_eq1}}}{{\simeq}}(T^{\operatorname{gl}}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Tgldown_computation1}}}{{\simeq}}\iota^{\uparrow}(\nabla_{i}^{\uparrow})[-1].

(165)

Since the functor
\iota^{\uparrow}
is fully faithful, (
165
) implies the equivalence (
156
), as required. The proof of equivalence (
157
) is analogous, using

T_{v_{i}}((k[2],S_{i}[2],\operatorname{id}_{S_{i}[2]}))\simeq(k[-1],0,0)
instead of (
159
).
∎

Due to
Lemma

9.2
.(3) and Proposition
9.5
, we often refer to either of the four functors
(\iota^{\downarrow})^{\bullet}\circ\iota^{\uparrow}
and
(\iota^{\uparrow})^{\bullet}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}
as dualities between the
\infty
-categories
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
and
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})
,
\bullet\in\{L,R\}
.

Remark 9.6
.

The autoequivalence

(\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\uparrow}\circ(\iota^{\uparrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\downarrow}\colon R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})\to R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})

describes the inverse Serre functor of the relative weakly left
3
-Calabi–Yau category
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
, cf.
Proposition

3.6
. This aligns with the fact that the difference of the shifts
[-1]
and
[2]
appearing in
Proposition

9.5
is indeed
-3
.∎

9.2
Silting collections in
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
and
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})

Let us now introduce the silting collections associated to the thimbles. There are four of them, as we have standard and costandard, and upwards and downwards, thimbles.

Definition 9.7

(Silting collections from thimbles)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave with
m
trivalent vertices. For all
i\in[1,m]
, we define

\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}\coloneqq T^{-,>0}_{\Delta_{m}^{\downarrow,\mathfrak{w}}}(\dots(T^{-,>0}_{\Delta_{i+1}^{\mathfrak{w},\downarrow}}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\downarrow})))\in R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\downarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})

\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{i}\coloneqq T^{+,<0}_{\nabla_{m}^{\downarrow,\mathfrak{w}}}(\dots(T^{+,<0}_{\nabla_{i+1}^{\mathfrak{w},\downarrow}}(\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\downarrow})))\in R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\downarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})

and

\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}\coloneqq T^{-,>0}_{\nabla_{m}^{\uparrow,\mathfrak{w}}}(\dots(T^{-,>0}_{\nabla_{i+1}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}}(\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow})))\in R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})

\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{i}\coloneqq T^{+,<0}_{\Delta_{m}^{\uparrow,\mathfrak{w}}}(\dots(T^{+,<0}_{\Delta_{i+1}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}}(\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow})))\in R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})

∎

Proposition

A.13
implies that
\{\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}\}
and
\{\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{i}\}
in
Definition

9.7
form silting collections in
R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\downarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
. In terms of the comparison to highest weight theory, cf.
Section

A.3

,
\{\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}\}
and
\{\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{i}\}
are a stable
\infty
-categorical analogue of the projective and injective objects, respectively.

Remark 9.8
.

(1) We refer to the objects
\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{k}\in R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}})
in
Definition

9.7
as the dual categorical Lusztig cycles of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
, for any
i,k\in[1,m]
. Specifically
\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}
is a left dual categorical Lusztig cycles, and
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{i}
a right dual categorical Lusztig cycles. Indeed, they are, respectively, left and right dual to the simple-minded collection given by the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{\bm{L}^{\mathfrak{w}}_{i}\}
.

(2) The analogs of
Theorem

4.5
and
Theorem

4.8
hold for these silting collections
\{\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}\}
and
\{\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{i}\}
in
R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\downarrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
. Namely, they are invariant under weave equivalences, and they undergo a mutation of silting collections under a weave mutation. This is a direct consequence of Koszul duality.∎

These collections of upwards and downwards silting objects in
Definition

9.7
are related via the the Ringel-type duality from
Lemma

9.2
as follows:

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave with
m
trivalent vertices. Then, for all
i\in[1,m]
, there exist equivalences in
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})

((\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\uparrow})(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i})[-1]\simeq\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}

(166)

and

((\iota^{\downarrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\uparrow})(\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{i})[2]\simeq\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{i}\,.

(167)

Proof.

These equivalences are implied by
Proposition

9.5
.
∎

Combining
Lemma

9.9
and
Proposition

A.13
, we find that
\{\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{i}\}
and
\{\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}\}
define silting collections in
R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})
.

In a highest weight abelian category, the tilting objects each admit, by definition, both a standard and a costandard flag. The following result gives an analogue of such a property for the stable
\infty
-category
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})
, in which the upwards thimbles form a full exceptional collection. We leave open the interesting question whether this property characterizes the upwards silting collection.

Lemma 9.10
.

There exists an equivalence of global sections

\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{i}\,.

Proof.

First, since
\{\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{j}\}
are right duals to
\{{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\}
, we obtain

\operatorname{Mor}({\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{j}[2])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Ringeldual_silting2}}}{{\simeq}}\operatorname{Mor}({\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},((\iota^{\uparrow})^{R}\circ\iota^{\downarrow})(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{j})))\simeq\operatorname{Mor}({\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{j})\simeq\delta_{ij}\cdot k\,.

(168)

Second, by using the relative right
3
-Calabi–Yau structure from
Lemma

9.3
and
[
Chr22a
, Prop. 5.19]
, we also have

\operatorname{Mor}({\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}},\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{j}[2])\simeq\operatorname{Mor}(\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{j}[2],{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})^{*}[-3].

(169)

Equivalences (
168
) and (
169
) thus imply

\operatorname{Mor}(\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{j}[-1],{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq\delta_{i,j}k\,.

(170)

Therefore we have the equivalences

\operatorname{Mor}(((\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\uparrow})(\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{j}[-1]),{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\simeq\operatorname{Mor}(\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{j}[-1],{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:proof_lemma_doublefiltration3}}}{{\simeq}}\delta_{ij}\cdot k\,,

which show that the collection of objects
\{((\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\uparrow})(\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{j}[-1])\}
is Koszul dual to the categorical Lusztig cycles
\{{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\}
. In consequence,

((\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\uparrow})(\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{i}[-1])\simeq\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Ringeldual_silting1}}}{{\simeq}}((\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\uparrow})(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}[-1])\,,

which implies that
\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{i}\simeq\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}
, as required.
∎

Remark 9.11
.

The upwards silting collection
\{\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}\}
is dual to the categorical Lusztig cycles as follows:

\operatorname{Mor}(\iota^{\uparrow}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}),\iota^{\downarrow}({\bm{L}}_{j}^{\mathfrak{w}}))\simeq\delta_{ij}\cdot k[-1]\,

This is implied by
Lemma

9.9
and
Theorem

A.21
by using the adjunctions.∎

9.3
Weave realization of dual categorical Lusztig cycles

The goal of this subsection is to describe the objects in the silting collections
\{\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}\}
and
\{\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\downarrow}_{i}\}
associated to
\{\Delta_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\}
in terms of the Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
itself. By
Lemma

9.9
and
Lemma

9.10
, we can equivalently aim at describing the upward objects
\{\mathbb{P}^{\uparrow}_{i}\}
in terms of
\mathfrak{w}
. This is achieved in
Theorem

9.13
and
Corollary

9.14
, cf. also
9.15
.

Definition 9.12

(Weave cycles realization)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave and
X\in R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})
a global section. By definition,
X
is said to be realized by a weave cycle
\vartheta:E(\mathfrak{w})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
if:

1.

\vartheta
satisfies the tropical Lusztig propagation rules at the
4
and
6
-valent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
.

2.

For any point
q\in e_{i}
in the edge
e_{i}\in{\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow}
, the object
X(e_{i})
admits a
(\beta_{q},{\bf a}_{q})
-filtration, where
(\beta_{q},{\bf a}_{q})
is the weighted braid word associated to
\vartheta
at the horizontal slice of
\mathfrak{w}
at the height of
q
.

By definition, if
X
lies in
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})
or
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
, a weave cycle realization of
X
consists of a weave cycle realization of its image in
R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\updownarrow})
.∎

A motivating instance for
Definition

9.12
is that the categorical Lusztig cycle
{\bm{L}}_{i}^{\mathfrak{w}}\in R\Gamma({\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\downarrow})
is realized by the Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{i}:E(\mathfrak{w})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
from
Definition

2.1
, as is implied by our constructions in
Sections

4
and
5
. In general, the downwards facing global section
\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}
of the dual silting collection might not admit a weave cycle realization. Nevertheless, we will now establish that there is a natural weave cycle realization of its upwards dual
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}
:

Theorem 9.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave with
m
trivalent vertices. Then, for any
i\in[1,m]
:

(i)

\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}\in R\Gamma({\bf G}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow})
admits a realization by a weave cycle.

(ii)

For any edge
e_{j}
of
{\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}^{\uparrow}
, the object
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}(e_{j})\in\mathcal{D}
is rigid.

Proof.

Let us denote

\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}\coloneqq T^{-,>0}_{\nabla^{\uparrow,\mathfrak{w}}_{j}}(\cdots(T^{-,>0}_{\nabla^{\uparrow,\mathfrak{w}}_{i+1}}(\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}))),\qquad i\in[1,m],\quad j\in[j,m].

(171)

Note that
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i,i}=\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}
and
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{m,i}=\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}
, and that (
171
) is in line with (
94
) and (
95
). Let us fix the index
i\in[1,m]
, we will prove both
(i)
and
(ii)
by an iterative argument, scanning the weave
\mathfrak{w}
from bottom to top. Specifically, for each
j\in[i,m]
, we will show that
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j+1}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i})\in\mathcal{D}
is a rigid object, and admits a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration, where the braid word
\beta
is given by a horizontal slice of
\mathfrak{w}
intersecting the edge
e_{j+1}
and the weights
{\bf a}
will be uniquely determined, cf.
Lemma

5.30
. Note that
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j+1}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i})\simeq\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j+1}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i})\in\mathcal{D}
, as follows from (
171
) and
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j+1}}(\nabla_{l}^{\uparrow,\mathfrak{w}})\simeq 0
for all
l\geq j+1
. Recall that we denote
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1})\coloneqq\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j+1}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i})
.

The iterative argument starts at
j=i
. At the edge
e_{i+1}
, we have
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i,i}(e_{i+1})\simeq S_{p_{i}}
. The object
S_{p_{i}}
admits a filtration for the weighted braid word arising from the slice above
p_{i}
with a unique non-zero weight
1
concentrated on the strand exiting
p_{i}
in the north-east direction.

For the iterative step, consider
j\in[i,m)
and suppose that
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1})
is rigid and it admits a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration as above. First, we have the following equivalences

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mor}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i},\nabla_{j+1}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow})^{*}

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{F}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{w}}(v_{j+1})}((0,\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1}),0),(k,S_{p_{j+1}},\operatorname{id}_{S_{p_{j+1}}}))^{*}

(172)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1}),S_{p_{j+1}})^{*}

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{p_{j+1}},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1}))[2]\,.

In (
172
), the first equivalence follows from the adjunction
\operatorname{ev}_{v_{j+1}}\dashv\operatorname{ind}^{R}_{v_{j+1}}
, the second is a direct computation, and the third follows from the fact that
\mathcal{D}
is
2
-Calabi–Yau. Second, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j+2}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j+1,i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:partial_dualsiltings}}}{{\simeq}}\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j+2}}(T^{-,>0}_{\nabla_{j+1}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}))

(173)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:weaverealization_proof_equiv1}}}{{\simeq}}\operatorname{ev}_{e_{j+2}}(\operatorname{fib}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}\to\tau_{>0}\left(\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{p_{j+1}},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1}))[2]\right)\otimes\nabla^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}_{j+1}))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+2})\to\tau_{>0}\left(\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(S_{p_{j+1}},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1}))[2]\right)\otimes S_{p_{j+1}})

\displaystyle\simeq T^{-,>0}_{S_{p_{j+1}}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+2}))

\displaystyle\simeq T^{-,>0}_{S_{p_{j+1}}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1}))\,,

where the last equivalence follows from
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1})\simeq\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+2})
. Since
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1})
is rigid,
Lemma

5.6
therefore implies that
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j+1,i}(e_{j+2})
is also rigid. By iterating the argument, this establishes
(i)
in the statement.

Let us now focus on
(ii)
. For the iterative step at
j\in[i,m)
, we consider the trivalent vertex
p\coloneqq p_{j+1}
of
\mathfrak{w}
and assume that the south edge of
p_{j+1}
corresponds to the
b
-th crossing of the braid word
\beta
(i.e. the
b
-th labeled strand in the horizontal slice of the weave). Here
\beta
is the braid word arising from the horizontal slice of
\mathfrak{w}
right below
p_{j+1}
. By the iterative hypothesis,
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1})
admits a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration, and we let
\mathcal{C}_{\ast}
be associated coherent chain complex such that its geometric realization

\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast})\simeq\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1})

(174)

corresponds to such
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration, cf.
Section

5.3.4
.(B). Our task is to show that

\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j+1,i}(e_{j+2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:weaverealization_proof_equiv2}}}{{\simeq}}T^{-,>0}_{\nabla_{j+1}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:weaverealization_proof_equiv3}}}{{\simeq}}T^{-,>0}_{\nabla_{j+1}^{\mathfrak{w},\uparrow}}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast}))\simeq T^{-,>0}_{S{p_{j+1}}}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast}))

also admits such a filtration, now corresponding to a weighted braid word arising as a horizontal slice of
\mathfrak{w}
right above
p
with an appropriate choice of weights. In order to compute
T^{-,>0}_{S{p_{j+1}}}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast}))
, we first consider the following morphism between two
3
-term coherent chain complexes in
\mathcal{D}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}S_{p}^{\oplus a_{b}}[-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{p}\otimes{\tau_{>0}(\operatorname{Mor}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}),S_{p})^{*})[-2]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{p}^{\oplus a_{b}}[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{p}\otimes\tau_{>0}\left(\operatorname{Mor}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b}),S_{p})^{*}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{coev}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{coev}}

(175)

where the left and right vertical morphisms in (
175
) are the canonical coevaluations maps, and the middle vertical morphism
\alpha
is of maximal rank. By (
174
), the top coherent complex
\mathcal{C}_{1}
of (
175
), highlighted in orange, has totalization
\operatorname{tot}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathcal{C}_{1}})\simeq\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1})
. Similarly, the totalization of the bottom coherent complex
\mathcal{C}_{2}
of (
175
), highlighted in blue, has totalization
\operatorname{tot}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{C}_{2}})\simeq\tau_{>0}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1}),S_{p})^{*}\otimes S_{p}
. The totalization of the (vertical) morphism
f:{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathcal{C}_{1}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{C}_{2}}
in (
175
) is the natural coevaluation morphism. Therefore, to compute
T^{-,>0}_{S{p_{j+1}}}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast}))
we must compute the fiber coherent complex
\operatorname{fib}(f)
and then its totalization.

Instead of directly computing the fiber coherent chain complex, we first fold the left square in (
175
), yielding the following morphism
g
of coherent
3
-term chain complexes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})[-2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{p}\otimes{\tau_{>0}(\operatorname{Mor}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}),S_{p})^{*})[-2]}}\oplus{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}S_{p}^{\oplus a_{b}}[-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{p}^{\oplus a_{b}}[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{p}\otimes\tau_{>0}\left(\operatorname{Mor}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b}),S_{p})^{*}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{coev}}

(176)

Note that
\operatorname{tot}(\operatorname{fib}(f))\simeq\operatorname{tot}(\operatorname{fib}(g))
, since folding preserves the totalization.

Let us compute the coherent complex
\operatorname{fib}(g)
. In (
176
),
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b})
describes the part of
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1})
arising from the weights of
\beta
to the left of the
b
-th crossing of
\beta
, i.e. to the left of the
b
-th weave edge for that horizontal slice. The term
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})
similarly describes the piece of
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1})
arising from the weights to the right of the
b
-th crossing of
\beta
. Therefore,
Lemma

5.4
implies that the truncated morphism object

\tau_{>0}\left(\operatorname{Mor}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b}),S_{p})^{*}\right)\simeq\tau_{>0}\left(\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b}))[2]\right)\simeq\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b}))[2]

(177)

is equivalent to the entire morphism object, and that

\tau_{>0}(\operatorname{Mor}(\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}),S_{p})^{*})\simeq\tau_{>0}\operatorname{Mor}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}))[2]\simeq\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}))[1]

(178)

is concentrated in degree
1
.

Passing to the fiber coherent
3
-term chain complex of
g
, we thus obtain the coherent
3
-term chain complex
\operatorname{fib}(g)

\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})[-2]\to\operatorname{cof}\left(S_{p}^{\oplus a_{b}+y}[-2]\to S_{p}^{\oplus a_{b}}\right)\to\operatorname{tot}(T^{-}_{S_{p}}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b}))\,,

(179)

where we have denoted

\framebox{$y\coloneqq\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})).$}\,

(180)

By grafting (
175
) at its middle term, the totalization of
\operatorname{fib}(g)
is equivalent to the totalization of a coherent chain complex of the following form

\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})[-3]\to S_{p}^{\oplus a_{b}+y}[-2]\to S_{p}^{\oplus a_{b}}\to\operatorname{tot}(T^{-}_{S_{p}}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast<b}))\,.

(181)

Now, the coherent complex (
181
) yields an associated
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
-filtration for its totalization, as in
Section

5.3.4
.(B), with
\beta^{\prime}
arising from the slice of
\mathfrak{w}
directly above
p=p_{j+1}
and the weights being

{\bf a}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{cases}{\bf a}_{i}&i<b\\
{\bf a}_{b}&i=b\\
{\bf a}_{b}+\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p_{j+1}},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}))&i=b+1\\
{\bf a}_{i-1}&i\geq b+2\,.\end{cases}

Since
\operatorname{tot}(\operatorname{fib}(g))\simeq\operatorname{tot}(\operatorname{fib}(f))
, and by (
175
), the totalization of
\operatorname{fib}(f)
is equivalent to
T^{-,>0}_{S_{p}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j}))
, we thus obtain that the object
T^{-,>0}_{S_{p_{j+1}}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{j,i}(e_{j+1}))
admits such a
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
-filtration. This establishes
(ii)
, where the weave cycle is uniquely determined by the ranks of the graded pieces of the
(\beta^{\prime},{\bf a^{\prime}})
-filtration.
∎

Theorem

9.13
allows us to compute the evaluation of
\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i}
at the bottom edge
e_{1}\in{\bf G}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, as we now show that it is equivalent to the evaluation of
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}
at the top edge
e_{\infty}
.

Corollary 9.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}\colon\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be a Demazure weave. Then the following holds:

1.

There exists an equivalence in
\mathcal{D}

\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i})\simeq\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}).

(182)

In particular, there exists a unique weight
{\bf a}
for the braid word
\beta
for which the object
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i})\in\mathcal{D}
admits a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration.

2.

The evaluation
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i})\in\mathcal{D}
is a rigid object.

Proof.

By the proof of
Proposition

9.5
, see e.g. (
165
), we have

\iota^{\downarrow}(\Delta_{i}^{\downarrow})\simeq(\iota^{\downarrow}\circ(\iota^{\downarrow})^{L}\circ\iota^{\uparrow})(\nabla_{i}^{\uparrow})[-1]\simeq((T^{\operatorname{gl}})^{-1}\circ\iota^{\uparrow})(\nabla_{i}^{\uparrow})[-1]\,

and thus also the equivalence

\iota^{\downarrow}(\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i})\simeq(T^{\operatorname{gl}})^{-1}(\iota^{\uparrow}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}))[-1]\,.

(183)

By
[
Chr22c
, Theorem 2.15]
, the inverse cotwist functor
(T^{\operatorname{gl}})^{-1}
is equivalent to the twist of the spherical adjunction
(\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{e_{\infty}},\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{e_{1}})\dashv(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}},\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}})
. We thus have

\iota^{\downarrow}(\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:corollary_proof_eq1}}}{{\simeq}}\operatorname{cof}(\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{e_{\infty}}\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i})\to\iota^{\uparrow}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}))[-1]\,.

(184)

Since by construction
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i})\simeq 0
, applying
\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}
to (
184
) yields

\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i})\simeq\operatorname{cof}((\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}\circ\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{e_{\infty}}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}})(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i})\to 0)[-1].

(185)

Finally, note that
\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{e_{\infty}}(L)
can be glued from local sections as in (
79
) for all
L\in\mathcal{D}
, and thus we have the equivalence of functors

\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}\circ\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{e_{\infty}}\simeq\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}.

(186)

In conclusion, we deduce the required equivalence (
182
) via

\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}(\mathbb{P}^{\downarrow}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:corollary_proof_eq3}}}{{\simeq}}\operatorname{cof}((\operatorname{ev}_{e_{1}}\circ\operatorname{ind}^{L}_{e_{\infty}}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}})(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i})\to 0)[-1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:corollary_proof_eq3}}}{{\simeq}}\operatorname{cof}(\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i})\to 0)[-1]\simeq\operatorname{ev}_{e_{\infty}}(\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i})\,.

(187)

The existence of a
(\beta,{\bf a})
-filtration follows from
Theorem

9.13
.
(i)
, with the uniqueness of the weight
{\bf a}
being guaranteed by
Lemma

5.30
. The rigidity stated in Part (2) is implied by combining
Theorem

9.13
.
(ii)
and (
182
).
∎

Construction 9.15
.

By the proof of
Theorem

9.13
.(i), the weave cycle
\rho_{i}:E(\mathfrak{w})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
realizing the object
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{i}
can be described as follows, scanning
\mathfrak{w}
bottom-to-top:

1.

For each edge
e\in E(\mathfrak{w})
containing a point below
p_{i}
, the weave cycle vanishes:
\rho_{i}(e)=0
.

2.

At the trivalent vertex
p_{i}
, the values of the weave cycle
\rho_{i}
are

\rho_{i}(e_{1})=0,\qquad\rho_{i}(e_{2})=1\,,

(188)

where
e_{1}
is the north-western edge of
p_{i}
, and
e_{2}
is the north-eastern edge. Note that by
(1)
above,
\rho_{i}
vanishes in the south edge of
p_{i}
.

3.

At each
4
-valent or
6
-valent vertex of
\mathfrak{w}
, the weave cycle
\rho_{i}
satisfies the tropical Lusztig rules.

4.

At each trivalent vertex
p_{j}
, with
j\in(i,m]
, with north-western edge
e_{1}
, north-eastern edge
e_{2}
and south edge
e_{s}
, the weave cycle
\rho_{i}
satisfies

\rho_{i}(e_{1})=\rho_{i}(e_{s})

(189)

and

\rho_{i}(e_{2})=\rho_{i}(e_{s})+y_{ij},

(190)

where
y_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is the dimension of a certain
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}
-group depending on
i
and
j
, cf. (
180
).∎

Recall that
[
CGG+25b
, Section 4.5&4.6]
introduced intersection pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on weave cycles. We conjecture that the weave cycles
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in[1,m]}
, as described in
9.15
, are dual to the Lusztig cycles
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in[1,m]}
with respect to this intersection pairing, i.e.

\langle\gamma_{i},\rho_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}\,.

(191)

Figure 29:
The weave cycles
\{\rho_{i}\}
realizing the silting collection dual to the simple-minded collection of Lusztig cycles for this right-inductive weave, cf.
Example

9.16
.(1). These weave cycles
\{\rho_{i}\}
are highlighted in pink.

Example 9.16
.

(1) Consider the right-inductive weave
\mathfrak{w}
for
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{4}
, as in
Figure

29
. The weave cycles
\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\}
realizing the silting collection
\{\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{1},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{2},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{3}\}
are depicted in pink in
Figure

29
, where a pink edge indicates weight 1, and a blue edge indicates weight 0. These weave cycles are obtained using
9.15
, as follows.

For
\rho_{1}
, we start at the trivalent vertex
p_{1}
, by
9.15
.(1), and assign weight 1 to the north-eastern edge of
p_{1}
, as in
9.15
.(2). As we scan upwards we reach the trivalent vertex
p_{2}
, whose assigned vanishing
2
-spherical object is
S_{p_{2}}\simeq S_{1}[1]
. To proceed according to
9.15
.(4), we use (
189
) and (
190
) to propagate upwards. From (
189
), we deduce that the weight of
\rho_{1}
on the north-western edge of
p_{2}
is zero. To apply (
190
), we must compute the value of

y_{12}\coloneqq\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p_{2}},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}))=\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{1}[1],\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})),

according to (
180
).
The braid word
\beta
that defines
\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}
in this case is
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{2}
, as it is obtained from the horizontal slice right below
p_{2}
. The corresponding weights are
{\bm{a}}=(a_{0},a_{1})=(0,1)
and hence the coherent chain complex is

\mathcal{C}_{\ast}=(S_{1}[-1]\longrightarrow 0).

Since
b=0
in this case, as it is the leftmost crossing of
\beta
, the required totalization
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})
is

\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})\simeq\operatorname{cof}(S_{1}[-1]\longrightarrow 0)\simeq S_{1}

and the value of the correction term
y_{12}
is

y_{12}=\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{1}[1],S_{1})=1,

(192)

since
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{1}[1],S_{1})=\operatorname{Ext}^{0}(S_{1},S_{1})=k
. By using
9.15
.(4) and
Equation

192
, we therefore assign weight
y_{12}=1
to
\rho_{1}
as we move upwards. This is why the north-eastern edges of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are highlighted in pink in
Figure

29
(right).

We now continue propagating
\rho_{1}
upwards until we reach
p_{3}
, where we must use (
189
) and (
190
) to propagate past
p_{3}
. As before, (
189
) implies that the weight of
\rho_{1}
on the north-western edge of
p_{3}
must be zero. From (
190
), the weight on the north-eastern edge is given by

y_{13}\coloneqq\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p_{3}},\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})).

The computation is similar to the above one for
y_{12}
. In this case
S_{p_{3}}\simeq S_{1}[2]
, the braid word is
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{3}
and the weights are
{\bm{a}}=(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2})=(0,1,1)
. Hence, the coherent chain complex
\mathcal{C}_{\ast}
has the form

\mathcal{C}_{\ast}=(S_{\sigma_{1}}^{\oplus a_{2}}[-2]\longrightarrow S_{\sigma_{1}^{2}}^{\oplus a_{1}}[-1]\longrightarrow S_{\sigma_{1}^{3}}^{\oplus a_{0}})\simeq(S_{1}[-2]\longrightarrow S_{1}\longrightarrow 0)

(193)

As above,
b=0
also in this case. Therefore the required totalization
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})
is equivalent to

\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b})\simeq\operatorname{cof}(S_{1}[-1]\longrightarrow\operatorname{cof}(S_{1}\longrightarrow 0))\simeq\operatorname{cof}(S_{1}[-1]\longrightarrow S_{1}[1]).

(194)

In consequence,

y_{13}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:example1_totalization_coherentchaincomplex_p3}}}{{=}}\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{1}[2],\operatorname{cof}(S_{1}[-1]\longrightarrow S_{1}[1]))=\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{1}[2],S_{1}[1])=1,

(195)

as the natural surjective map
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{1}[2],S_{1}[1])\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{1}[2],\operatorname{cof}(S_{1}[-1]\longrightarrow S_{1}[1]))
from applying
\operatorname{Mor}(S_{1}[2],\mathchar 45\relax)
to the cofiber sequence associated to (
194
) is an isomorphism. This proves that
\rho_{1}
as drawn in Figure
29
is indeed the correct weave cycle realizing
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{1}
. The proof of the correctness of
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{3}
is much simpler, and the necessary argument and computations are already contained in the proof for
\rho_{1}
above. Finally, note that the duality (
191
) with respect to the weave intersection pairing indeed holds in this example.

Figure 30:
The dual Lusztig weave cycles
\{\rho_{i}\}
from
Example

9.16
.(2), highlighted in pink.

(2) Consider the left-inductive weave
\mathfrak{w}
for
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{4}
, as in
Figure

30
. In this case, all the correction terms
y_{12},y_{13},y_{23}
vanish. Indeed, scanning this left-inductive weave
\mathfrak{w}
upwards, any trivalent vertex
p_{i}
appears for the rightmost crossing of the braid word associated to the horizontal slice right below
p_{i}
. Thus the
b
value in
Equation

180
always corresponds to the index for the rightmost crossing and so
\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}
is the zero complex. Therefore the correction terms

y_{ij}\coloneqq\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p_{2}},\operatorname{tot}(0))=\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{p_{2}},0)=0

must always vanish. By applying
9.15
, we obtain the weave cycles
\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\}
realizing the silting collection
\{\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{1},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{2},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{3}\}
for this left-inductive weave
\mathfrak{w}
. As in (1), the duality (
191
) with respect to the weave intersection pairing holds as well.

Figure 31:
A few of the weave cycles
\{\rho_{i}\}
from
Example

9.16
.(3), highlighted in pink, with the corresponding (absolute) Lusztig cycles
\{\gamma_{i}\}
highlighted in yellow.

(3) Consider the weave
\mathfrak{w}
depicted in
Figure

31
. The Lusztig cycles
\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4},\gamma_{5}
associated to the trivalent vertices
p_{3},p_{4},p_{5}
are highlighted in yellow. Specifically, these are all binary weave cycles, with the only values being
0
and
1
, so we directly highlight the edges where the cycle evaluates to 1. The upwards dual weave cycles
\rho_{3},\rho_{4},\rho_{5}

realizing the objects
\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{3},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{4},\reflectbox{$\mathbb{P}$}^{\uparrow}_{5}
in the silting collection are highlighted in purple. These weave cycles
\rho_{3},\rho_{4},\rho_{5}
are obtained by applying
9.15
, as follows.

For
\rho_{5}
, it suffices to apply only rules
9.15
.(1)
and
9.15
.(2), as there are no weave vertices above
p_{5}
. For
\rho_{4}
and
\rho_{3}
, we use all the rules from
9.15
. In order to apply
9.15
.(4) for
\rho_{4}
and
\rho_{3}
, we claim that the corresponding correction terms
y_{45}
, for
\rho_{4}
, and
y_{34}
and
y_{35}
, for
\rho_{3}
, all vanish. First, the corresponding coherent chain complex
\mathcal{C}_{\ast>b}
at
p_{5}
for
\rho_{4}
is the zero complex because
\rho_{4}
has no non-zero weights to the right of
p_{5}
in the horizontal slice right below
p_{5}
. Hence
y_{45}=0
indeed vanishes. Second, let us argue that
y_{34}=0
. For that, we compute

S_{p_{4}}=T_{S_{2}}^{-2}T_{S_{1}}^{-1}(S_{2})\simeq T_{S_{2}}^{-1}(S_{1})

(196)

and note that the coherent chain complex
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast})
for
\rho_{3}
at
p_{4}
has the form

S_{\sigma_{2}}^{\oplus a_{5}}[-5]\longrightarrow S_{\sigma_{2}^{2}}^{\oplus a_{4}}[-4]\longrightarrow S_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}}^{\oplus a_{3}}[-3]\longrightarrow S_{\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}}^{\oplus a_{2}}[-2]\longrightarrow S_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}}^{\oplus a_{1}}[-1]\longrightarrow S_{\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}}^{\oplus a_{0}}

as
\beta=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}
. Since the weights for
\rho_{3}
are
{\bm{a}}=(0,0,0,0,0,1)
, and
b=2
for
p_{4}
, the totalization
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>2})
required to compute
y_{34}
is

\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>2})\simeq\operatorname{tot}(S_{2}[-5]\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow 0)\simeq S_{2}.

(197)

The computations (
196
) and (
197
) then imply

\displaystyle y_{34}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:y34_1}}}{{=}}\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(T_{S_{2}}^{-1}(S_{1}),\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>2}))

(198)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:y34_3}}}{{=}}\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(T_{S_{2}}^{-1}(S_{1}),S_{2})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{1},T_{S_{2}}(S_{2}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(S_{1},S_{2}[-1])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{0}(S_{1},S_{2})=0\

Third, the vanishing
y_{35}=0
is similar. Indeed, the spherical object at
p_{5}
is
S_{p_{5}}\simeq T_{S_{2}}^{-1}(S_{1})
, the braid word below
p_{5}
is
\beta=(\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1})^{2}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
, the weights for
\rho_{3}
are
{\bm{a}}=(0,0,0,0,0,0,1)
, and
b=5
for
p_{5}
. Thus
\operatorname{tot}(\mathcal{C}_{\ast>5})\simeq S_{2}
in this case. In consequence

\displaystyle y_{35}=\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(T_{S_{2}}^{-1}(S_{1}),S_{2})=0.

This establishes the correctness of the binary weave cycles
\{\rho_{3},\rho_{4},\rho_{5}\}
as drawn in
Figure

31
. Note that there are five Lusztig cycles but, since
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
are frozen,
\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4},\gamma_{5}
are the only mutable Lusztig cycles. The corresponding intersections are

(\langle\gamma_{i},\rho_{j}\rangle)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mbox{for }i,j\in[3,5],

with
i=j=3
being the top left entry and
i=j=5
the bottom right entry. The only subtle entry is
\langle\lambda_{4},\gamma_{5}\rangle=0
, where the intersection is 0 despite the (support of the) cycles geometrically overlapping. Hence the duality (
191
) holds.∎

9.4
Ancillary remarks on tilting theory and exceptional collections

Part of the results and constructions of this paper are inspired by and related to the theory of highest weight abelian categories, cf.
[
CPS88
]
or
Definition

A.23
. This final subsection summarizes a few key aspects in the main text as they relate to this perspective.

Must of our results occur in the context of stable
\infty
-categories, or at least triangulated categories, in contrast to that of abelian categories. Indeed, while abelian categories suffice to tackle reduced braid words, cf. e.g. (
32
),
Remark

5.2
and
[
GLS06
,
GLS08
,
BIRS09
]
, the derived setting is required in order to establish a theory for non-reduced positive braid words. Therefore, there is merit in suggesting a potential candidate for the notion of a highest weight structure in the stable context. In a nutshell, our constructions and results lead to the following potential tenet:

A stable
k
-linear
\infty
-category endowed with a full exceptional collection can be considered as the stable analog of an abelian category endowed with a highest weight structure.

This tenet is analyzed in detail in Appendix
A
. First, in Appendix
A.2
, we associate a simple-minded collection and a Koszul dual silting collection to a stable
\infty
-category with a full exceptional collection. These collections are the stable analogs of the simple objects and projective objects in a highest weight abelian category, respectively. Similarly to how the projective objects in a highest weight abelian category arise via universal extensions of the standard objects, the silting collection arises via universal (possibly higher) extensions between the standard objects by
Proposition

A.13
. We also refer to
Theorem

A.27
for a precise relationship with highest weight abelian categories.

Second, highest weight abelian categories admit so-called tilting objects, which are objects characterized by admitting both a standard and a costandard flag. A stable version of the tilting object, which is both a higher extension of the standard exceptional objects and a higher extension of the costandard exceptional objects, appears in
Lemma

9.10
. In the theory of highest weight abelian categories, tilting objects are used to construct the Ringel duality functors, which are functors from a highest weight abelian category to its Ringel dual. Within the formalism of perverse schobers, we presented an abstract construction of two Ringel-type duality functors in
Lemma

9.2
.(3). They intertwine the standard and costandard exceptional collections, cf.
Proposition

9.5
, as well the silting object and the stable version of tilting object, cf.
Lemma

9.9
. A difference with respect to classical Ringel duality is that the definitions of these functors are not module-theoretical and rather in terms of perverse schobers.

The following table illustrates parts of the above summary, see Appendix
A
for more details.

Highest weight abelian category

Table 1:
Summary of the stable
